Dynamical Loop Equation
[

Abstract
We introduce dynamical versions of loop (or Dyson-Schwinger) equations for large families of two–dimensional interacting particle systems, including Dyson Brownian motion, Nonintersecting Bernoulli/Poisson random walks, β \beta –corners processes, uniform and Jack-deformed measures on Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns, Macdonald processes, and ( q , κ ) (q,\kappa) -distributions on lozenge tilings. Under technical assumptions, we show that the dynamical loop equations lead to Gaussian field type fluctuations. As an application, we compute the limit shape for ( q , κ ) (q,\kappa) –distributions on lozenge tilings and prove that their height fluctuations converge to the Gaussian Free Field in an appropriate complex structure.



A]Vadim Gorin
B]Jiaoyang Huang,

??
University of California, Berkeley,
??
??

??
University of Pennsylvania ,
??

1
Introduction

1.1
Overview

Interacting particle systems in one time and one space dimension with non-local interactions appear in various probability models, such as random matrices, random tilings, and random growth models.

In the continuous setting, the most famous example is
[
48
]
, where Dyson observed that the eigenvalues of a matrix-valued Hermitian Brownian motion form an interacting particle system with a logarithmic Coulomb interaction and quadratic potential. In many important cases one dimensional marginal densities of those
(1+1)
-dimensional interacting particle systems can be computed explicitly, and identified with log-gases.
For such one dimensional log-gas type systems, loop (or Dyson-Schwinger) equation is an important tool to study their macroscopic fluctuations.
They were first used in the theoretical physics literature (e.g., in the work of Migdal
[
84
]
) and were later introduced to the mathematical community by Johansson
[
73
]
to derive macroscopic central limit theorems for general
\beta
-ensembles of eigenvalues of random matrices, see also
[
23
,
24
,
79
]
. One advantage of the asymptotic arguments based on the loop equations is that they tend to be very universal and extend to wide families of stochastic systems.

On the discrete side, marginals given by appropriate versions of log–gases were used in the analysis of random tilings starting from
[
38
,
75
,
19
]
and in the study of random interface growth models starting from
[
74
]
. Generalizing these distibutions, Borodin, Guionnet and Gorin
[
18
]
introduced discrete
\beta
-ensembles, and studied their Gaussian macroscopic fluctuations using discrete loop equations, which originated in the work of Nekrasov and his collaborators
[
86
,
90
,
89
]
; see also
[
39
]
.

Central limit theorems for macroscopic fluctuations of log-gases (in investigations of random matrices, random tilings, and random partitions) reveal that the limiting fields in these theorems can be viewed as one–dimensional slices of a unique two–dimensional limiting object, the
Gaussian Free Field
, see
[
16
,
12
,
39
,
43
,
30
]
for the results of such type. This fits into a general belief that the Gaussian Free Field should appear as a universal limit of the macroscopic fluctuations for random two-dimensional surfaces of various origins.

The aim of this paper is to lift the asymptotic analysis from one-dimensional sections to full two-dimensional fields by developing very general dynamical versions of loop equations, the
dynamical loop equations
. They provide a new toolbox to study macroscopic fluctuations of two dimensional interacting particle systems, directly applicable to many well-known stochastic dynamics both in discrete and continuous settings, such as: Dyson Brownian motion
[
48
]
, nonintersecting Bernoulli/Poisson random walks
[
59
,
78
,
67
]
,
\beta
–corner processes
[
60
,
16
]
, measures on Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns
[
31
,
97
]
, Macdonald processes
[
14
]
, and
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on lozenge tilings
[
19
,
39
]
. We prove that under mild technical assumptions, the dynamical loop equations lead to fluctuations described by Gaussian fields with accessible covariance.

There were hints in the literature, suggesting that the framework of the loop equations might be adaptable to multi-time two-dimensional setting,
as in
[
4
,
67
,
40
,
68
,
41
]
. However, these results were mostly isolated and
relied on specifics of the stochastic systems they dealt with. In contrast, we work directly with transition probabilities of the stochastic evolutions of interest and allow for them a very general form, parameterized by three analytic functions and an arbitrary real parameter, playing the same role as
\beta
in the random matrix theory. Our approach covers many of the old examples and simultaneously allows asymptotic analysis of new stochastic systems, which were not accessible by previous tools.

As our main application, we study the height fluctuations of
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on lozenge tilings introduced in
[
19
]
. In the special case
\kappa=0
, the
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on lozenge tilings become the
q^{\text{volume}}
–weighted lozenge tilings (or plane partitions), whose particular cases were investigated in
[
104
,
94
,
34
,
2
]
. We compute the limit shapes for the
(q,\kappa)
–random tilings of a special class of polygonal domains (“trapezoids”) with arbitrary many sides and discover that the shapes can be parameterized by algebraic curves in an appropriate coordinate system, thus giving the positive answer to the question raised in
[
19
, end of Section 2]
. We further
show that the centered height fluctuations of
(q,\kappa)
-random tilings of these domains converge to the Gaussian free field. In particular, this verifies the conjecture in
[
39
, Conjecture 8.4.1]
for
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on lozenge tilings of hexagonal domains
1
1

1

For another proof of this conjecture see the forthcoming paper
[
44
]
.
,
and the prediction in
[
76
]
for
q^{\text{volume}}
–weighted lozenge tilings.

In the next two subsections we, first, state the dynamical loop equation in its most general form, and then present in details our results on
(q,\kappa)
–random lozenge tilings.

1.2
Dynamical loop equations

In the most general discrete setting of our interest, we deal with discrete-time Markov chains with deterministic initial conditions. The state space of the chains, denoted
\mathbb{GT}_{n}
, consists of
n
–tuples of integers
{\bm{\lambda}}:\lambda_{1}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lambda_{n}
which we call signatures of rank
n
or (in the case
\lambda_{n}\geqslant 0
) Young diagrams with at most
n
rows.
2
2

2

The notation
\mathbb{GT}
comes from the names Gelfand and Tsetlin, who used combinatorics of signatures in the study of representation theory of classical Lie groups.
In this text we allow transitions of two types for the Markov chains:

1.

Ascending transition
:
n
is fixed and at each step coordinates
\lambda_{i}
either stay the same or grow by one. I.e., non-zero transition probabilities are from
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}
to
\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}
under restriction

\mu_{i}-\lambda_{i}\in\{0,1\}\qquad\text{ for }i=1,2,\dots,n.

2.

Descending transition
:
n
decreases by
1
at each step and we transition to
interlacing
signatures. I.e., non-zero transition probabilities are from
\bm{\lambda}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n+1}
to
\bm{\mu}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}
under the restriction

\lambda_{1}\geqslant\mu_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\mu_{n}\geqslant\lambda_{n+1}.

(1)

Although requiring all transitions to be of one of these two types might seem restrictive, we present in Section
3
a large number of natural examples fitting into our formalism. In fact, the two types of transitions are closely related to each other: as we show in Section
2.2
one can be obtained from another by the particle-hole duality. Hence, in this section it suffices to present our results only in the ascending setting.

Fig 1:
We identify
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}
with
n
-particles
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}-\theta,\lambda_{3}-2\theta,\dots,\lambda_{n}-(n-1)\theta)
.

Fix
\theta>0
and let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. We identify each signature
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}
, with an
n
-particle system
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}-\theta,\lambda_{3}-2\theta,\dots,\lambda_{n}-(n-1)\theta)
. In other words, we use a bijection between
\mathbb{GT}_{n}
and the
\theta
-shifted lattice

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigl\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\mid x_{1}\in\mathbb{Z},\quad x_{i}-x_{i+1}\in\theta+\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0},\quad i=1,2,\dots,n-1\bigr\},

(2)

given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{GT}_{n}\ni{\bm{\lambda}}\leftrightarrow{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n},\qquad x_{i}=\lambda_{i}-(i-1)\theta,\quad i=1,2,\dots,n.

(3)

Graphically this procedure is illustrated in Figure
1
. In the special case
\theta=1
,
\mathbb{W}_{1}^{n}
is the set of strictly ordered
n
-tuples of integers and we simply denote it by
\mathbb{W}^{n}
.

Fix
\theta>0
, a particle configuration
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}
, an interaction function
b(z)
, and weight functions
\phi^{+}(z),\phi^{-}(z)
. Our central object is the following transition probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{Z({\bm{x}})}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{b(x_{i}+\theta e_{i})-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{+}(x_{i})^{e_{i}}\phi^{-}(x_{i})^{1-e_{i}},

(4)

where
{\bm{x}},{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}
,
{\bm{e}}=(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
Z({\bm{x}})
is a normalization constant
3
3

3

Here and below we silently assume that such a constant exists.
guaranteeing that

\sum_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})=1.

(5)

We remark that, as in
[
18
]
,
\theta
can be linked to the parameter
\beta
of the random matrix theory (taking values
\beta=1,2,4
for real/complex/quaternionic matrices) through
\theta=\beta/2
. Thus, outside
\theta=1
we do not expect Markov chains with transitions (
4
) to be free fermionic or analysable through the techniques of determinantal point processes.

Some examples of Markov chains with transitions in the form (
4
) and their limits are explained in Section
3
. They include nonintersecting Bernoulli/Poisson random walks,
\beta
–corners processes, Dyson Brownian motion, uniform and Jack-deformed measures on Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns, Macdonald processes, and
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on lozenge tilings. The following dynamical version of the loop (or Schwinger-Dyson) equation provides a unified approach to study the macroscopic fluctuations of dynamics with transitions (
4
).

Theorem 1.1
(Dynamical Loop Equation).

Choose an open set
U\subset\mathbb{C}
, a particle configuration
{{\bm{x}}=(x_{1}>x_{2}>\dots>x_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}}
such that the interval
[x_{n},x_{1}]\subset U
, a parameter
\theta>0
, two holomorphic functions
\phi^{+}(z)
,
\phi^{-}(z)
on
U
and a conformal (i.e., holomorphic and injective) function
b(z)
on
U
. Assume that the random
n
–tuple
{\bm{e}}=(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n}
is distributed according to the transition probability (
4
). Then the following observable is a holomorphic function of
z\in U
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{b(z+\theta)-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(z)-b(x_{j})}+\phi^{-}(z)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{b(z)-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(z)-b(x_{j})}\right].

(6)

Remark 1.2
.

The condition
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}
is not crucial for the validity of Theorem
1.1
and one can replace
x_{i}
by other complex numbers in
U
; yet, all the examples considered in our paper have this condition or its limiting forms.

We give two proofs of Theorem
1.1
in Section
2.1
. The first proof is a direct verification that each possible (simple) pole of (
6
) has a zero residue. The second proof identifies (
6
) with a ratio of two partition functions
Z({\bm{x}})
of the ensemble (
4
) — with
n+1
particles and with
n
particles — and notices that these partition functions are holomorphic functions of
x_{i}
. The second proof suggests possible further generalizations of Theorem
1.1
, see Remark
2.1
.

Special or limiting cases of the above dynamical loop equations were known before. For Dyson Brownian motion equivalent expressions were implicitly used in
[
4
,
8
,
72
]
to study the limiting empirical particle density and its fluctuations.
For
\beta
-nonintersecting Poisson random walks and uniformly random lozenge tiling, they were developed by the second named author in
[
67
,
68
]
to study the macroscopic fluctuations. Certain formulas, resembling our dynamical loop equations, can be also found in the work of Nekrasov
[
87
,
88
]
4
4

4

There is no Markovian dynamics in these papers, however, certain holomorphic observables on random ensembles of
n
Young diagrams are being studied as one of the examples. The probabilities in these ensembles depend on a degree
2n
polynomial and by choosing the polynomial appropriately, one can force each diagram to be either empty or one box — resembling our
{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. In order to closer match our weights (
4
) with certain specific
b(\cdot)
and
\phi^{\pm}(\cdot)
functions, one needs to deal with (colored) theories with defects in the terminology of
[
87
,
88
]
.
. The first main contribution of this paper is to introduce the general form (
4
) (see also (
27
)) of transition probability and analyze it by dynamical loop equations.

It is also instructive to compare Theorem
1.1
with two-level discrete loop equations of
[
40
,
41
]
. An important distinction is that in our setup
{\bm{x}}
is deterministic, while in
[
40
,
41
]
it is random (of log-gas type). We are going to significantly rely on this feature in the applications of Theorem
1.1
: it allows us to iterate the equations and reach joint distributions at arbitrary times, rather than only at adjacent ones.

Proceeding to a broader context, we refer to
[
63
]
for an introduction to the loop equations and historic overview. A general phenomena in the study of many particle systems with strong mean field interactions is that the moments of a large class of test functions admit an infinite system of equations that are called the loop equations, Dyson-Schwinger
equations, Master equations, or Ward identities depending on the context. These equations are often asymptotically closed in the limit of growing dimensions and can be used to get delicate asymptotic information on the particle systems. This strategy has been developed at the formal level (i.e. assuming that the asymptotic expansions should exist) in physics starting from
[
3
]
, see
[
22
,
35
,
36
,
52
,
53
,
54
]
for more recent and much more detailed studies. The approach was put on the rigorous grounds starting from
[
73
]
and we refer to
[
63
]
for an outline, as well as many references on the related developments in the mathematical literature. The theoretical physics literature made a curious observation that the asymptotic expansions in these particle systems (developed through the loop equations or by other means) satisfy the so-called topological recursion, which then connects them to enumerative geometry and generating functions for maps, cf.
[
101
]
,
[
27
]
, and
[
102
]
. It is yet to be seen, whether connections to enumerative geometry can be also found in the study of our dynamical loop equations.

In Section
4
we prove that under technical assumptions the dynamical loop equations lead to Gaussian fluctuations with explicitly computable mean and covariance. More precisely, take a small scaling parameter
\varepsilon
and assume that
n
is inverse proportional to
\varepsilon
, i.e.
n=\lfloor{\mathsf{n}}\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
for some
{\mathsf{n}}>0
. We encode the particle configurations
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}
by a smoothed version of the empirical density:

\displaystyle\rho(s;{\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{1}(\varepsilon x_{i}\leqslant s\leqslant\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta),\quad\rho(s;{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}})=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{1}(\varepsilon(x_{i}+e_{i})\leqslant s\leqslant\varepsilon(x_{i}+e_{i})+\varepsilon\theta).

Then under the transition probability (
4
),
\rho(s;{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}})
is a random probability density. Its difference with
\rho(s;{\bm{x}})
has the following decomposition:

\rho(s;{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}})=\text{``stochastic term"}+\text{``deterministic term"}.

(7)

In Theorem
4.5
, under technical assumptions, by analyzing the loop equation (
6
) we prove that as
\varepsilon\to 0
the stochastic term is asymptotically a Gaussian field and obtain asymptotic expansions for the deterministic term and the covariance of the stochastic term. As a consequence, for any two dimensional interacting particle system, if we can view it as a Markov process with transition probabilities (
4
) (with potentially time-dependent functions
b
and
\phi^{\pm}
), then its asymptotic limit shape can be found from deterministic terms in (
7
) and macroscopic fluctuations converge to a two-dimensional Gaussian field, which is found from the stochastic terms in (
7
). Note that technical assumptions of Theorem
4.5
still remain to be checked on case-by-case basis; we demonstrate in Section
5.2
how this is done for
(q;\kappa)
–distributions on lozenge tilings.

Our analysis of the decomposition (
7
) based on Theorem
1.1
has one important difference from the numerous previous one-dimensional results based on the loop equations for log-gas type systems, as in
[
23
,
24
,
79
,
73
,
18
]
. Those texts relied on a two-step procedure, in which one needs to establish a priori estimates on the concentration of the empirical density in the first step before using the loop equations in the second step. Such estimates rely on a different set of tools, such as variational problems or large deviations techniques. In contrast, our approach does not require any a priori estimates and extracts all asymptotic information directly from Theorem
1.1
: since we deal with elementary one-step transitions, we can rely on the determinstic fact that
{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}
is close to
{\bm{x}}
.

A reader might be wondering why we chose to deal with the smoothed empirical density
\rho(s;{\bm{x}})
rather than with a more standard empirical measure
\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\varepsilon x_{i}}
. While we could have dealt with the latter as well (at the expense of modifications of some of the formulas), the former leads to “better” functions (polynomials, rational functions, etc) before taking limits, which is convenient when we aim at algebraic answers, as in the following Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
. For instance, if
x_{i}=-i\theta
,
1\leq i\leq n
, then

\exp\left(\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{z-s}{\rm d}s\right)=\exp\left(\int^{0}_{-\varepsilon\theta n}\frac{{\rm d}s}{z-s}\right)=\frac{z+\varepsilon\theta n}{z}.

On the other hand, if we had used the standard empirical measure, then a similar expression would have become a much more singular function of
z
.

1.3
Height fluctuations of
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on lozenge tilings

We illustrate the strength of Theorem
1.1
by applying it to the study of the fluctuations of height functions of
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on lozenge tilings introduced in
[
19
]
, which were not accessible by previous methods.

We use the triangular grid with coordinate system
(t,x)
based on two lattice directions inclined to each other under
120
degrees, as in the left panel of Figure
2
.
x
increases in the vertical direction and
t
increases in the down–right direction. The lozenges are defined as unions of adjacent elementary triangles of the grid and they have three types:

We are going to refer to the first type as
horizontal
lozenges; they are shown in red in our drawings.

Fig 2:
Left panel: trapezoid domain with
N=8
,
T=12
,
a_{1}=-6
,
b_{1}=-4
,
a_{2}=-2
,
b_{2}=4
; and
(t,x)
–coordinate system. Middle panel: one possible lozenge tiling and the values for
\tilde{x}
in (
9
). Right panel: non-intersecting paths and values of the height function.

For any integers
N>0
,
T>0
, and
-T\leqslant a_{1}<b_{1}<a_{2}<b_{2}<\dots<a_{r}<b_{r}\leqslant N
, with
\sum_{i=1}^{r}(b_{i}-a_{i})=N
, we consider a trapezoidal domain
P
with right vertical side
[0,N]
at horizontal coordinate
T
and left vertical sides
[a_{i},b_{i}]
at horizontal coordinate
0
. Other sides of the domain are segments going in down-right direction from points
(0,a_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,r
; segment in up-left direction from the point
(T,N)
; segments in up-right direction from points
(0,b_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,r
; and segment in down-left direction from
(T,0)
. As a result,
P
is a polygonal domain with
3r+3
sides. We refer to the left panel of Figure
2
for an example with
r=2
.

We let
\Omega(P)
denote the set of all lozenge tilings of the domain
P
; one such tiling for a particular choice of the parameters is shown in the middle panel of Figure
2
. Uniformly random lozenge tilings of
P
are now well understood, see
[
57
]
for general overview and
[
96
,
97
,
58
,
46
,
47
,
45
,
29
]
for more specific results, and our task is to go beyond these results and consider more advanced measures on tilings. The
(q,\kappa)
-distribution on lozenge tilings of
P
assigns to any
{\mathcal{T}}\in\Omega(P)
the probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\mathcal{T}})=\frac{w({\mathcal{T}})}{\sum_{{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}\in\Omega(P)}w({\mathcal{T}}^{\prime})},

(8)

where the weights
w({\mathcal{T}})
of a tiling is the product of weights of its horizontal lozenges:

\displaystyle w({\mathcal{T}})=\prod_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}\in{\mathcal{T}}}w({\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}),\qquad w({\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}})=\kappa q^{x-t/2}-\kappa^{-1}q^{-x+t/2}=\kappa q^{\tilde{x}}-\kappa^{-1}q^{-\tilde{x}},

(9)

and
(t,x)
is the coordinate of the left corner of a horizontal lozenge
{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}\in{\mathcal{T}}
. The number
\tilde{x}=x-t/2
appearing (
9
) has a simple meaning: this is the vertical coordinate counted so that its fixed level lines are horizontal, i.e. by using the Cartesian coordinate system (rather than non-orthogonal
(t,x)
coordinate system that we stick to), see the middle panel of Figure
2
.

We need to make sure that the weights (
8
) are positive. There are three possible restrictions on the parameters which guarantee positivity:

1.

imaginary case:
q
is a positive real number,
\kappa
is an arbitrary pure imaginary complex number.

2.

real case:
q
is a positive real number,
\kappa
is a real number with additional restrictions depending on the trapezoidal domain;
\kappa^{2}
cannot lie inside the interval
[q^{-(N+b_{r}-1)},q^{T+a_{1}-1}]
or
[q^{T+a_{1}-1},q^{-(N+b_{r}-1)}]
depending on whether
q>1
or
q<1
.

3.

trigonometric case:
q
and
\kappa
are complex numbers of modulus
1
,
q=e^{i\alpha},\kappa=e^{\mathbf{i}\beta}
, with additional restrictions depending on the trapezoidal domain;

2\beta-\alpha(T+a_{1}-1)
and
2\beta+\alpha(N+b_{r}-1)
must lie in the same interval
(2k\pi,2(k+1)\pi)
, for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Figure
3
shows samples from
(q,\kappa)
–distribution on lozenge tilings of hexagons for various values of the parameters. For special values of
q
and
\kappa
the formulas (
8
),(
9
) can be simplified:
\kappa=\infty
leads to the weight
q^{\text{volume}}
on tilings, where by volume we mean the total volume enclosed under the stepped surface corresponding to the tiling (the surface becomes visible by treating three types of lozenges as projections of three sides of a unit cube, cf. the middle panel of Figure
2
);
\kappa=0
leads to the weight
q^{-\text{volume}}
;
q=1
leads to the uniform measure on tilings; in the limit
q\to 1
,
\kappa\to 1
and after division by
q-1
, the weight (
9
) turns into a linear function of
\tilde{x}
.

Fig 3:
Samples of tilings of
100\times 100\times 100
hexagon. Left panel: imaginary case with
\kappa=\infty
,
q=0.97
. Center panel: real case with
q=0.99
,
\kappa=q^{-110}
. Right panel: trigonometric case with
\alpha=0.015
,
\beta=1.57
.

The weights (
9
) were first introduced in
[
19
]
for lozenge tilings of hexagons. The same paper showed that tilings with such weights lead to rich algebraic structures. In other words, in contrast to generic weights, (
9
) should be treated as an exactly-solvable or integrable case: the analysis of
[
19
]
revealed connections to
q
–Racah orthogonal polynomials; in addition, the limit shape of the tilings was found to be encoded in an appropriate coordinate system by a quadratic polynomial in two variables. These results have led naturally to a question: is such integrability a specific feature of the hexagons, or does it extend to tilings with weights (
9
) for more general domains? Our results show that the latter is true.
5
5

5

One conceptual explanation is as follows: the weights (
8
),(
9
) are not ad hoc, instead they are deeply related to the combinatorics of the Koornwinder symmetric polynomials, as we detail in Section
3.7.2
.
We now present three theorems explaining how the macroscopic behavior of
(q,\kappa)
–random lozenge tilings of
P
is encoded in certain algebraic equations.

We introduce a small real parameter
\varepsilon\ll 1
and assume that
r
and
\kappa
stay fixed, while
q
,
N
,
T
,
a_{i},b_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,r
, depend on
\varepsilon
in such a way that as
\varepsilon
goes to
0
we have:

\displaystyle\varepsilon N\to{\mathsf{N}},\quad\varepsilon T\to{\mathsf{T}},\quad\varepsilon\ln(q)\to\ln({\mathsf{q}}),\quad\varepsilon a_{i}\to{\mathsf{a}}_{i},\quad\varepsilon b_{i}\to{\mathsf{b}}_{i},\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant r,

(10)

with asymptotic parameters
{\mathsf{q}}
,
{\mathsf{N}}
,
{\mathsf{T}}
,
{\mathsf{a}}_{i},{\mathsf{b}}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,r
satisfying
-{\mathsf{T}}\leqslant{\mathsf{a}}_{1}<{\mathsf{b}}_{1}<{\mathsf{a}}_{2}<{\mathsf{b}}_{2}<\dots<{\mathsf{a}}_{r}<{\mathsf{b}}_{r}\leqslant{\mathsf{N}}
, and

\sum_{i=1}^{r}({\mathsf{b}}_{i}-{\mathsf{a}}_{i})={\mathsf{N}}
. We also keep positivity constraints, so that in the real case
{\mathsf{q}},\kappa\in\mathbb{R}
and
\kappa^{2}
cannot lie inside the interval
[{\mathsf{q}}^{-({\mathsf{N}}+{\mathsf{b}}_{r}-1)},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}+{\mathsf{a}}_{1}-1}]
or
[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}+{\mathsf{a}}_{1}-1},{\mathsf{q}}^{-({\mathsf{N}}+{\mathsf{b}}_{r}-1)}]
depending on whether
{\mathsf{q}}>1
or
{\mathsf{q}}<1
.
The conditions imply that the rescaled trapezoidal domain
\varepsilon P
converges to a limiting domain
{\mathsf{P}}
.

It is convenient to state the asymptotic results on tilings in terms of their
height functions.
We are going to use the following version of the height function
h
defined on the vertices of the triangular lattice inside
P
. Let us ignore

lozenges in the tiling and trace only the lozenges of the remaining two types

and

. Then these lozenges form
N

non-intersecting paths
traversing
P
from the left boundary to the right boundary, as shown in the right panel of Figure
2
. We define
h(t,x)
as the total number of paths below a given point
(t,x)
; this is originally defined for integer values of
t
and
x
and then extended to all real values by linear interpolation. Because tilings are random, so is the function
h(t,x)
; however, its values on the boundary of
P
are deterministic: for instance, it equals
0
on the two bottom-most segments of the boundary of
P
and it equals
N
on the two upper-most segments of the boundary of
P
.

A very general
variational principle for tilings
of
[
37
]
implies that in the regime (
10
) the height functions
h(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}},\varepsilon^{-1})
for random lozenge tilings of
P
converge in probability to a deterministic limit:

\displaystyle\varepsilon h(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}},\varepsilon^{-1})\rightarrow{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},),\quad({\mathsf{t}},)\in{{\mathsf{P}}}.

(11)

The limiting height function
{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},)
is the minimizer of a certain surface tension integral, as noticed in
[
19
, Section 2.4]
. In general such minimization problems are hard to solve. However, in our situation
{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},)
is explicit and we compute it for all trapezoidal domains
P
in Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
.

For a point
({\mathsf{t}},)
in the interior
of
{{\mathsf{P}}}
, we identify the gradient of the limiting height function
\nabla{\mathsf{h}}
with the local densities of three types of lozenges:

p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}}({\mathsf{t}},)=1-\partial{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},),\quad p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_up.pdf}}}}({\mathsf{t}},)=-\partial_{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},),\quad p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_down.pdf}}}}({\mathsf{t}},)=\partial{\mathsf{h}}(,{\mathsf{t}})+\partial_{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},).

(12)

A central feature of random lozenge tilings is that they exhibit boundary-induced phase transitions. Depending on the shape of the domain, they can admit frozen regions, where the associated height function is flat almost deterministically, and liquid regions, where the height function appears more rough and random and all three types
of lozenges are asymptotically present. More precisely, the liquid region is defined as

{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}}):=\{({\mathsf{t}},)\in{\mathsf{P}}:0<p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}}({\mathsf{t}},),p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_up.pdf}}}}({\mathsf{t}},),p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_down.pdf}}}}({\mathsf{t}},)<1\},

(13)

and the rest of the domain is called the frozen region. In particular, in Figure
3
we clearly see frozen regions near the boundaries of the hexagon: in each such region only one type of lozenges is present. Our first result is an explicit rational parameterization of the
arctic curve
separating frozen and liquid regions.

Fig 4:
Arctic curves via Theorem
1.3
. Left panel:
{\mathsf{q}}=\tfrac{1}{10}
,
\kappa=\frac{\mathbf{i}}{10}
. Right panel:
{\mathsf{q}}=20
,
\kappa=\frac{\mathbf{i}}{10}
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Consider the limit regime (
10
) and assume that we are in the real or imaginary case, so that
{\mathsf{q}}
is real and
\kappa
is either real or purely imaginary. For any
u\in{\mathbb{C}}
, let

f_{0}(u)=\frac{({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{u})(\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-u})}{(\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-u})({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{u})}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{u})(\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-u})}{(\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-u})({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{u})}.

(14)

and

\displaystyle U(u)=\frac{f_{0}(u){\mathsf{q}}^{-u}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}}{1-f_{0}(u)},\qquad V(u)=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}-f_{0}(u)\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}}{1-f_{0}(u)}.

(15)

The arctic curve separating frozen and liquid regions can be parameterized as
({\mathsf{t}}(u),(u))
found from:

{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}=\frac{V^{\prime}(u)}{U^{\prime}(u)},\qquad{\mathsf{q}}^{=}\frac{{\mathsf{w}}\pm\sqrt{{\mathsf{w}}^{2}-4\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}}}{2\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}},\quad\text{ where }\quad{\mathsf{w}}=V(u)-\frac{U(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}},

(16)

and the parameter
u
is chosen in such a way that
{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}
belongs to the real line
{\mathbb{R}}
.

Remark 1.4
.

The choice of the sign in (
16
) arises from the solution of the quadratic equation in
{\mathsf{q}}
:

\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}{\mathsf{q}}^{+}{\mathsf{q}}^{-}={\mathsf{w}}.

When
\kappa
is purely imaginary, we should choose
+
in (
16
) in order to guarantee the positivity of
{\mathsf{q}}
. When
\kappa
is real, the two choices lead to two curves, which are images of each other under the map
(,{\mathsf{t}})\mapsto(-+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa,{\mathsf{t}})
. One of them is the desired arctic curve, while the second one is outside the polygon
{\mathsf{P}}
.

Remark 1.5
.

We are not going to detail the trigonometric case here and in the following theorems. Our approach still applies and, algebraically, all the formulas should remain the same; however, various small features (e.g., the choice of the curve on the complex plane where the parameter
u
belongs in Theorem
1.3
) need to be changed.

Examples of the arctic curves drawn using Theorem
1.3
are shown in Figure
4
.

Inside the liquid region, following the general philosophy of
[
76
]
,
[
57
, Lectures 9,10]
we encode the triplet of local densities by the complex slope
f
, which is a complex number with positive imaginary part and such that the triangle
(0,1,f)
has angles
(\pi p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}},\pi p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_up.pdf}}}},\pi p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_down.pdf}}}})
, as in Figure
5
:

\displaystyle\arg f=\pi p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}},\quad\arg(f-1)=\pi(p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}}+p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_down.pdf}}}}).

(17)

Fig 5:
Triangle
(0,1,f)
has angles
(\pi p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}},\pi p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_up.pdf}}}},\pi p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_down.pdf}}}})
.

In the next theorem, we state the convergence of the height function, and provide an explicit characterization of the complex slope for the
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on lozenge tilings of trapezoid domains.

Theorem 1.6
.

Consider the limit regime (
10
) and assume that we are in the real or imaginary case.
Then the height function
h(x,t)
of random tilings
converges in probability,

\displaystyle\sup_{({\mathsf{t}},)\in{{\mathsf{P}}}}|\varepsilon h(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}},\varepsilon^{-1})-{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},)|\to 0.

(18)

Further, using
U(u)
and
V(u)
from (
15
), consider an equation on an unknown function
u=u({\mathsf{t}},)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{q}}^{-}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}=V(u)-\frac{U(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}}.

(19)

Then
({\mathsf{t}},)\in{\mathsf{P}}
belongs to the liquid region
{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}})
defined by (
12
) and (
13
) if and only if (
19
) has a solution such that
{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}
is non-real. In the latter case the complex slope (
17
) at
({\mathsf{t}},)
is given by

\displaystyle f_{\mathsf{t}}()=\frac{({\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u})-({\mathsf{q}}^{-}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}})}{({\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u})-({\mathsf{q}}^{-+{\mathsf{t}}}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}})}.

(20)

Remark 1.7
.

For any
({\mathsf{t}},)\in{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}})
we show in Propositions
5.18
and
5.19
that (
19
) has four solutions with non-real
{\mathsf{q}}^{u}
:
u({\mathsf{t}},)
;
-u({\mathsf{t}},)-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa
; and their complex conjugates.
When we plug them into (
20
), we get two values for
f_{\mathsf{t}}()
, which are complex conjugate of each other. This uniquely determines
f_{\mathsf{t}}()
, since by our definitions the complex slope
f
is in the upper-half plane.

Remark 1.8
.

As explained in
[
19
, Section 2.4]
, the variational problem for the limit shape implies that the complex slope
f
satisfies a version of the complex Burgers equation inside the liquid region:

\frac{\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f}{f}-\frac{\partial_{f}}{1-f}=\ln({\mathsf{q}})\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-}},\qquad({\mathsf{t}},)\in{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}}).

(21)

On the other hand, (
20
), (
19
), and (
15
) imply
6
6

6

Expressing
f_{0}(u)
through
U(u)
and
V(u)
via (
15
) and equating the results, one gets a linear relation between
U(u)
and
V(u)
. Combining with (
19
), one gets a system of two linear equations on
U(u)
and
V(u)
, whose solution gives (
22
).

U(u)={\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}\frac{f{\mathsf{q}}^{-}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f},\qquad\qquad V(u)=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-}-f\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f}.

(22)

The right-hand sides of (
22
) are the first integrals of the Burgers equation (
21
). Definition of
U(u)
and
V(u)
of (
14
) (
15
) then implies that there is an algebraic relation between these two first integrals. This generalizes the algebraicity observation of
[
76
]
in the
\kappa=0
case.

The existence of the non-random limit in (
18
) can be alternatively deduced from the variational principle for random tilings of
[
37
]
, see discussion in
[
19
, Section 2.4]
. The novel part of Theorem
1.6
is the explicit description of the limit
{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},)
through (
20
). In the particular case when
r=1
and the tiled domain is a hexagon, Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
coincide with
[
19
, Theorem 2.1]
, but for more general polygons with
r>1
the formulas are new.
If we set
q=1
, then these results degenerate into the formulas of
[
96
]
for uniformly random tilings of trapezoids
P
.

Our next theorem describes the fluctuations of the height function
h(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}},\varepsilon^{-1})
around its expectation. Let
\mathbb{H}^{+}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(z)>0\}
be the upper half-plane.
The Gaussian free field on
\mathbb{H}^{+}
with zero boundary conditions is a random generalized Gaussian field
\mathbf{GFF}(z)
,
z\in\mathbb{H}^{+}
, which has mean
0
and covariance:

\mathbb{E}\mathbf{GFF}(z)\mathbf{GFF}(w)=K(z,w)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\left|\frac{z-w}{z-\bar{w}}\right|.

(23)

Due to the singularity of (
23
) at
z=w
, the values of
\mathbf{GFF}(z)
at points are not-well defined, but we can make sense of the integrals of
\mathbf{GFF}(z)
against (sufficiently smooth) test–measures, see
[
99
]
,
[
105
]
,
[
9
]
,
[
57
, Lecture 11]
for more details.

We also need a bijection between
\mathbb{H}^{+}
and the liquid region
{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}})
.

Lemma 1.9
.

For each
({\mathsf{t}},)\in{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}})
, define
\bar{\Omega}({\mathsf{t}},)={\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}
, where
u
is a non-real solution to (
19
), chosen so that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\bar{\Omega}({\mathsf{t}},))>0
. Then
\bar{\Omega}
is a bijection between
{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}})
and
\mathbb{H}^{+}
.

Remark 1.10
.

Continuing Remark
1.7
, we note that by Propositions
5.18
and
5.19
,
\bar{\Omega}
is uniquely determined by (
19
) and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\bar{\Omega}({\mathsf{t}},))>0
. The choice of
u
guaranteeing
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(f_{\mathsf{t}}())>0
might differ by conjugation from the choice of
u
guaranteeing
{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(\bar{\Omega}({\mathsf{t}},))>0}
.

Theorem 1.11
.

Consider the limit regime (
10
) and assume that we are in the real or imaginary case. Then inside the liquid region,
({\mathsf{t}},)\in{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}})
,
we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sqrt{\pi}\bigl(h(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}},\varepsilon^{-1})-\mathbb{E}[h(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}},\varepsilon^{-1})]\bigr)=\bar{\Omega}\text{-pullback of GFF in }\mathbb{H}^{+},

in the sense of convergence of the joint moments for pairings with appropriate test-measures.

We refer to Theorem
6.15
for a more precise statement of Theorem
1.11
.

If
q=1
, then
(q,\kappa)
-distributions turn into the uniform distribution on lozenge tilings of
P
and Theorem
1.11
degenerates into the results of
[
97
,
29
]
. For general
(q,\kappa)
and
r=1
, i.e. for the hexagons, Theorem
1.11
was conjectured in
[
39
, Conjecture 8.4.1]
. The same article also contains a partial result establishing Gaussian fluctuations along a single vertical section by using discrete loop equations for log-gases; see also
[
26
]
for another approach. For
\kappa=0
and
q\neq 1
, i.e. for the
q^{\text{volume}}
–weighted lozenge tilings Theorem
1.11
verifies for the trapezoidal domains
P
a general conjecture of Kenyon and Okounkov, cf.
[
76
]
and
[
57
, Lectures 11-12]
. The only previous Gaussian free field type result for
q^{\text{volume}}
–weighted tilings is
[
2
]
dealing with plane partitions, which can be obtained from tilings of the hexagons by sending side lengths to infinity while keeping
q
fixed. For general values of the
(q,\kappa)
parameters, we are led to a conjecture:

Conjecture 1.12
.

The macroscopic fluctuations of the height function in the liquid region for
(q,\kappa)
–distributed random lozenge tilings of arbitrary domains are asymptotically described by the Gaussian Free Field in the complex structure given by either of the first integrals (
22
) of the complex Burgers equation (
21
).

We refer to
[
57
, Lectures 11-12]
for the detailed explanations and heuristics underlying Conjecture
1.12
in the special case of the uniform measure,
q=1
.
The formulas (
22
) imply that the first integrals are holomorphic functions of
\Omega({\mathsf{t}},)
in Theorem
1.11
. Hence, these first integrals define the same complex structure in the liquid region as
\Omega
(or its complex conjugate
\bar{\Omega}
, depending on the choice of
u
; conjugation of the complex structure does not change the covariance of GFF) and Theorem
1.11
verifies Conjecture
1.12
for trapezoidal domains
P
. What Conjecture
1.12
says is that our result should extend from trapezoids to more general domains.

As we will see in Section
3.7
, the
(q,\kappa)
-distribution (
8
) on lozenge tilings can be viewed as a Markov process of nonintersecting paths, and the transition probability is given in the form (
4
). Our analysis of
(q,\kappa)
–distributions relies on Theorem
4.5
, which uses the dynamic loop equations of Theorem
1.1
to obtain an asymptotic expansion for the one-step transition probabilities. We remark that checking the assumptions of Theorem
4.5
takes efforts and we devote Section
5.2
to it. Eventually, the deterministic part of the expansion (
7
) leads to Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
after careful analysis and massaging of the formulas in Section
5
. The form of the stochastic part implies that the height fluctuations converge to a Gaussian field, described by a stochastic evolution equation; we analyze this equation and prove that it outputs the Gaussian Free Field in Section
6
.

In addition to the dynamical loop equations, the second key ingredient of the proofs is the use of the
complex characteristic flow
which we introduce. The flow is related to the characteristic curves of the first order PDE (
21
), but has an important difference. Namely, in (
21
) (and its relatives for uniform and
q^{\text{volume}}
-weighted tilings in
[
76
]
), the variables
({\mathsf{t}},)
in the equation are real and belong to the liquid region
{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}})
; this complicates the analysis, because the shape of the liquid region is not known a priory. In our approach, we observe that there is a way to extend the definition of the complex slope (
17
) from real to complex
z
, see (
178
), thus arriving at the doubly complex slope. The advantage of the latter is that it decouples from the notion of the liquid region, but the connection to the first order PDE (
21
) is preserved (with replaced by complex
z
). We further investigate all the main observables of the random tilings along the complex curves
z^{\mathsf{t}}
,
{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant 0
, which arise from the characteristics of the doubly complex version of (
21
), see Section
5.4
. We discover that all the formulas are dramatically simplified along these complex curves, which eventually allows us to arrive at the explicit answers of Theorems
1.3
,
1.6
, and
1.11
. In random matrix setting, the complex characteristic flow has been previously used in
[
72
,
8
,
71
,
69
]
to analyze the complex Burgers equation, which describes the evolution of the Stieltjes transform of the empirical eigenvalue density. Comparing with those analyses, our complex Burgers equation (
21
) has extra symmetries (
217
) and the characteristic flow has singular behavior, which makes the analysis more involved.

2
Dynamical Loop Equation

In this section we first give the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Then we apply the particle-hole duality to produce a version of the transition probability (
4
) for descending transitions and derive the corresponding dynamical loop equations.

2.1
Proof of Theorem
1.1

The only possible singularities of (
6
) are simple poles at
z=x_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,n
. Thus, our plan is to fix any
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
and check that the residue of (
6
) at
z=x_{i}
vanishes. The first term in (
6
) has a residue at
z=x_{i}
if and only if
e_{i}=0
. The residue is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bm{e}}:e_{i}=0}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})\phi^{+}(x_{i})\frac{b(x_{i}+\theta)-b(x_{i})}{b^{\prime}(x_{i})}\prod_{j:j\neq i}\frac{b(x_{i}+\theta)-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j})}.

(24)

The second term has a residue at
z=x_{i}
if and only if
e_{i}=1
. The residue is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bm{e}}:e_{i}=1}\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})\phi^{-}(x_{i})\frac{b(x_{i})-b(x_{i}+\theta)}{b^{\prime}(x_{i})}\prod_{j:j\neq i}\frac{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j})}.

(25)

For any pair
{\bm{e}}^{0}=(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{i-1},0,e_{i+1},\dots,e_{n})
and
{\bm{e}}^{1}=(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{i-1},1,e_{i+1},\dots,e_{n})
,
the ratio of the transition probabilities
\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}^{1}|{\bm{x}})
and
\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}^{0}|{\bm{x}})
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}^{1}|{\bm{x}})}{\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}^{0}|{\bm{x}})}=\frac{\phi^{+}(x_{i})}{\phi^{-}(x_{i})}\prod_{j:j\neq i}\frac{b(x_{i}+\theta)-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}.

(26)

Plugging (
26
) into (
24
) and (
25
), we see that the residues at
{\bm{e}}^{0}
and
{\bm{e}}^{1}
cancel out:

\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}^{0}|{\bm{x}})\phi^{+}(x_{i})\frac{b(x_{i}+\theta)-b(x_{i})}{b^{\prime}(x_{i})}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{b(x_{i}+\theta)-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j})}\\
+\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}^{1}|{\bm{x}})\phi^{-}(x_{i})\frac{b(x_{i})-b(x_{i}+\theta)}{b^{\prime}(x_{i})}\prod_{j:j\neq i}\frac{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j})}=0.

Summing over
(e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{i-1},e_{i+1},\dots,e_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}
, we conclude that
the residue of (
6
) at
z=x_{i}
vanishes.
∎

For any complex numbers
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in U
, we denote the partition function

Z_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{b(x_{i}+\theta e_{i})-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{+}(x_{i})^{e_{i}}\phi^{-}(x_{i})^{1-e_{i}}.

We claim that
Z_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is a holomorphic function on
U^{n}
. Indeed, the only possible singularities of
Z_{n}
are on the lines
x_{i}=x_{j}
. On the other hand, directly from the definition, the function

F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}):=Z_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}(b(x_{i})-b(x_{j}))

is skew-symmetric in
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
; hence, it vanishes on the lines
x_{i}-x_{j}
and

\frac{F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}{\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})}

has no singularities. Therefore,

Z_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\frac{F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}{\prod_{i<j}(x_{i}-x_{j})}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{x_{i}-x_{j}}{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j})}

also has no singularities.

The expectation (
6
) can be identified with a ratio of two partition functions
\frac{Z_{n+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},z)}{Z_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}
and its holomorphicity in
z
follows from the holomorphicity of the partition functions.
∎

Remark 2.1
.

One can introduce more advanced weights, which would lead to holomorphic partition functions: in Theorem
1.1
we are allowing the increments
e_{i}
of
x_{i}
to be in
\{0,1\}
, but more generally one can allow
m
complex increments:
e_{i}\in\{v_{1},\dots,v_{m}\}
. Given
m
holomorphic functions
\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{m}
, this would lead to the factor
\phi_{i}(x_{i})
in
\prod_{i=1}^{n}
part of the distribution generalizing (
4
) whenever
e_{i}=v_{i}
and to
m^{n}
possible transitions. The argument for the holomorphicity of the partition function and of the corresponding observable
\frac{Z_{n+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},z)}{Z_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})}
remains the same, leading to an extension of Theorem
1.1
with (
6
) being replaced by expectation of the sum of
m
(rather than two) terms.

2.2
Particle-Hole Duality

In this section, we study descending transitions. Take two signatures
{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n+1}
and
{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}
, with interlacing condition
{\bm{\mu}}\prec{\bm{\lambda}}
, as in (
1
). Similarly to (
3
), we encode them by particle systems

\displaystyle x_{i}=\lambda_{i}-(i-1)\theta,\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1,\qquad y_{i}=\mu_{i}-(i-1)\theta,\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant n.

Then
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{(n+1)}
and
{\bm{y}}=(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{(n)}
, and the interlacing condition
{\bm{\mu}}\prec{\bm{\lambda}}
is equivalent to
y_{i}\in\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+1+\theta,x_{i+1}+2+\theta,\dots,x_{i}-1,x_{i}\}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

Given
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{(n+1)}
, we denote the lattice

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+1+\theta,x_{i+1}+2+\theta,\dots,x_{i}-1,x_{i}\}.

We fix two functions
b(z)
,
w(z)
and consider the following transition probability:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{Z({\bm{x}})}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\bigl(b(y_{i})-b(y_{j})\bigr)\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left[w(y_{i})\prod\limits_{\begin{smallmatrix}\ell\in\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\\
\ell>y_{i}\end{smallmatrix}}\frac{1}{b(\ell)-b(y_{i})}\prod\limits_{\begin{smallmatrix}\ell\in\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\\
\ell<y_{i}\end{smallmatrix}}\frac{1}{b(y_{i})-b(\ell)}\right],

(27)

where
{\bm{y}}=(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}
with
y_{i}\in\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+1+\theta,x_{i+1}+2+\theta,\dots,x_{i}-1,x_{i}\}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

We remark that the transition probability (
27
) is obtained from the ascending transition (
4
) by the particle-hole duality or, equivalently, by transposition of the involved Young diagrams, i.e. by mirroring Figure
1
with respect to the vertical axis. More precisely, starting from (
27
) we do the following three steps:

•

We replace the particle configurations
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{y}}
, by the holes, i.e. by the complementary configurations
\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\}
and
\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\setminus\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n}\}
;

•

We divide all coordinates by
\theta
;

•

We replace
\theta
by
\theta^{-1}
.

Applying these operations to (
27
) we get (
4
), as can be seen through the identities
(where we denote the configurations of holes as
{\bm{x}}^{\prime}=\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\setminus{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{y}}^{\prime}=\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\setminus{\bm{y}}
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}\prod_{i<j}\bigl(b(x^{\prime}_{i})-b(x^{\prime}_{j})\bigr)&=\frac{\prod_{i<j}\bigl(b(x_{i})-b(x_{j})\bigr)\prod_{\ell>\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})}\bigl(b(\ell)-b(\ell^{\prime})\bigr)}{\prod_{i}(\prod_{\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\ni\ell>x_{i}}\bigl(b(\ell)-b(x_{i})\bigr)\prod_{\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\ni\ell<x_{i}}\bigl(b(x_{i})-b(\ell)\bigr)}\\
\prod_{i<j}\bigl(b(y^{\prime}_{i})-b(y^{\prime}_{j})\bigr)&=\frac{\prod_{i<j}\bigl(b(y_{i})-b(y_{j})\bigr)\prod_{\ell>\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})}\bigl(b(\ell)-b(\ell^{\prime})\bigr)}{\prod_{i}(\prod_{\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\ni\ell>y_{i}}\bigl(b(\ell)-b(y_{i})\bigr)\prod_{\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})({\bm{x}})\ni\ell<x_{i}}\bigl(b(y_{i})-b(\ell)\bigr)}.\end{split}

(28)

Because of this connection, Theorem
1.1
can be recast for the ascending process. We record the result in the following theorem, supplied with a self-contained proof.

Theorem 2.2
.

Choose an open set
U\subset\mathbb{C}
, a parameter
\theta>0
, a particle configuration
{{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n+1}}
such that
[x_{n+1},x_{1}]\subset U
, two holomorphic functions
\phi^{+}(z)
,
\phi^{-}(z)
on
U
and a conformal (i.e., holomorphic and injective) function
b(z)
on
U
. Assume that the random
n
–tuple
{\bm{y}}
is distributed according to the transition probability (
27
) with weight function
w(z)
satisfying

\displaystyle\frac{w(z+1)}{w(z)}=\frac{\phi^{+}(z)}{\phi^{-}(z)}.

(29)

Then the following observable is a holomorphic function of
z\in U
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}\bigl(b(z)-b(x_{i})\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(b(z)-b(y_{i})\bigr)}+\phi^{-}(z)\prod\limits_{\ell\in\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})}\dfrac{\bigl(b(z+1)-b(\ell)\bigr)}{\bigl(b(z)-b(\ell)\bigr)}\cdot\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}\bigl(b(z)-b(x_{i})\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(b(z+1)-b(y_{i})\bigr)}\right].

(30)

The possible singularities of the first term in (
30
) are simple poles at the points
z=m\in\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+1+\theta,x_{i+1}+2+\theta,\dots,x_{i}-1\}
; they arise whenever
m=y_{i}
for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Note that we excluded
m=x_{i}
: there is no pole at such point due to the factor
b(z)-b(x_{i})
in the numerator.

The possible singularities of the second term in (
30
) are also simple poles at the points
z=m\in\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+1+\theta,x_{i+1}+2+\theta,\dots,x_{i}-1\}
; they arise whenever
m=y_{i}-1
for any
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Note that we excluded
m=x_{i+1}+\theta-1
: there is no pole at such point due to the factor
b(z+1)-b(\ell)
,
\ell=x_{i+1}+\theta
, in the numerator; also note that the denominators in the first product in the second term in (
30
) do not produce additional poles, since for each zero of
b(z)-b(\ell)
in the denominator, we have a matching zero in the numerator coming either from
b(z+1)-b(\ell+1)
or from
b(z)-b(x_{i})
.

In the rest of the proof we fix
i=1,\dots,n
and check that the residues of two terms in (
30
) at a point
z=m\in\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+1+\theta,x_{i+1}+2+\theta,\dots,x_{i}-1\}
differ by a sign and, hence, cancel out. For the first term, the residue is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bm{y}}:y_{i}=m}\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})\cdot\phi^{+}(m)\cdot\frac{b(m)-b(x_{i})}{b^{\prime}(m)}\cdot\frac{\prod\limits_{j\neq i}\bigl(b(m)-b(x_{j})\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{j\neq i}\bigl(b(m)-b(y_{j})\bigr)},

(31)

while for the second term the residue is given by

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bm{y}}:y_{i}=m+1}\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})\phi^{-}(m)\frac{b(x_{i})-b(m)}{b^{\prime}(m)}\prod_{\ell\in\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\setminus\{m,m+1\}}\frac{b(m+1)-b(\ell)}{b(m)-b(\ell)}\frac{\prod\limits_{j\neq i}\bigl(b(m)-b(x_{j})\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{j\neq i}\bigl(b(m+1)-b(y_{j})\bigr)}.

(32)

For any pair of particle configurations
{\bm{y}}^{1}=(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{i-1},m+1,y_{i+1},\dots,y_{n})
and
{\bm{y}}^{0}=(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{i-1},m,y_{i+1},\dots,y_{n})
,
the ratio of the transition probabilities
\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}={\bm{y}}^{1}|{\bm{x}})
and
\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}={\bm{y}}^{0}|{\bm{x}})
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}={\bm{y}}^{0}|{\bm{x}})}{\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}={\bm{y}}^{1}|{\bm{x}})}=\frac{w(m)}{w(m+1)}\prod_{\ell\in\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\setminus\{m,m+1\}}\frac{b(m+1)-b(\ell)}{b(m)-b(\ell)}\prod_{j\neq i}\frac{b(m)-b(y_{j})}{b(m+1)-b(y_{j})}.

(33)

We recall from (
29
) that
w(m)/w(m+1)=\phi^{-}(m)/\phi^{+}(m)
.
Plugging (
33
) into the expressions of (
32
) and (
31
), we see that the residuals at
{\bm{y}}^{0}
and
{\bm{y}}^{1}
cancel out:

0=\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}={\bm{y}}^{0}|{\bm{x}})\phi^{+}(m)\frac{b(m)-b(x_{i})}{b^{\prime}(m)}\frac{\prod\limits_{j\neq i}\bigl(b(m)-b(x_{j})\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{j\neq i}\bigl(b(m)-b(y_{j})\bigr)}\\
+\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}={\bm{y}}^{1}|{\bm{x}})\phi^{-}(m)\frac{b(x_{i})-b(m)}{b^{\prime}(m)}\prod_{\ell\in\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})\setminus\{m,m+1\}}\frac{b(m+1)-b(\ell)}{b(m)-b(\ell)}\frac{\prod\limits_{j\neq i}\bigl(b(m)-b(x_{j})\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{j\neq i}\bigl(b(m+1)-b(y_{j})\bigr)}.

Summing the last identity over
(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{i-1},y_{i+1},\dots,y_{n})
with
y_{j}\in\{x_{j+1}+\theta,x_{j+1}+1+\theta,\dots,x_{j}\}
, we conclude that the sum of (
32
) and (
31
) vanishes and (
30
) has no residue and, hence, no singularity at
z=m
.
∎

3
Examples of Dynamical Loop Equations

In this section, we show that many well-known stochastic dynamics, including nonintersecting Bernoulli/Poisson random walks
[
59
,
78
,
67
]
,
\beta
–corners processes
[
60
,
16
]
, Dyson Brownian motion
[
48
]
, measures on Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns
[
60
,
17
,
97
]
, Macdonald processes
[
14
]
, and
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on lozenge tilings
[
19
]
can be transformed to the forms of (
4
), (
27
), and their degenerations. As a corollary, we derive the dynamical loop equations for all these systems.

3.1
Nonintersecting Bernoulli random walks

For
n\geqslant 1
, we consider
n
independent Bernoulli random walks on
\mathbb{Z}
, with each walker jumping by
1
in the positive direction with probability
p\in(0,1)
, or
staying with the complementary probability
1-p
at each time step,
and condition them to never intersect. If we denote the configuration of walkers at time
t
as
{\bm{x}}(t)=(x_{1}(t)>x_{2}(t)>\dots>x_{n}(t))\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
, then for any
{\bm{e}}=\{0,1\}^{n}
, the transition probability
\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}(t+1)={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}}(t)={\bm{x}})
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}(t+1)={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}}(t)={\bm{x}})=\frac{V({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}})}{V({\bm{x}})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}p^{e_{i}}(1-p)^{1-e_{i}},

(34)

where
V({\bm{x}})
is the Vandermonde determinant

\displaystyle V({\bm{x}})=\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}(x_{j}-x_{i}).

(35)

The computation leading to (
34
) can be found in
[
78
]
. Recently, local statistics of nonintersecting Bernoulli random walks were studied in
[
59
]
. There are two natural generalizations of (
34
). First, we can consider a
\theta
version and, second, we can allow the jump probability
p
to depend on
x_{i}
.
Hence, for general
\theta>0
, we fix
\theta=\beta/2
and define the
\theta
-nonintersecting Bernoulli random walk
as a discrete time Markov process
{\bm{x}}(t)=(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t),\dots,x_{n}(t))\in\mathbb{W}^{n}_{\theta}
, as in (
2
),
with the transition probability
\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}(t+1)={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}}(t)={\bm{x}})
given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}(t+1)={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}}(t)={\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{Z({\bm{x}})}\frac{V({\bm{x}}+\theta{\bm{e}})}{V({\bm{x}})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{+}(x_{i})^{e_{i}}\phi^{-}(x_{i})^{1-e_{i}}.

(36)

Corollary 3.1
.

Fix any particle configuration
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1}>x_{2}>\dots>x_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}
and consider transition probability (
36
), with weight functions
\phi^{+}(z),\phi^{-}(z)
analytic in a neighborhood of
[x_{n},x_{1}]
. Then the following observable is analytic in a neighborhood of
[x_{n},x_{1}]

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{z-x_{j}+\theta(1-e_{j})}{z-x_{j}}+\phi^{-}(z)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{z-x_{j}-\theta e_{j}}{z-x_{j}}\right].

We take
b(z)=z
in Theorem
1.1
.
∎

3.2
Nonintersecting Poisson random walks

Taking the limit as
p\rightarrow 0
and scaling to
the continuous time as
\tau=\lfloor{p^{-1}t}\rfloor
, for
t>0
,
turns the nonintersecting Bernoulli random
walk (
34
) into the nonintersecting Poisson random walk. This is a continuous time dynamics obtained by conditioning
n

independent Poisson random walks to never intersect. This Markov chain
{\bm{x}}(t)=(x_{1}(t)>x_{2}(t)>\dots>x_{n}(t))\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
has jump rates

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}(t+{\rm d}t)={\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}}(t)={\bm{x}})=\begin{cases}\frac{V({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}_{j})}{V({\bm{x}})}{\rm d}t+\OO({\rm d}t^{2}),&{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}_{j},\\
1-n{\rm d}t+\OO({\rm d}t^{2}),&{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}},\\
\OO({\rm d}t^{2}),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(37)

In other words, the nonintersecting Poisson random walk is a continuous time Markov process, with generator

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{n}f({\bm{x}})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{V({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}_{j})}{V({\bm{x}})}(f({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}_{j})-f({\bm{x}})),\quad 1\leqslant j\leqslant n.

(38)

This nonintersecting process appeared in various settings, cf.
[
78
,
92
,
15
,
61
]
. More generally, we can also consider a general
\theta
version (as in
[
60
,
67
]
) and add spatial inhomogeneities in
x
.
Thus, we fix
\theta>0
and define the
\theta
-nonintersecting Poisson random walk
, denoted by
{\bm{x}}(t)=(x_{1}(t),x_{2}(t),\dots,x_{n}(t))\in\mathbb{W}^{n}_{\theta}
, as a discrete time Markov process
with generator given by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\theta}^{n}f({\bm{x}})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{V({\bm{x}}+\theta{\bm{e}}_{j})}{V({\bm{x}})}\phi(x_{j})\bigl(f({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}_{j})-f({\bm{x}})\bigr).

(39)

The following statement can be obtained as a limit of Corollary
3.1
; we also provide a direct proof.

Corollary 3.2
.

Take a particle configuration
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1}>x_{2}>\dots>x_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}
and consider the generator (
39
), with weight function
\phi(z)
analytic in a complex neighborhood of
[x_{n},x_{1}+1]
. Let
G({\bm{x}})
be the Stieltjes transform of the particle configuration
{\bm{x}}
:

G({\bm{x}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{z-x_{i}}.

Then the following observable is analytic in the same neighborhood of
[x_{n},x_{1}+1]

\phantom{{}={}}\theta{\mathcal{L}}_{\theta}^{n}G({\bm{x}})+\phi(z)\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{z-x_{j}+\theta}{z-x_{j}}-\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{z-1-x_{j}+\theta}{z-1-x_{j}}\right).

(40)

Let us show that the following expression in complex variable
u
has no singularities:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left[\frac{V({\bm{x}}+\theta{\bm{e}}_{j})}{V({\bm{x}})}\phi(x_{j})\frac{u-x_{j}-\theta}{u-x_{j}}\right]+\phi(z)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{u-x_{j}+\theta}{u-x_{j}}.

(41)

Indeed, the sum might have simple poles at
u=x_{i}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. However, the residue at such a pole is

\displaystyle-\theta\frac{V({\bm{x}}+\theta{\bm{e}}_{i})}{V({\bm{x}})}\phi(x_{i})+\theta\phi(x_{i})\prod_{j:j\neq i}\frac{x_{i}-x_{j}+\theta}{x_{i}-x_{j}}=0.

Therefore (
41
) is analytic in a neighborhood of
[x_{n},x_{1}]
. Subtracting (
41
) at
u=z
and
u=z-1
and noting

\theta{\mathcal{L}}_{\theta}^{n}G({\bm{x}})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{V({\bm{x}}+\theta{\bm{e}}_{j})}{V({\bm{x}})}\phi(x_{j})\left(\frac{z-x_{j}-\theta}{z-x_{j}}-\frac{z-1-x_{j}-\theta}{z-1-x_{j}}\right),

we conclude that (
40
) has no singularities.
∎

The dynamical loop equation for the
\theta
-nonintersecting Poisson random walk with trivial weight
\phi(z)=1
was discovered in
[
67
, Corollary 2.10]
, and used to show that the macroscopic fluctuations of this process with general initial data converge to the Gaussian free field.

3.3
Dyson Brownian motion

Consider nonintersecting Poisson random walk (
37
). Its rescaled version

{\varepsilon^{1/2}({\bm{x}}(\varepsilon^{-1}t)-\varepsilon^{-1}t)}
,
t\geqslant 0
, converges as
\varepsilon\to 0
to the Dyson Brownian motion with
\beta=2
, which is a solution to the following stochastic differential equations:

\displaystyle{\rm d}x_{i}(t)={\rm d}B_{i}(t)+\sum_{j:j\neq i}\frac{{\rm d}t}{x_{i}(t)-x_{j}(t)},\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant n,

(42)

with independent standard Brownian motions
B_{i}(t)
. A generalization of (
42
) involves replacing
\theta=\frac{\beta}{2}=1
by an arbitrary positive real number and adding a potential. The resulting diffusion is a solution to the SDE

\displaystyle{\rm d}x_{i}(t)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}{\rm d}B_{i}(t)+\sum_{j:j\neq i}\frac{{\rm d}t}{x_{i}(t)-x_{j}(t)}+W^{\prime}(x_{i}(t)){\rm d}t,\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant n.

(43)

Similarly to (
42
), it should be possible to obtain (
43
) from (
39
) by a diffusive scaling limit (for
\beta=2\theta\geqslant 1
and no potential this was proven in
[
60
, Theorem 3.2]
, see also
[
67
, Theorem 1.2]
). The solution to (
43
) is called the
\beta
-Dyson Brownian Motion with potential
W
; it originates in the work of Dyson from 1960s on the evolution of eigenvalues of random matrices, cf.
[
48
]
. Recently the Dyson Brownian motion (
43
) played a central role in the three-step approach to the proofs of the universal asymptotic behavior of local statistics of eigenvalues for various ensembles of random matrices in a series of works
[
81
,
80
,
50
,
51
,
49
,
1
]
, developed by Erdős, Yau and their collaborators.

By taking the diffusive scaling limit from the dynamical loop equations of Corollaries
3.1
and
3.2
, we get the following dynamical loop equation for
\beta
-Dyson Brownian motion with analytic potential. We also present a direct proof.

Corollary 3.3
.

Consider the
\beta
-Dyson Brownian motion (
43
), suppose that the potential
W(z)
analytic in a complex neighborhood of the real axis, and take any
0\leqslant t_{1}<t_{2}
. Let

\displaystyle m_{n}(z;t)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{z-x_{i}(t)}.

Then the following stochastic integral is an analytic function of
z
in the same neighborhood

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}

\displaystyle\left({\rm d}m_{n}(z;t)-\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\rm d}B_{i}(t)}{(z-x_{i}(t))^{2}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(\partial_{z}\frac{m_{n}^{2}(z;t)}{2}+\partial_{z}[m_{n}(z;t)W^{\prime}(z)]+\frac{\beta-2}{2\beta}\partial^{2}_{z}m_{n}(z;t)\right)\right){\rm d}t.

By Ito’s formula and (
43
), the integrand simplifies to

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{W^{\prime}(x_{i})-W^{\prime}(z)-(x_{i}(t)-z)W^{\prime\prime}(z)}{(z-x_{i}(t))^{2}}{\rm d}t.

(44)

Analyticity of
W(z)
implies that the expression

\frac{W^{\prime}(x)-W^{\prime}(z)-(x-z)W^{\prime\prime}(z)}{(z-x)^{2}}

is not singular at
z=x
. Hence, (
44
) is analytic.
∎

Special versions of Corollary
3.3
(for trivial potential
W\equiv 0
) were used in
[
72
]
and
[
33
,
62
]
(see also
[
4
, Section 4.3]
) to prove that the macroscopic fluctuations of the
\beta
-Dyson Brownian motion converge to a Gaussian field, which can be further identified with the Gaussian free field, cf.
[
13
,
67
]
. We remark that the loop equations in
[
33
,
62
,
4
]
are different from those in Corollary
3.3
. Instead of the Stieltjes transforms, polynomial test functions are used, and the corresponding loop equations are used to study the evolution of polynomial functionals of Gaussian large random matrices. For convex potential, it has been proven in
[
103
]
that the
\beta
-Dyson Brownian motion converge to a Gaussian field.

3.4
Measures on Gelfand-Tsetlin Patterns

A Gelfand-Tsetlin pattern of depth
T
is an interlacing sequences of signatures

\displaystyle\emptyset\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(1)\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(2)\prec\dots\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(T-1)\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(T).

(45)

The patterns were originally introduced in the context of the branching rules for restrictions of irreducible representations of the unitary group
U(N)
onto the subgroup
U(N-1)
. The characters of these representations are Schur symmetric polynomials and, therefore, the branching rule is equivalent to the following combinatorial formula for the Schur polynomials:

\displaystyle s_{\bm{\lambda}}(u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{T})=\sum_{\emptyset\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(1)\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(2)\prec\dots\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(T-1)\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(T)={\bm{\lambda}}}u_{1}^{|{\bm{\lambda}}(1)|}u_{2}^{|{\bm{\lambda}}(2)|-|{\bm{\lambda}}(1)|}\dots u_{T}^{|{\bm{\lambda}}(T)|-|{\bm{\lambda}}(T-1)|},

(46)

where
|{\bm{\lambda}}(n)|
is the sum of coordinates of a signature
|{\bm{\lambda}}(n)|\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}
,
n=1,2,\dots,T
.
We would like to study the uniform measure on all Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns of depth
T
with the fixed top row
{\bm{\lambda}}(T)
and refer to
[
21
]
and
[
57
, Lecture 22]
for more details on the interplay between Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns, random tilings, and symmetric functions.
The total number of the Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns with a fixed top row can be computed by combining (
46
) with evaluation formulas for the Schur functions (known as Weyl dimension formulas in the representation-theoretic literature):

\displaystyle\sum_{\emptyset\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(1)\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(2)\prec\dots\prec{\bm{\lambda}}(T)={\bm{\lambda}}}1=s_{\bm{\lambda}}(1,1,\dots,1)=\frac{V(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}-1,\lambda_{3}-2,\dots,\lambda_{T}-(T-1))}{V(1,2,\dots,T)},

(47)

where
V
is the Vandermonde determinant (
35
).
We can view the uniform measure on all Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns as a Markov process on signatures with descending transitions from level
T
down to level
1
. The transition probability from
(n+1)
-st level to the
n
-level is computed using (
47
) to be

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{\lambda}}(n)={\bm{\mu}}|{\bm{\lambda}}(n+1)={\bm{\lambda}})=\frac{s_{\bm{\mu}}(\bm{1})}{s_{\bm{\lambda}}(\bm{1})}=\frac{1}{n!}\frac{V(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}-1,\dots,\mu_{n}-(n-1))}{V(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}-1,\dots,\lambda_{n+1}-n)},

(48)

where
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\dots,\lambda_{n+1})\in\mathbb{GT}_{n+1}
,
{\bm{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots,\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}
and
{\bm{\mu}}\prec{\bm{\lambda}}
. We encode
{\bm{\lambda}}
and
{\bm{\mu}}
as particle systems
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{y}}
, respectively as in Section
2.2
with
\theta=1
:
x_{i}=\lambda_{i}-(i-1)
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1
, and
y_{i}=\mu_{i}-(i-1)
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. We can rewrite the transition probability (
48
) as

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{n!}\frac{V({\bm{y}})}{V({\bm{x}})}.

(49)

This matches (
27
), with
\theta=1
,
b(z)=z
,
y_{i}\in\{x_{i+1}+1,x_{i+1}+2,x_{i+1}+2,\dots,x_{i}\}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, and

\displaystyle w(z)=\Gamma(z-x_{n+1})\Gamma(x_{1}-z+1),\qquad\phi^{+}(z)=z-x_{n+1},\quad\phi^{-}(z)=x_{1}-z.

More generally, we consider the Jack-deformed Gibbs distributions on Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns, as introduced by
[
60
, Definition 2.12]
.
For general
\theta>0
, the transition probability is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{\lambda}}(n)={\bm{\mu}}|{\bm{\lambda}}(n+1)={\bm{\lambda}})=\frac{J_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(1;\theta)J_{\bm{\mu}}(\bm{1};\theta)}{J_{\bm{\lambda}}(\bm{1};\theta)},\qquad{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n},\quad{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n+1},\quad{\bm{\mu}}\prec{\bm{\lambda}},

(50)

where
J_{\lambda}
is the Jack symmetric polynomial, and
J_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}
is its skew version. At
\theta=1
,
J_{\lambda}
turns into the Schur polynomial
s_{\lambda}
and
J_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(1,1)
is the indicator of
{\bm{\mu}}\prec{\bm{\lambda}}
; hence, we get back to (
48
).

We encode the signatures
{\bm{\lambda}},{\bm{\mu}}
as particle systems
{\bm{x}},{\bm{y}}
, given by
x_{i}=\lambda_{i}-(i-1)\theta
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1
, and
y_{i}=\mu_{i}-(i-1)\theta
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. This turns the transition probability (
50
) into a special case of (
27
); we recode this statement in the following claim, whose proof is postponed till Appendix
A
.

Claim 3.4
.

The transition probability (
50
) in terms of
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{y}}
is proportional to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})\propto\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}(y_{i}-y_{j})\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}\frac{\Gamma(y_{i}-x_{j+1})\Gamma(x_{i}-y_{j}+\theta)}{\Gamma(y_{i}-x_{j+1}-\theta+1)\Gamma(x_{i}-y_{j}+1)},

(51)

which is identified with a special case of (
27
), with
b(z)=z
and weight
w(z)={\Gamma(x_{1}-z+\theta)}{\Gamma(z-x_{n+1})}
.

Theorem
2.2
gives the dynamical loop equation for transition probabilities generalizing (
51
).

Corollary 3.5
.

Fix
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n+1}
and consider the following transition probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})\propto\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}(y_{i}-y_{j})\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}\frac{\Gamma(y_{i}-x_{j+1})\Gamma(x_{i}-y_{j}+\theta)}{\Gamma(y_{i}-x_{j+1}-\theta+1)\Gamma(x_{i}-y_{j}+1)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}w(y_{i}),

(52)

where
y_{i}\in\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+\theta+1,\dots,x_{i}\}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, and
the weight function
w(z)
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{w(z+1)}{w(z)}=\frac{\phi^{+}(z)}{\phi^{-}(z)}.

If we assume that
\phi^{+}(z),\phi^{-}(z)
are holomorphic in a complex neighborhood of
[x_{n+1}+\theta,x_{1}]
,
then the following observable is holomorphic in the same neighborhood:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}(z-x_{i})}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}(z-y_{i})}-\phi^{-}(z)\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}(z-x_{i}-\theta+1)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}(z-y_{i}+1)}\right].

(53)

Using Claim
3.4
and Theorem
2.2
with
b(z)=z
, we obtain analyticity of the following observable:

\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\cdot(z-x_{n+1})\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}\bigl(z-x_{i}\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(z-y_{i}\bigr)}\right.\\
\left.+\phi^{-}(z)\cdot(x_{1}-z+\theta-1)\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}\bigl(z-x_{i}\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(z+1-y_{i}\bigr)}\prod\limits_{\begin{smallmatrix}\ell\in\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+\theta+1,\dots,x_{i}\}\\
i=1,\dots,n\end{smallmatrix}}\dfrac{\bigl(z+1-\ell\bigr)}{\bigl(z-\ell\bigr)}\right].

(54)

Computing the telescoping product over
\ell
and cancelling the factors, we convert (
54
) into

(z-x_{n+1})\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}\bigl(z-x_{i}\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(z-y_{i}\bigr)}-\phi^{-}(z)\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}\bigl(z-x_{i}-\theta+1\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(z+1-y_{i}\bigr)}\right].

The last expression differs from (
53
) by
(z-x_{n+1})
factor, hence, we have shown so far only that (
53
) is holomorphic except, possibly, at
z=x_{n+1}
. However, neither of the terms under expectation in (
53
) have a singularity at
z=x_{n+1}
. Thus, the sum is holomorphic also at
z=x_{n+1}
.
∎

3.5
\beta
–Corners processes

Let us consider again the uniform measure on Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns (
47
), (
48
) and rescale the discrete integer lattice to the continuous space via
{\bm{x}}=\lfloor{\tilde{\bm{x}}/\delta}\rfloor
,
\delta\to 0
. After the limit, the transition probability from the particle configuration
\tilde{\bm{x}}=(\tilde{x}_{1}>\tilde{x}_{2}>\dots>\tilde{x}_{n+1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
at level
n+1
to the particle configuration
\tilde{\bm{y}}=(\tilde{y}_{1}>\tilde{y}_{2}>\dots>\tilde{y}_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
at level
n
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\bm{y}}|\tilde{\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{n!}\frac{V(\tilde{\bm{y}})}{V(\tilde{\bm{x}})},

(55)

with additional requirement that
\tilde{\bm{y}}
interlaces with
\tilde{\bm{x}}
, i.e.
\tilde{x}_{i+1}<\tilde{y}_{i}<\tilde{x}_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. The same limit can be performed for each
\theta>0
in transition probabilities (
51
) and (
52
), resulting in

\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\bm{y}}|\tilde{\bm{x}})\propto\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\tilde{y}_{i}-\tilde{y}_{j})\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\tilde{y}_{i}-\tilde{x}_{i+1})^{\theta-1}(\tilde{x}_{i}-\tilde{y}_{j})^{\theta-1}\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{W(\tilde{y}_{i})},

(56)

again with interlacing
\tilde{\bm{x}}
and
\tilde{\bm{y}}
. The distributions of the form (
56
) for specific choices of
W(y)
and their discrete approximations were recently introduced in
[
60
,
16
]
under the name of
\beta
–corners processes with
\beta=2\theta
, see also
[
55
]
. As shown in
[
91
]
with ideas going back as far as
[
56
]
, at
\beta=1,2,4
, the transition probabilities of this type appear in joint distributions of eigenvalues of corners of self-adjoint real/complex/quaternionic matrices with uniformly random eigenvectors.

Here is the dynamical loop equation for the
\beta
–corners process with analytic potential.

Corollary 3.6
.

Fix
\tilde{\bm{x}}=(\tilde{x}_{1}>\tilde{x}_{2}>\dots>\tilde{x}_{n+1})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
and consider the following transition probability:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\bm{y}}|\tilde{\bm{x}})\propto\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}(\tilde{y}_{i}-\tilde{y}_{j})\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\tilde{y}_{i}-\tilde{x}_{j+1})^{\theta-1}\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}(\tilde{x}_{i}-\tilde{y}_{j})^{\theta-1}\prod_{i=1}^{n}e^{W(\tilde{y}_{i})},\\
&\tilde{\bm{y}}=(\tilde{y}_{1}>\tilde{y}_{2}>\dots>\tilde{y}_{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\quad\tilde{x}_{i+1}<\tilde{y}_{i}<\tilde{x}_{i},\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant n.\end{split}

Suppose that
\theta>0
and the weight function
W(z)
is holomorphic in a complex neighborhood of
[\tilde{x}_{n+1},\tilde{x}_{1}]
. Then
the following function of
\tilde{z}
is holomorphic in the same neighborhood:

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n+1}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{x}_{j})}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{y}_{j})}\left(W^{\prime}(\tilde{z})+\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\frac{\theta-1}{\tilde{z}-\tilde{x}_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\tilde{z}-y_{j}}\right)\right].

(57)

Let us compute the scaling limit for the dynamical loop equation (
53
), setting
{\bm{x}}=\lfloor{\tilde{\bm{x}}/\delta}\rfloor
,
{\bm{y}}=\lfloor{\tilde{\bm{y}}/\delta}\rfloor
,
z=\tilde{z}/\delta
,
w(z)=\exp(W(\delta z))
. We can further set

\phi^{-}(z)=1,\qquad\phi^{+}(z)=\frac{w(z+1)}{w(z)}=e^{\delta W^{\prime}(\delta z)+\OO(\delta^{2})}.

Multiplying (
53
) by
\delta^{-1}
, for
\tilde{z}
outside
[\tilde{x}_{n+1},\tilde{x}_{1}]
we get

\delta^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n+1}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{x}_{j})}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{y}_{j})}\left(\exp\bigl(W(\tilde{z}+\delta)-W(\tilde{z})\bigr)-\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n+1}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{x}_{j}+\delta(1-\theta))}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n+1}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{x}_{j})}\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{y}_{j})}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{y}_{j}+\delta)}\right)\right]\\
=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n+1}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{x}_{j})}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}(\tilde{z}-\tilde{y}_{j})}\left(W^{\prime}(\tilde{z})+\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\frac{\theta-1}{\tilde{z}-\tilde{x}_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\tilde{z}-\tilde{y}_{j}}+\OO(\delta)\right)\right].

(58)

Let us denote through
f_{\delta}(\tilde{z})
the expression of (
58
) and by
f_{0}(\tilde{z})
its
\delta\to 0
limit, which matches (
57
). Choose a positively oriented complex contour
\gamma
enclosing
[\tilde{x}_{n+1},\tilde{x}_{1}]
, such that
f_{\delta}(\tilde{z})
is holomorphic inside
\gamma
by Corollary
3.5
, and use the Cauchy integral formula to write for
\tilde{z}
inside
\gamma
:

f_{\delta}(\tilde{z})=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\int_{\gamma}\frac{f_{\delta}(u)}{u-\tilde{z}}{\rm d}u.

Using the computation (
58
), we send
\delta\to 0
in the last identity and get the Cauchy integral formula for
f_{0}(\tilde{z})
, which implies that
f_{0}(\tilde{z})
is holomorphic.
∎

3.6
Macdonald processes

We use the following notations:

\displaystyle(a;q)_{\infty}=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1-aq^{i-1}),\quad f(u)=\frac{(tu;q)_{\infty}}{(qu;q)_{\infty}},\quad\Gamma_{q}(x)=(1-q)^{1-x}\frac{(q;q)_{\infty}}{(q^{x};q)_{\infty}}.

(59)

3.6.1
Macdonald polynomials and specializations

The next class of Markov chains is built out of the specializations of Macdonald polynomials. We refer to
[
82
, Section VI]
and
[
14
]
for definitions and properties of Macdonald symmetric functions and use them as a black box in this section. Macdonald symmetric functions
P
and
Q
are indexed by
partitions and implicitly depend on two parameters
q,t\in(0,1)
. The coefficients for the symmetric functions are in
\mathbb{Q}[q,t]
. Macdonald symmetric functions
P_{\bm{\lambda}}({\bm{x}};q,t)
and
Q_{\bm{\lambda}}({\bm{x}};q,t)
are elements of the algebra
\Lambda
of the symmetric functions in infinitely many variables
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
uniquely determined by the following two properties:

1.

P_{\bm{\lambda}}
,
|\bm{\lambda}|=m
, can be expressed in terms of the monomial symmetric functions via a strictly upper unitriangular transition matrix:

P_{\bm{\lambda}}=m_{\bm{\lambda}}+\sum_{{\bm{\mu}}<{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{Y}_{m}}R_{{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\mu}}}P_{{\bm{\mu}}},

where
R_{{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\mu}}}
are functions of
q,t
and
