{\bm{\mu}}<{\bm{\lambda}}
is comparison in the dominance order on the set
\mathbb{Y}_{m}
of all partitions of
m
(equivalently, Young diagrams with
m
boxes).

2.

They are pairwise orthogonal with respect to the scalar product defined on the power sums via

\langle p_{\bm{\lambda}},p_{\bm{\mu}}\rangle_{q,t}=\delta_{{\bm{\lambda}}{\bm{\mu}}}z_{\bm{\lambda}}(q,t),\qquad p_{\lambda}=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}p_{\lambda_{i}},\qquad z_{\bm{\lambda}}(q,t)=\prod_{i\geqslant 1}i^{m_{i}}(m_{i})!\prod_{i=1}^{\ell({\bm{\lambda}})}\frac{1-q^{\lambda_{i}}}{1-t^{\lambda_{i}}},

where
{\bm{\lambda}}=1^{m_{1}}2^{m_{2}}\dots
, i.e.
m_{i}
is the multiplicity of
i
in
{\bm{\lambda}}
,
\ell({\bm{\lambda}})
is the number of rows, and
p_{k}=(x_{1})^{k}+(x_{2})^{k}+\dots
,
k\geqslant 1
.

We further define
Q_{\bm{\lambda}}=\frac{P_{\bm{\lambda}}}{\langle P_{\bm{\lambda}},P_{\bm{\lambda}}\rangle_{q,t}}.
Finally, the skew Macdonald polynomials
P_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}
and
Q_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}
are defined through the expansions:

\displaystyle P_{\bm{\lambda}}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,y_{1},y_{2},\dots;q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{\mu}}}P_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(x_{1},x_{1},\dots;q,t)P_{\bm{\mu}}(y_{1},y_{2},\dots;q,t),

\displaystyle Q_{\bm{\lambda}}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,y_{1},y_{2},\dots;q,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\bm{\mu}}}Q_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(x_{1},x_{1},\dots;q,t)Q_{\bm{\mu}}(y_{1},y_{2},\dots;q,t).

Computations in the algebra of symmetric functions
\Lambda
can be converted into numeric identities by means of
specializations
, which are algebra homomorphism from
\Lambda
to the set of complex numbers. Specialization
\rho
is uniquely determined by its values on any set of algebraic generators of
\Lambda
and we use
(p_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}
as such generators. The value of
\rho
on a symmetric function
f
is denoted
f(\rho)
.
Given two specializations
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
, we define their union
(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
through the formula:

p_{k}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=p_{k}(\rho)+p_{k}(\rho^{\prime}),\quad k\geqslant 1.

A specialization
\rho
is called
Macdonald nonnegative
if its values on all (skew) Macdonald symmetric functions are non-negative, i.e., if for all partitions
\bm{\lambda}
and
\bm{\mu}
,

\displaystyle P_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\rho;q,t)\geqslant 0,

The description of Macdonald nonnegative specializations was conjectured in
[
77
]
and proven in
[
83
]
:

Theorem 3.7
(
[
83
]
).

For any fixed
q,t\in(0,1)
, Macdonald nonnegative specializations can be parameterized by triplets
(\bm{\alpha}=\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\geqslant 1},\bm{\beta}=\{\beta_{i}\}_{i\geqslant 1},\gamma)
of nonnegative numbers satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i})<\infty
. The specialization
\rho
corresponding to a triplet
(\bm{\alpha},\bm{\beta},\gamma)
is defined by for
k\geqslant 2

p_{1}(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}+\frac{1-q}{1-t}\left(\gamma+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\beta_{i}\right),\qquad p_{k}(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\alpha_{i})^{k}+(-1)^{k-1}\frac{1-q^{k}}{1-t^{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\beta_{i})^{k}.

Following
[
14
, Section 2.3]
, we create Markov chains out of the Macdonald-positive specializations:

Definition 3.8
.

Given two specializations
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
we define the ascending transition through

\mathbb{P}({\bm{\lambda}}\mid{\bm{\mu}})=\frac{1}{\Pi(\rho;\rho^{\prime})}\frac{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(\rho;q,t)}{P_{\bm{\mu}}(\rho;q,t)}Q_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\rho^{\prime};q,t),

(60)

where
{\bm{\lambda}}
and
{\bm{\mu}}
are partitions with
{\bm{\mu}}\subset{\bm{\lambda}}
and
\Pi(\rho;\rho^{\prime})
is the result of applying
\rho
to the
x_{i}
variables and
\rho^{\prime}
to the
y_{j}
variables in the infinite product

\Pi=\prod_{i,j\geqslant 1}\frac{(tx_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}{(x_{i}y_{j};q)_{\infty}}.

Definition 3.9
.

Given two specializations
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
we define the descending transition through

\mathbb{P}({\bm{\mu}}\mid{\bm{\lambda}})=\frac{P_{\bm{\mu}}(\rho;q,t)}{P_{\bm{\lambda}}(\rho,\rho^{\prime};q,t)}Q_{{\bm{\lambda}}/{\bm{\mu}}}(\rho^{\prime};q,t),

where
{\bm{\lambda}}
and
{\bm{\mu}}
are partitions with
{\bm{\mu}}\subset{\bm{\lambda}}
.

3.6.2
Loop equations for the ascending process

For particular choices of
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
, the ascending process of Definition
3.8
fits into our formalism of dynamic loop equations. Namely, we fix
n=1,2,\dots
and set in the notations of Theorem
3.7

\rho:\,\alpha_{i}=t^{i-1},\,1\leqslant i\leqslant n;\qquad\rho^{\prime}:\beta_{1}=b,

(61)

with all other parameters set to
0
.

Claim 3.10
.

The transition probability of Definition
3.8
under the specializations (
61
) is non-degenerate only for partitions
{\bm{\lambda}},{\bm{\mu}}
with at most
n
parts, i.e.
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lambda_{n})
and
{\bm{\mu}}=(\mu_{1}\geqslant\mu_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\mu_{n})
. Further, if we set
t=q^{\theta}
and identify

{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n},\quad x_{i}=\mu_{i}-\theta(i-1),\quad x_{i}+e_{i}=\lambda_{i}-\theta(i-1),\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant n,

(62)

then
e_{i}\in\{0,1\}
and the transition probability is proportional to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})\propto b^{\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{q^{x_{i}+\theta e_{i}}-q^{x_{j}+\theta e_{j}}}{q^{x_{i}}-q^{x_{j}}},

(63)

which is a special case of (
4
), with
b(z)=q^{z}
and
\phi^{+}(z)=b,\phi^{-}(z)=1
.

By taking
q\rightarrow 1
, Macdonald symmetric polynomials recover the Jack symmetric polynomials with parameter
\theta
. The global fluctuations of these measures related to Jack symmetric polynomials have been previously studied in
[
42
,
85
,
70
]
by different methods. We postpone the proof of Claim
3.10
till Appendix
A
.
Theorem
1.1
gives the dynamical loop equation for transition probabilities generalizing the ascending Macdonald process (
63
).

Corollary 3.11
.

Fix
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1}>x_{2}>\dots>x_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}
, and consider the following transition probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})\propto\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{q^{x_{i}+\theta e_{i}}-q^{x_{j}+\theta e_{j}}}{q^{x_{i}}-q^{x_{j}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{+}(x_{i})^{e_{i}}\phi^{-}(x_{i})^{1-e_{i}},\quad{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}.

Suppose that the weighs
\phi^{+}(z),\phi^{-}(z)
are holomorphic functions in a neighborhood of
[x_{n},x_{1}]
. Then
the following observable is holomorphic in the same neighborhood

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{q^{z+\theta}-q^{x_{j}+\theta e_{j}}}{q^{z}-q^{x_{i}}}+\phi^{-}(z)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{q^{z}-q^{x_{j}+\theta e_{j}}}{q^{z}-q^{x_{j}}}\right].

This is Theorem
1.1
with
b(z)=q^{z}
.
∎

3.6.3
Loop equations for the descending process

For particular choices of
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
, the descending process of Definition
3.9
also fits into our formalism of dynamic loop equations. Namely, we fix
n=1,2,\dots
and set in the notations of Theorem
3.7

\rho:\,\alpha_{i}=t^{i-1},\,1\leqslant i\leqslant n;\qquad\rho^{\prime}:\alpha_{1}=t^{n},

(64)

with all other parameters setting to
0
.

Claim 3.12
.

The transition probability of Definition
3.9
under the specializations (
64
) is non-degenerate only for partitions
{\bm{\lambda}}
with at most
(n+1)
parts, i.e.
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lambda_{n+1})
, and
{\bm{\mu}}
with at most
n
parts
{\bm{\mu}}=(\mu_{1}\geqslant\mu_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\mu_{n})
, and such that
{\bm{\mu}}\prec{\bm{\lambda}}
. Further, if we set
t=q^{\theta}
and denote

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n+1},

\displaystyle x_{i}=\lambda_{i}-\theta(i-1),\quad

\displaystyle 1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1,

\displaystyle{\bm{y}}=(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n},

\displaystyle y_{i}=\mu_{i}-\theta(i-1),\quad

\displaystyle 1\leqslant i\leqslant n,

then the transition probability is proportional to

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}(q^{-y_{i}}-q^{-y_{j}})\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}-x_{j+1})}{\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}-x_{j+1}+1-\theta)}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(x_{i}-y_{j}+\theta)}{\Gamma_{q}(x_{i}-y_{j}+1)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}q^{-((\theta-1)(n-i)+\theta)y_{i}},

(65)

which is a special case of (
27
), with
b(z)=q^{-z}
and weight

w(z)=q^{-z^{2}/2+z(x_{n+1}+1/2)}{\Gamma_{q}(x_{1}-z+\theta)}{\Gamma_{q}(z-x_{n+1})}.

(66)

We postpone the proof of Claim
3.12
till Appendix
A
. Note that for special values of
\theta
, the weights (
65
) can be dramatically simplified. For instance, at
\theta=1
all
\Gamma_{q}
factors cancel out and the
\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}
part disappears from the formula. For
\theta=2
, the formula is also simple:

\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})\propto\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}(q^{-y_{i}}-q^{-y_{j}})\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}\bigl[(1-q^{y_{i}-x_{j+1}-1})(1-q^{x_{i}-y_{j}+1})\bigr]\prod_{i=1}^{n}q^{-(n-i+2)y_{i}}.

(67)

Theorem
2.2
gives the dynamical loop equation for transition probabilities generalizing (
65
).

Corollary 3.13
.

Fix
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n+1}
and consider the following transition probability

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}(q^{-y_{i}}-q^{-y_{j}})\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}-x_{j+1})}{\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}-x_{j+1}+1-\theta)}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(x_{i}-y_{j}+\theta)}{\Gamma_{q}(x_{i}-y_{j}+1)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}q^{-((\theta-1)(n-i)+\theta)y_{i}}w(y_{i}),

with
y_{i}\in\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+1+\theta,x_{i+1}+2+\theta,\dots,x_{i}-1,x_{i}\}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Suppose that

\displaystyle\frac{w(z+1)}{w(z)}=\frac{\phi^{+}(z)}{\phi^{-}(z)},

with
\phi^{+}(z)
and
\phi^{-}(z)
holomorphic in a complex neighborhood of
[x_{n+1},x_{1}]
.
Then the following observable is holomorphic in the same neighborhood:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n+1}(q^{-z}-q^{-x_{j}})}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}(q^{-z}-q^{-y_{j}})}-q^{(\theta-1)(n+1)}\phi^{-}(z)\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n+1}(q^{-z}-q^{-x_{i}+1-\theta})}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}(q^{-z-1}-q^{-y_{j}})}\right].

Using Claim
3.12
and Theorem
2.2
with
b(z)=q^{-z}
, we obtain the holomorphicity of

\mathbb{E}\left[(1-q^{z-x_{n+1}})\phi^{+}(z)\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}\bigl(q^{-z}-q^{-x_{i}}\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(q^{-z}-q^{-y_{i}}\bigr)}\right.\\
+\left.(1-q^{x_{1}-z+\theta-1})q^{z-x_{n+1}}\phi^{-}(z)\prod\limits_{\ell\in\mathbb{L}({\bm{x}})}\dfrac{\bigl(q^{-z-1}-q^{-\ell}\bigr)}{\bigl(q^{-z}-q^{-\ell}\bigr)}\cdot\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}\bigl(q^{-z}-q^{-x_{i}}\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(q^{-z-1}-q^{-y_{i}}\bigr)}\right].

(68)

Let us simplify the product over
\ell
in the second term:

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\,\prod_{\ell\in\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+1+\theta,\dots,x_{i}-1,x_{i}\}}\dfrac{q^{-1}\bigl(q^{-z}-q^{1-\ell}\bigr)}{\bigl(q^{-z}-q^{-\ell}\bigr)}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left[q^{-(x_{i}-x_{i+1}+1-\theta)}\frac{q^{-z}-q^{-x_{i+1}+1-\theta}}{q^{-z}-q^{-x_{i}}}\right].

Therefore, (
68
) is

(1-q^{z-x_{n+1}})\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\dfrac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}\bigl(q^{-z}-q^{-x_{i}}\bigr)}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(q^{-z}-q^{-y_{i}}\bigr)}\right.\\
-\left.q^{(\theta-1)(n+1)}\phi^{-}(z)\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n+1}(q^{-z}-q^{-x_{i}+1-\theta})}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\bigl(q^{-z-1}-q^{-y_{i}}\bigr)}\right].

It remains to note that neither of the terms under expectation has a singularity at
z=x_{n+1}
and, hence, we can divide by
(1-q^{z-x_{n+1}})
and the result is still holomorphic.
∎

3.7
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on tilings and Koornwinder polynomials

In this section we first explain how
(q,\kappa)
–distributions on tilings of Section
1.3
fit into the formalism of dynamic loop equations. Then we show that these distributions are particular cases of more general Markov chains which are obtained from quasi-branching rules for Koornwinder symmetric polynomials.

3.7.1
Weighted lozenge tilings

We deal with random lozenge tilings of a trapezoid, as in Figures
2
and
6
. The probability of each tiling
{\mathcal{T}}
is proportional to the weight given in terms of

lozenges, as in (
8
), (
9
):

\displaystyle w({\mathcal{T}})=\prod_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}\in{\mathcal{T}}}w({\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}),\qquad w({\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}})=\kappa q^{x-t/2}-\kappa^{-1}q^{-x+t/2}=\kappa q^{\tilde{x}}-\kappa^{-1}q^{-\tilde{x}}.

(69)

We further identify a tiling with a collection of
N
non-intersecting random walks
{\bm{x}}(t)=(x_{1}(t)>\dots>x_{n}(t))
,
0\leqslant t\leqslant T
, by tracing the trajectories of

and

lozenges, as in Figure
6
. The trapezoid is uniquely determined by specifying
N
,
T
, arbitrary initial configuration
{\bm{x}}(0)
, and prefixed ending configuration
{\bm{x}}(T)=(N-1,N-2,\dots,0)
. The measure (
69
) leads to a Markov chain structures on
{\bm{x}}(t)
with explicit transition probabilities.

Fig 6:
Tracking positions of particles in non-intersecting paths, tilings turn into a Markov chain in
\mathbb{W}^{N}
.

Theorem 3.14
.

\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
corresponding to the
(q,\kappa)
-distributions on lozenge tilings with weights (
69
) is a Markov chain with transition probability proportional to

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}(t+1)={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}}(t)={\bm{x}})\propto\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant N}\frac{b_{t}(x_{i}+e_{i})-b_{t}(x_{j}+e_{j})}{b_{t}(x_{i})-b_{t}(x_{j})}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{t}^{+}(x_{i})^{e_{i}}\phi_{t}^{-}(x_{i})^{1-e_{i}},

(70)

where
{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{N}
and

b_{t}(x)=q^{-x}+\kappa^{2}q^{x-t},

\phi_{t}^{+}(x)=q^{T+N-1-t}(1-q^{x-N+1})(1-\kappa^{2}q^{x-T+1}),\qquad\phi_{t}^{-}(x)=-(1-q^{x+T-t})(1-\kappa^{2}q^{x+N-t}).

We remark that the weights
\phi_{t}^{\pm}(x)
encode a part of the boundary of the tiled trapezoid. More precisely,
\phi_{t}^{+}(x)
has a zero at
x=N-1
and
\phi_{t}^{-}(x)
has a zero at
t-x=T
. Therefore, if at time
t
a particle is at
N-1
, it will stay; if a particle is at
t-T
, it will jump one unit to the right.

Up to shift
x\mapsto x+S
, this is
[
19
, Proposition 4.2]
. Let us, however, present a sketch of the proof. The Markov property that “given present, future and past are independent” immediately follows from the definition of the system. Further, let
Z(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};T)
denote the partition function, i.e. this is the total sum of weights (
69
) over the tilings corresponding to non-intersecting paths
{\bm{x}}(t)=(x_{1}(t)>\dots>x_{n}(t))
,
0\leqslant t\leqslant T
, with
{\bm{x}}(0)=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
and
{\bm{x}}(T)=(N-1,N-2,\dots,0)
. We can express

\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}(t+1)={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}}(t)={\bm{x}})=\frac{Z({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}};T-t-1)}{Z({\bm{x}};T-t)}\prod_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}}\left(\kappa q^{x({\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}})-t/2}-\kappa^{-1}q^{-x({\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}})+t/2}\right),

(71)

where the product is taken over all horizontal lozenges on the vertical line with ordinate
t+1
; the positions of these lozenges complement the configuration
{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}
. The key observation is that
Z(x_{1},\dots,x_{N};T)
has an explicit product formula: this computation relying on explicit evaluation of certain determinants is present in
[
19
, Appendix]
; another way to get the same answer is through explicit evaluation of principally-specialized Koornwinder polynomials, as we explain in Theorem
3.19
below. Plugging the result into (
71
), we arrive at (
70
).
∎

Theorem
1.1
gives the dynamical loop equation for transition probabilities (
70
).

Corollary 3.15
.

Fix
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1}>x_{2}>\dots>x_{n})\in\mathbb{W}^{n}
and
0\leqslant t<T
. Consider transition probability (
70
).
Then the following observable is holomorphic in a complex neighborhood of
[x_{n},x_{1}]
,

\mathbb{E}\left[q^{T+N-1-t}(1-q^{z-N+1})(1-\kappa^{2}q^{z-T+1})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{b_{t}(z+\theta)-b_{t}(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b_{t}(z)-b_{t}(x_{j})}\right.\\
-\left.(1-q^{z+T-t})(1-\kappa^{2}q^{z+N-t})\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{b_{t}(z)-b_{t}(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b_{t}(z)-b_{t}(x_{j})}\right],

where
b_{t}(z)=q^{-z}+\kappa^{2}q^{z-t}
.

3.7.2
Quasi-branching of Koornwinder symmetric polynomials

Following the notations in
[
98
, Definition 2]
, we denote
K_{{\bm{\lambda}}}^{(n)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};q,t;t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})
the Koorwinder symmetric (Laurent) polynomial in
n
variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
. We explained in Sections
3.4
and
3.6
how branching rules for Schur, Jack, and Macdonald polynomials lead to measures on Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns, which fit into our formalism of dynamical loop equations. Generally speaking, the branching rules for Koornwinder polynomials are much more complicated, and do not lead to any simple measures on Gelfand–Tsetlin patterns. However, when we deal with a special principal specialization, there is a dramatic simplification known as
quasi-branching rule
. The following result is
[
98
, Theorem 5.21]
.

Theorem 3.16
.

For any partition
{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n+1}
with
\lambda_{n+1}\geqslant 0
, we have

\frac{K_{{\bm{\lambda}}}^{(n+1)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},t_{0};\,q,t;\,t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})}{K_{{\bm{\lambda}}}^{(n+1)}(q^{n}t_{0},q^{n-1}t_{0},\dots,t_{0};\,q,t;\,t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})}\\
=\sum_{{\bm{\mu}}\prec{\bm{\lambda}}}\psi_{{\bm{\lambda}}\setminus{\bm{\mu}}}^{(i)}(t^{n+1};\,q,t,t^{n}\sqrt{t_{0}t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}/qt})\frac{K_{\bm{\mu}}^{(n)}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n};\,q,t;\,t_{0}t,t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})}{K_{\bm{\mu}}^{(n)}(q^{n}t_{0},q^{n-1}t_{0},\dots,qt_{0};\,q,t;\,t_{0}t,t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})},

(72)

where the coefficients are given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{{\bm{\lambda}}\setminus{\bm{\mu}}}^{(i)}(u;q,t,s)&=(u/t)^{|{\bm{\lambda}}|-|{\bm{\mu}}|}t^{n({\bm{\mu}})-n({\bm{\lambda}})}\frac{C_{\bm{\lambda}}^{0}(s^{2}qt/u;q,t)C_{\bm{\mu}}^{0}(u/t;q,t)}{C_{\bm{\lambda}}^{0}(u;q,t)C_{\bm{\mu}}^{0}(s^{2}qt/u;q,t)}\\
&\prod_{(i,j)\in{\bm{\lambda}}\atop\lambda_{j}^{\prime}=\mu_{j}^{\prime}}\frac{1-q^{\lambda_{i}+j-1}t^{-\lambda_{j}^{\prime}-i+3}s^{2}}{1-q^{\mu_{i}-j+1}t^{\mu_{j}^{\prime}-i}}\prod_{(i,j)\in{\bm{\lambda}}\atop\lambda_{j}^{\prime}\neq\mu_{j}^{\prime}}\frac{1-q^{\lambda_{i}-j}t^{\lambda_{j}^{\prime}-i+1}}{1-q^{\mu_{i}+j}t^{-\mu_{j}^{\prime}-i+1}s^{2}}\\
&\prod_{(i,j)\in{\bm{\mu}}\atop\lambda_{j}^{\prime}=\mu_{j}^{\prime}}\frac{1-q^{\lambda_{i}-j+1}t^{\lambda_{j}^{\prime}-i}}{1-q^{\mu_{i}+j-1}t^{2-\mu_{j}^{\prime}-i}s^{2}}\prod_{(i,j)\in{\bm{\mu}}\atop\lambda_{j}^{\prime}\neq\mu_{j}^{\prime}}\frac{1-q^{\lambda_{i}+j}t^{2-\lambda_{j}^{\prime}-i}s^{2}}{1-q^{\mu_{i}-j}t^{\mu_{j}^{\prime}-i+1}},\end{split}

(73)

with
|{\bm{\lambda}}|=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\lambda_{i}
,
n(\bm{\lambda})=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}-1)/2
, and similarly for
{\bm{\mu}}
. Also

C_{\bm{\mu}}^{0}(x;q,t)=\prod_{(i,j)\in{\bm{\mu}}}(1-q^{j-1}t^{1-i}x)=\prod_{i\geqslant 1}(t^{1-i}x;q)_{\mu_{i}}.

By taking principle specialization on both sides of (
72
), i.e.
x_{i}=q^{n+1-i}t_{0}
with
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, we get a probability measure over Gelfand-Tsetlin patterns, with the transition probability given by

\mathbb{P}({\bm{\mu}}|{\bm{\lambda}})=\psi_{{\bm{\lambda}}\setminus{\bm{\mu}}}^{(i)}(t^{n+1};q,t,t^{n}\sqrt{t_{0}t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}/qt}),\qquad{\bm{\lambda}}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n+1},\,\lambda_{n+1}\geqslant 0,\,{\bm{\mu}}\in\mathbb{GT}_{n},\,{\bm{\mu}}\prec{\bm{\lambda}}.

(74)

We denote
t=q^{\theta}
for some
\theta>0
, and encode
{\bm{\lambda}}
and
{\bm{\mu}}
as particle systems
{\bm{x}}
and
{\bm{y}}
, with

\displaystyle{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n+1},

\displaystyle x_{i}=\lambda_{i}-\theta(i-1),\quad

\displaystyle 1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1,

\displaystyle{\bm{y}}=(y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n+1})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n},

\displaystyle y_{i}=\mu_{i}-\theta(i-1),\quad

\displaystyle 1\leqslant i\leqslant n.

The interlacing condition
{\bm{\mu}}\prec{\bm{\lambda}}
becomes
x_{i+1}+\theta\leqslant y_{i}\leqslant x_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. The transition probability (
74
) becomes a special case of (
27
), as Claim
3.17
(whose proof is postponed till Appendix
A
) shows:

Claim 3.17
.

Let
q^{v/2}=t^{n}\sqrt{t_{0}t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}/qt}
. We can rewrite the transition probability (
74
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})&\propto\prod_{1\leqslant i<k\leqslant n}(q^{-y_{i}}-q^{-y_{k}})\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k\leqslant n}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}-x_{k+1})\Gamma_{q}(x_{i}-y_{k}+\theta)}{\Gamma_{q}(x_{i}-y_{k}+1)\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}-x_{k+1}-\theta+1)}\\
&\times\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k\leqslant n}(1-q^{y_{i}+y_{k}+v})\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{n+1}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}+x_{k}+v+\theta)}{\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}+x_{k}+1+v)}\right]\prod_{i=1}^{n}q^{-((\theta-1)(n-i)+\theta)y_{i}},\end{split}

(75)

which is a special case of (
27
), with
b(z)=q^{-z}+q^{z+v}
and weight

\displaystyle w(z)=q^{-z^{2}/2+z(x_{n+1}+1/2)}{\Gamma_{q}(x_{1}-z+\theta)}{\Gamma_{q}(z-x_{n+1})}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(z+x_{1}+v+\theta)}{\Gamma_{q}(z+x_{n+1}+v+1)}.

Corollary 3.18
.

Fix
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n+1}
and consider the following transition probability

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}|{\bm{x}})&\propto\prod_{1\leqslant i<k\leqslant n}(q^{-y_{i}}-q^{-y_{k}})\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k\leqslant n}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}-x_{k+1})\Gamma_{q}(x_{i}-y_{k}+\theta)}{\Gamma_{q}(x_{i}-y_{k}+1)\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}-x_{k+1}-\theta+1)}\\
&\times\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k\leqslant n}(1-q^{y_{i}+y_{k}+v})\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{k=1}^{n+1}\frac{\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}+x_{k}+v+\theta)}{\Gamma_{q}(y_{i}+x_{k}+1+v)}\right]\prod_{i=1}^{n}q^{-((\theta-1)(n-i)+\theta)y_{i}}w(y_{i}),\end{split}

(76)

with
y_{i}\in\{x_{i+1}+\theta,x_{i+1}+1+\theta,x_{i+1}+2+\theta,\dots,x_{i}-1,x_{i}\}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Suppose that

\displaystyle\frac{w(z+1)}{w(z)}=\frac{\phi^{+}(z)}{\phi^{-}(z)},

with
\phi^{+}(z)
and
\phi^{-}(z)
holomorphic in a complex neighborhood of
[x_{n+1},x_{1}]
.
Then the following observable is holomorphic in the same neighborhood:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{+}(z)\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n+1}(q^{-z}+q^{z+v}-q^{-x_{j}}-q^{x_{j}+v})}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}(q^{-z}+q^{z+v}-q^{-y_{j}}-q^{y_{i}+v})}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-q^{(\theta-1)(n+1)}\phi^{-}(z)\frac{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n+1}(q^{-z}+q^{z+v}-q^{-x_{i}+1-\theta}-q^{x_{i}+\theta-1+v})}{\prod\limits_{j=1}^{n}(q^{-z-1}+q^{z+1+v}-q^{-y_{j}}-q^{y_{j}+v})}\right].

We omit the proof, as it is very similar to Corollaries
3.5
and
3.13
.

3.7.3
Koornwinder polynomials at
q=t
and tilings

In this section we explain that
q=t
case of the measures of Section
3.7.2
matches the measures on tilings of Section
3.7.1
after the particle-hole involution. We start from a statement of independent interest.

Theorem 3.19
.

For any
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}
, the following identity of Laurent polynomials in
\sigma
holds:

\sum_{\emptyset={\bm{\lambda}}^{(0)}\prec\dots\prec{\bm{\lambda}}^{(n)}={\bm{\lambda}}}\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(\sigma q^{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}+\frac{k+1}{2}-i}-\frac{1}{\sigma q^{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}+\frac{k+1}{2}-i}}\right)=Z_{n}\prod_{i<j}\bigl[x(\lambda_{i}-i)-x(\lambda_{j}-j)\bigr],

(77)

where

x(\lambda_{i}-i)=\sigma q^{\lambda_{i}-i+\frac{n+1}{2}}+\frac{1}{\sigma q^{\lambda_{i}-i+\frac{n+1}{2}}},

Z_{n}=(-1)^{-n(n-1)/2}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{q^{(i-\frac{n+1}{2})i}}{(q;q)_{i-1}}.

Remark 3.20
.

An equivalent form of the same statement is

\sum_{\emptyset={\bm{\lambda}}^{(0)}\prec\dots\prec{\bm{\lambda}}^{(n)}={\bm{\lambda}}}\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\sigma q^{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}+\frac{k+1}{2}-i}-\frac{1}{\sigma q^{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}+\frac{k+1}{2}-i}}}{\sigma q^{\frac{k+1}{2}-i}-\frac{1}{\sigma q^{\frac{k+1}{2}-i}}}=\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{x(\lambda_{i}-i)-x(\lambda_{j}-j)}{x(-i)-x(-j)}.

(78)

On one hand, the sum in Theorem
3.19
is readily identified with a sum over lozenge tilings, where
\lambda_{i}^{(k)}-i
encode the positions of horizontal lozenges and the weights match (
69
) after a change of notations; by particle-hole involution it also gives rise to (
70
), as we explained in the proof of Theorem
3.14
. On the other hand, the proof
7
7

7

An alternative proof is hidden in
[
19
, Appendix]
. A possibility of our present proof can be hinted by
[
10
, Theorem 7.5.1]
.
of Theorem
3.19
can be obtained by combining the
q=t
version of Theorem
3.16
with known explicit evaluations of Koornwinder polynomials at principal specialization; we detail this in Appendix
A
.

Theorem
3.19
has an interesting corollary of representation-theoretic flavor:

Corollary 3.21
.

Take a signature
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lambda_{n}\geqslant 0)
. We have
8
8

8

\mathrm{Dim}_{SO(2n)}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
is the dimension of the irreducible representation of group
SO(2n)
spelled out in the same notation at
https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representations_of_classical_Lie_groups
or in
[
20
, Section 2.1]
.

\sum_{\emptyset={\bm{\lambda}}^{(0)}\prec{\bm{\lambda}}^{(1)}\prec\dots\prec{\bm{\lambda}}^{(n)}={\bm{\lambda}}}\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}+\frac{n+k}{2}-i}{\frac{n+k}{2}-i}\\
=\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{\lambda_{i}-i-(\lambda_{j}-j)}{j-i}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{2n+\lambda_{i}-i+\lambda_{j}-j}{2n-j-i}=\mathrm{Dim}_{SO(2n)}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}).

(79)

Take a signature
{\bm{\lambda}}=(\lambda_{1}\geqslant\lambda_{2}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{GT}_{n}
. We claim that for any
\mathfrak{s}
,

\sum_{\emptyset={\bm{\lambda}}^{(0)}\prec{\bm{\lambda}}^{(1)}\prec\dots\prec{\bm{\lambda}}^{(n)}={\bm{\lambda}}}\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\prod_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\mathfrak{s}+\lambda^{(k)}_{i}+\frac{k+1}{2}-i}{\mathfrak{s}+\frac{k+1}{2}-i}\\
=\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{\lambda_{i}-i-(\lambda_{j}-j)}{(j-i)}\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{2\mathfrak{s}+n+1+\lambda_{i}-i+\lambda_{j}-j}{2\mathfrak{s}+n+1-j-i}.

(80)

Indeed, notice that

x(a)-x(b)=\frac{1}{\sigma q^{a+\frac{n+1}{2}}}\left(\sigma q^{a+\frac{n+1}{2}}-\sigma q^{b+\frac{n+1}{2}}\right)\left(\sigma q^{a+\frac{n+1}{2}}-\frac{1}{\sigma q^{b+\frac{n+1}{2}}}\right).

Setting
\sigma=q^{\mathfrak{s}}
and sending
q\to 1
, (
78
) turns into (
80
). In particular, taking
\mathfrak{s}=\frac{n-1}{2}
in (
80
) and demanding that
\lambda_{n}\geqslant 0
, we get (
79
).
∎

Remark 3.22
.

Grigori Olshanski noticed that (
79
) and (
80
) can be deduced from the (multiplicity-free) branching rules for restrictions of irreducible representations of
SO(2n)
onto the subgroup
SO(2n-1)
, thus, avoiding the machinery of Koornwinder polynomials. The plan is as follows. We first notice that (
80
) for one particular value of
\mathfrak{s}
implies the same identity for all
\mathfrak{s}
: indeed, shifting by integer values of
\mathfrak{s}
we are getting the same statement for shifted
\lambda_{i}
; on the other hand, we are proving a polynomial identity, hence, its validity at infinitely many integer shifts of a single
\mathfrak{s}
implies the validity at all
\mathfrak{s}
.

Next, we are checking (
80
) by induction in
n
for a particular value of
\mathfrak{s}
. This check is reduced to the statement that the dimension of an irreducible representation of
SO(2n)
is the sum of dimensions of irreducible representations of
SO(2n-1)
into which it splits.

It would be interesting to produce a direct representation-theoretic proof of the identity (
79
).

4
Analysis of Dynamical Loop Equation

4.1
Statement of the asymptotic theorem

The aim of present Section
4
is to analyze the transition probability
\mathbb{P}({\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})=a_{\bm{e}}
of (
4
):

\displaystyle a_{\bm{e}}=\frac{1}{Z({\bm{x}})}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant n}\frac{b(x_{i}+\theta e_{i})-b(x_{j}+\theta e_{j})}{b(x_{i})-b(x_{j})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi^{+}(x_{i})^{e_{i}}\phi^{-}(x_{i})^{1-e_{i}},\quad{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n},

(81)

where
{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}
, as defined in (
2
); in particular,
x_{i}\geqslant x_{i+1}+\theta
for
i=1,2,\dots,n-1
. The prefactor
\frac{1}{Z({\bm{x}})}
is chosen so that
\sum_{{\bm{e}}}a_{\bm{e}}=1
. We encode the particle configurations
{\bm{x}}
,
{\bm{y}}
in (
81
) by their (smoothed) empirical densities:

\displaystyle\rho(s;{\bm{x}})=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{1}(\varepsilon x_{i}\leqslant s\leqslant\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta),\qquad\rho(s;{\bm{y}})=\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bm{1}(\varepsilon y_{i}\leqslant s\leqslant\varepsilon y_{i}+\varepsilon\theta).

(82)

We impose several conditions on the asymptotic behavior of various ingredients of (
81
) as
n\to\infty
:

Assumption 4.1
.

Fix constants
{\mathsf{N}}>0
and
\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}
.
We introduce a small parameter
\varepsilon={\mathsf{N}}/n\ll 1
, and assume the particle configuration
{\bm{x}}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})\in\mathbb{W}_{\theta}^{n}
satisfies
\mathbf{l}\leqslant\varepsilon x_{n}\leqslant\varepsilon x_{1}\leqslant\mathbf{r}-\varepsilon
. We further assume

1.

There exists an open complex neighborhood
\Lambda
of
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, such that for
z=\varepsilon\xi
,

\displaystyle b(\xi)={\mathsf{b}}(z)\quad\text{ and }\quad\phi^{\pm}(\xi)={\varphi}^{\pm}(z),\quad z\in\Lambda.

(83)

where
{\mathsf{b}}(z)
and
\varphi^{\pm}(z)
are holomorphic functions on
\Lambda
. In addition, we require
{\mathsf{b}}(z)
to be conformal (injective and biholomorphic) and
{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{z})=\overline{{\mathsf{b}}(z)}
.

2.

The functions
{\mathsf{b}}(z)
,
[\partial_{z}{\mathsf{b}}(z)]^{-1}
, and
\varphi^{\pm}(z)
are uniformly bounded, namely there exists a universal
9
9

9

By “universal” we mean not depending on
n
,
\varepsilon
, or
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
.
constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle|{\mathsf{b}}(z)|\leqslant C,\quad|\partial_{z}{\mathsf{b}}(z)|\geqslant 1/C,\ \quad|\varphi^{\pm}(z)|\leqslant C,\quad z\in\Lambda.

(84)

Remark 4.2
.

In the rest of this section, we view
{\mathsf{N}},\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r},C
in Assumption
4.1
as fixed constants. On the other hand, in our applications the functions
{\mathsf{b}},\varphi^{\pm}
might depend on
\varepsilon
.

Remark 4.3
.

Injectivity of
{\mathsf{b}}
implies that it is a (real) monotone function on
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
. Without loss of generality, throughout this section we silently assume that
{\mathsf{b}}(x)
is an increasing function of
x\in[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
.

Throughout this section we analyze (
81
) in two separate situations:

1.

Probabilistic case.
The weight functions
\varphi^{\pm}
in (
83
) are real analytic and positive on
(\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r})
:

\varphi^{\pm}(\bar{z})=\overline{\varphi^{\pm}(z)},\quad z\in\Lambda;\quad\text{ and }\quad\varphi^{\pm}(x)>0,\quad x\in(\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}).

(85)

In this situation (
81
) is a probability measure.

2.

Complex case.
The weight functions
\varphi^{\pm}
might be complex, but we additionally assume

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{e}}|a_{\bm{e}}|\leqslant C,

(86)

for a universal constant
C
.

While eventually we are only interested in the former case, the latter appears in the intermediate steps of our analysis: in the proofs we deform the weights
\phi^{\pm}
in (
81
) so that (
81
) becomes a complex-valued measure.

Our last assumption for the asymptotic analysis of (
81
) involves the following functions defined in terms of the empirical density (
82
) and the functions
\phi^{\pm}(z)
of Assumption
4.1
:

{\mathcal{B}}(z)={\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(z)+\varphi^{-}(z),\qquad\qquad{\mathcal{G}}(z)=\exp\left[\theta\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right].

(87)

Note that
{\mathcal{B}}(z)
is a holomorphic function for
z\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
.

Assumption 4.4
.

For any compact set
S\subset\bigl(\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]\bigr)
there exists a small universal constant
c=c(S)>0
such that for
z\in S
we have
c<|{\mathcal{B}}(z)|<c^{-1}
. Moreover, for any closed contour
\omega\subset\bigl(\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]\bigr)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{B}}(z)}{{\mathcal{B}}(z)}{\rm d}z=0,

(88)

which implies that there exists a well-defined single-valued branch of the function
\ln{\mathcal{B}}(z)
in
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
.

The last assumption plays the same role in our asymptotic analysis as the non-criticality condition in the analysis of the (discrete)
\beta
-ensembles, cf.
[
18
,
23
,
24
,
7
,
25
]
. Here is our central asymptotic result:

Theorem 4.5
.

Consider transition probability (
81
) with parameters satisfying Assumptions
4.1
and
4.4
for all small enough
\varepsilon
and in the probabilistic case (
85
). Then for any
z\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
we have as
\varepsilon\to 0
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}\theta}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi\mathbf{i}\theta}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)}(w,z)\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}\,+\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)\,+\,\OO\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right),

(89)

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
z
, and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)}(w,z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\varphi^{+}(w){\mathcal{G}}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\Biggl(\frac{\theta{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(w)}+\frac{\theta}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}^{\prime}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(u){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u){\rm d}u}{({\mathsf{b}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}(w))^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(w)({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))-({\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w))^{2}-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}\rho(s;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}s\Biggr)-\frac{\partial_{w}{\mathcal{B}}(w)}{2{\mathcal{B}}(w)},

where the contour
\omega^{\prime}_{-}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
w
.

Moreover,
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)
are mean
0
random variables such that
\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)\}_{z\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]}
are asymptotically Gaussian with covariance given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z_{1})}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\cdot\frac{\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z_{2})}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}\theta}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}(w)\varphi^{+}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z_{1})}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z_{1}))^{2}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z_{2})}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z_{2}))^{2}}{\rm d}w

\displaystyle+o(1),

(90)

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
z_{1},z_{2}
. The higher order joint moments of
\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)\}_{z\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]}
converge as
\varepsilon\to 0
to the Gaussian joint moments.

The implicit constants in the
\OO(\varepsilon^{2})
, in
o(1)
, and in higher order joint moments are uniform in all the involved parameters and particle configurations
{\bm{x}}
satisfying Assumption
4.1
and
4.4
, as long as the constants
C
and
c
of Assumptions
4.1
and
4.4
are fixed and
z
(or
z_{1},z_{2}
) belongs to a compact subset of
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
.

Remark 4.6
.

In the rest of this paper, when we write a quantity
{\mathcal{E}}(z;{\bm{x}})
is uniformly small, it refers to that for any compact subset
F\subset\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\sup_{\begin{smallmatrix}z\in F,\\
\text{${\bm{x}}$ satisfying Assumption \ref {a:asymp} and \ref {a:stable}}\end{smallmatrix}}{\mathcal{E}}(z;{\bm{x}})\rightarrow 0,

(91)

as long as the constants
C
and
c
of Assumptions
4.1
and
4.4
are fixed.

Remark 4.7
.

When we apply Theorem
4.5
in our study of random lozenge tilings, the exact expression for
{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)}(w,z)
is never used. However, our method of the proof of Theorem
4.5
provides this expression and, hence, we decided to include it for potential future applications.

Remark 4.8
.

Dividing (
4.5
) by
b^{\prime}(z)
and changing the variables
u=b(z)
, we can equivalently rewrite it as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{u-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}\theta}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-u)^{2}}\,

\displaystyle+\,\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi\mathbf{i}\theta}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)}(w,z)\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-u)^{2}}\,

\displaystyle+\Delta{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}(u)\,+\,\OO\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right),

(92)

with an appropriately covariance for the Gaussian process
{\mathcal{M}}^{\prime}(u)
. One advantage of the form (
4.8
) is that we can take
u
in it to be an arbitrary complex number outside
[{\mathsf{b}}(\mathbf{l}),{\mathsf{b}}(\mathbf{r})]
; see also Remarks
4.12
and
4.15
.

In general words, Theorem
4.5
shows that, conditional on
{\bm{x}}
, the empirical density of the particle configuration
{\bm{y}}={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}
(distributed with weights (
81
)) consists of two parts: a computable deterministic part and a random fluctuation, which is asymptotically a Gaussian field. However, instead of directly studying the difference of the empirical densities, we study the difference of their centered and modified Stieltjes transforms:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s.

(93)

We remark that (
93
) is different from the standard definition of Stieltjes transform. However, for any function
g(z)
which is analytic in a neighborhood of
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega}g(z)\left[\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{y}})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right]{\rm d}z=\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}g(s)\rho(s;{\bm{y}}){\rm d}s,

(94)

where
\omega
is a contour enclosing
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
. Hence, Theorem
4.5
can be used to deduce the asymptotic behavior of the pairings of
\rho(s;{\bm{y}})
with arbitrary analytic test functions.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
4.5
. For a reader familiar with the arguments in
[
18
]
, some parts of the proof might look similar, so let us emphasize two important differences. First, as a starting point in
[
18
]
a concentration bound on the empirical measure was used; this bound was produced by a separate method unrelated to the loop equations. In our present approach such a bound is no longer needed and we rely on the dynamical loop equation and nothing else. Second, an asymptotic expansion of the discrete loop equations in
[
18
]
was directly leading to the expectation of the centered Stieltjes transform, which could then be used to identify the first term in an analogue of (
4.5
). This is different for our dynamic loop equations: instead the expectation of exponentiated Stieltjes transform appears. There is no direct connection between
\mathbb{E}\xi
and
\mathbb{E}\exp(\xi)
for a random variable
\xi
, hence, this new feature leads to a significant difference in the argument.

4.2
Plan of the proof for Theorem
4.5

In this subsection we present the general scheme for the proof of Theorem
4.5
omitting technical details which are going to be reconstructed in the following subsections. We rely on the dynamical loop equation of Theorem
1.1
, which states that the expression

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{C}}(z):=\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi^{+}(z)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}+\varphi^{-}(z)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right]\end{split}

(95)

is a holomorphic function of
z\in\Lambda
.

Step 1.
We use (
95
) to analyze the following quantity (which directly appears in its second term):

{\mathcal{A}}(z):=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right].

(96)

We remark that under Assumption
4.1
,
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
is analytic on
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
.
This quantity encodes the information about the measure
\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}})
, which we want to understand. Our approach is to use holomorphicity of (
95
) to express
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
explicitly in terms of contour integrals of
{\mathcal{B}}(z)
defined in (
87
). For that we expand
{\mathcal{C}}(z)
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}(z)={\mathcal{A}}(z){\mathcal{B}}(z)+\OO(\varepsilon).

(97)

Note that in (
97
) we know explicitly
{\mathcal{B}}(z)
, and in addition we know that
{\mathcal{C}}(z)
is holomorphic (but we do not have any formulas for
{\mathcal{C}}(z)
). A priory, this does not give enough information to extract anything about
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
, but the situation changes when we impose Assumption
4.4
. More precisely, adopting Assumption
4.4
, we can decompose
{\mathcal{B}}(z)
into two parts

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}(z)=e^{h_{+}({\mathsf{b}}(z))}e^{-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(z))},

(98)

where
h_{+}({\mathsf{b}}(z))
is analytic in a neighborhood of
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, and
h_{-}(u)
is analytic in a neighborhood of
u=\infty
with
h_{-}(u)=\OO(1/u)
as
u
approaches
\infty
. (
98
) is a version of the Wiener-Hopf decomposition, adapted to our setting. In more detail, computing the difference of the integrals as the residue at
w=z
, we have

\displaystyle\ln{\mathcal{B}}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{+}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)},

(99)

where a positively oriented contour
\omega_{+}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
and
z
; a positively oriented contour
\omega_{-}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
but not
z
. We can rewrite (
99
) as an expansion (
98
) with

h_{+}(u):=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{+}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-u},\qquad h_{-}(u):=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-u}.

(100)

Note that the particular choices of
\omega_{\pm}
are not important in (
99
), as long as
z
is inside
\omega_{+}
and outside
\omega_{-}
, while
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
is inside both contours. However, in order for the definition (
100
) to make sense, we need to fix these contours:
\omega_{-}
should be a tight loop around
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, while
\omega_{+}
should be a larger contour, enclosing
\omega_{-}
and staying inside
\Lambda
, cf. Figure
7
. We treat (
100
) as the definition of holomorphic
h_{+}(u)
for
u
inside
b^{-1}(\omega_{+})
and as the definition of holomorphic
h_{-}(u)
for
u
outside
b^{-1}(\omega_{-})
. The identity (
98
) is valid for
z
in the annulus enclosed by
\omega_{\pm}
.

Fig 7:
Contours
\omega_{\pm}
and
z
in the annulus between them.

To get the first order asymptotics of
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
we multiply both sides of (
97
) by
e^{-h_{+}({\mathsf{b}}(z))}
, and notice that the left-hand side remains analytic in a complex neighborhood of
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
. Then we can recover
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
through a contour integral: using the above contours
\omega_{\pm}
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{{\mathcal{C}}(w)e^{-h_{+}({\mathsf{b}}(w))}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{({\mathcal{A}}(w)e^{-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(w))}+\OO(\varepsilon)){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}\\
&=-{\mathcal{A}}(z)e^{-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(z))}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{+}}\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(w)e^{-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(w))}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}+\OO(\varepsilon).\end{split}

(101)

We claim that the last contour integral over
\omega_{+}
is equal to
1
. Indeed, to see that we change the variables
u=b(w)
, resulting in

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{b(\omega_{+})}\frac{e^{-h_{-}(u)}}{u-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{u-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{u-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right]\,{\rm d}u.

The last integrand has no singularities outside
b(\omega_{+})
and, therefore, the integral is equal to the residue at
u=\infty
. This residue is
1
, because
h_{-}(u)=1+\OO(\tfrac{1}{u})
and the expression under expectation is
1+\OO(\tfrac{1}{u})
as
u\to\infty
. Hence, (
101
) gives the first order asymptotics of
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
:

\displaystyle\ln{\mathcal{A}}(z)=h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(z))+\OO(\varepsilon).

(102)

The formula (
102
) is developed in further detail in Proposition
4.11
. With additional efforts (but using similar ideas) the higher order asymptotics of
\ln{\mathcal{A}}(z)
can be obtained by further expanding the
\OO(\varepsilon)
error term in (
97
); we do this for the first two orders in Proposition
4.16
.

Step 2.
The next step is to extract the probabilistic information — the first two (deterministic) terms in the right-hand side of (
4.5
) — from the asymptotic expansion of
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
. Observe that
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
can be transformed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\varepsilon\theta\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{e_{i}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(\varepsilon x_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}+\oo(1)\right)\right].

(103)

On the other hand, in order to get the deterministic terms in the right-hand side of (
4.5
), we need to analyze

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon e_{i})}-\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right)+\oo(1)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[-\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{e_{i}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(\varepsilon x_{i}){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}))^{2}}+\oo(1)\right].

(104)

There are two differences between the expectation in (
103
) which we already know and the expectation in (
104
) which we need to find. First, the sum over
i
in the latter is (up to constant factors) the
z
–derivative of the sum over
i
in the former. Second, the sum is being exponentiated in (
103
). Generally speaking, there is no direct connection between
\mathbb{E}\xi
and
\mathbb{E}\exp(\xi)
for a random variable
\xi
, which makes the second difference a non-trivial difficulty.

We overcome this technical obstacle in Proposition
4.16
. For that we notice that the logarithmic derivative
\partial_{z}\ln{\mathcal{A}}(z)
can be interpreted as the expectation of the form (
104
), but with respect to a deformed measure. Further, we offset the deformation by inserting certain explicit additional complex factors in
\phi^{\pm}
in (
81
). Because the analysis of Step 1 did not rely on positivity of the weights, it continues to hold for the resulting new complex measure, eventually leading to the evaluation of the first two terms of (
4.5
).

Step 3.
The last step is to prove asymptotic Gaussianity of the random variable
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)
in (
4.5
) and compute its covariance. This is done in Proposition
4.18
. In more detail, we notice that the computation of the Laplace transform of
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)
is equivalent to the computation of the deterministic terms in (
4.5
) for a slightly deformed measure. The developments of Steps 1 and 2 continue to be valid for this measure. Hence, we find the leading asymptotic behavior of the Laplace transforms of
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(z)
, which matches the Laplace transforms of Gaussian random variables with covariance given by (
90
).

The rest of this section adds necessary details to the three steps argument sketched above.

4.3
Properties of
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
and
{\mathcal{C}}(z)

We start our detailed proof of Theorem
4.5
by collecting estimates and asymptotic expansions for
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
and
{\mathcal{C}}(z)
from (
96
) and (
95
), respectively. We start from
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
.

Proposition 4.9
.

We work under Assumption
4.1
, either in probabilistic case with (
85
) or in the complex case with (
86
). Fix any compact subset
S\subset\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
. Then

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{A}}(z)|\leqslant C,\qquad z\in S,

(105)

and

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\partial_{z}{\mathcal{A}}(z)=\mathbb{E}\Biggl[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}

\displaystyle\times\left(\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s-\frac{\theta}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}{\rm d}s+\OO(\varepsilon^{2})\right)\Biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}\cdot\left(\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}})){\rm d}s}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}+\OO(\varepsilon)\right)\right],

(106)

where the constant
C
and implicit constants in
\OO(\cdot)
depend only on
S
.

Whenever
\dist(z,[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}])>\delta
, we have
{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})\geqslant\delta/C
from (
84
). Hence,

\displaystyle\left|\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right|\leqslant\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\frac{|{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})|}{\delta/C}\right)\leqslant\left(1+\frac{\OO(\varepsilon)}{\delta/C}\right)^{n}\leqslant C_{\delta},

(107)

where we used
n=\OO(\varepsilon^{-1})
. Then (
105
) follows from (
107
) and (
86
).

The derivative of
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{z}{\mathcal{A}}(z)&=\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right]=\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right)^{e_{i}}\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right)^{e_{i}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta)}-\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right)\right].\end{split}

(108)

We use the following identity

\displaystyle f(x+\varepsilon\theta)-f(x)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{x}^{x+\varepsilon\theta}(f(y+\varepsilon)-f(y)){\rm d}y-\frac{1}{2}\int_{x}^{x+\varepsilon\theta}(f^{\prime}(y+\varepsilon)-f^{\prime}(y)){\rm d}y+\OO(\varepsilon^{3}),

(109)

where the implicit constant in
\OO(\varepsilon^{3})
is uniform over thrice differentiable functions
f(x)
satisfying a uniform bound on the third derivative of the form
\sup_{y\in[x,x+2\varepsilon]}|f^{(3)}(y)|\leqslant C
.
Using (
109
) with
f(x)={\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)/({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(x))
, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta)}-\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right)\\
&=\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s-\frac{\theta}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}{\rm d}s+\OO(\varepsilon^{2})\\
&=\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s+\OO(\varepsilon),\end{split}

(110)

where we bound the middle term in the second line by
\OO(\varepsilon)
.
∎

Our next task is to rewrite
{\mathcal{C}}(z)
in (
95
) as a product of
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
and
{\mathcal{B}}(z)
with a small error; in other words, we would like to give a rigorous justification to (
97
).

Proposition 4.10
.

Under Assumption
4.1
(either in probabilistic case with (
85
) or in the complex case with (
86
)),
{\mathcal{C}}(z)
satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}(z)={\mathcal{A}}(z){\mathcal{B}}(z)\left(1+\varepsilon{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(z)\right),\qquad z\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}],

(111)

where
{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(z)
is given by (here
{\mathcal{G}}(z)=\exp\left[\theta\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right]
, as in (
87
)):

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(z)=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{A}}(z){\mathcal{B}}(z)}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\left(\varphi^{+}(z){\mathcal{G}}(z){\mathcal{E}}^{(0)}(z)+\OO(\varepsilon)\right)\right],\end{split}

(112)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{E}}^{(0)}(z)&=\frac{\theta^{2}}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\\
&+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(z)({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))-({\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z))^{2}-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}\rho(s;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}s.\end{split}

(113)

If we assume that
z
varies in a compact subset of
\Lambda\subset[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, then the implicit constant in the
\OO(\varepsilon)
is uniform in
z
and
|{\mathcal{E}}^{(0)}(z)|
,
|{\mathcal{A}}(z){\mathcal{B}}(z){\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(z)|
are uniformly bounded.

We rewrite
{\mathcal{C}}(z)
as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-b(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\left(\varphi^{+}(z)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}+\varphi^{-}(z)\right)\right].

(114)

We would like to approximate the last product with
{\mathcal{B}}(z)
. For that we Taylor expand:

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(1+\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\left(1+\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log\left(1+\frac{\varepsilon\theta{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)+\tfrac{1}{2}(\varepsilon\theta)^{2}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(z)+\OO(\varepsilon^{3})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\varepsilon\theta{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\varepsilon\theta)^{2}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\varepsilon\theta{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z))^{2}}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}))^{2}}+\OO(\varepsilon^{3})\right)\right].

(115)

For any
f
with uniformly bounded second derivative, we have the following identity

\displaystyle f(x+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})=\int_{x}^{x+\varepsilon\theta}\left(\frac{f(y)}{\varepsilon\theta}-\frac{f^{\prime}(y)}{2}\right){\rm d}y+\frac{e_{i}}{\varepsilon}\int_{x}^{x+\varepsilon\theta}\bigl(f(y+\varepsilon)-f(y)\bigr){\rm d}y+\OO(\varepsilon^{2}).

(116)

Using (
116
) with
f(x)={\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)/({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(x))
, we rewrite the first term in the right-hand side of (
115
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\varepsilon\theta{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}&=\theta\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}-\frac{\varepsilon\theta^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}{\rm d}s\\
&+\theta^{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s+\OO(\varepsilon^{2}).\end{split}

(117)

We also transform the last two terms in the right-hand side of (
115
) into integrals of
\rho(s;{\bm{x}})
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\frac{(\varepsilon\theta)^{2}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}-\frac{(\varepsilon\theta{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z))^{2}}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}))^{2}}\right]\\
&=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\left(\frac{\theta^{2}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}-\frac{(\theta{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z))^{2}}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}\right)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}s+\OO(\varepsilon^{2}).\end{split}

(118)

By plugging (
117
), (
118
) into (
115
), we get

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}={\mathcal{G}}(z)\exp\left[\varepsilon{\mathcal{E}}^{(0)}(z)+\OO(\varepsilon^{2})\right],

(119)

where
{\mathcal{G}}(z)=\exp\left[\theta\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right]
, corresponding to the first term on the right-hand side of (
117
), and
{\mathcal{E}}^{(0)}(z)
is

\displaystyle\theta^{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{\varepsilon({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))}{\rm d}s+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(z)({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))-({\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z))^{2}-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}\rho(s;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}s.

By plugging (
119
) into (
114
), we transform
{\mathcal{C}}(z)
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{C}}(z)&=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-b(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\left(\varphi^{+}(z)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}+\varphi^{-}(z)\right)\right].\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-b(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\left(\varphi^{+}(z){\mathcal{G}}(z)e^{\varepsilon{\mathcal{E}}^{(0)}(z)}+\varphi^{-}(z)+\OO(\varepsilon^{2})\right)\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-b(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\left({\mathcal{B}}(z)+\varepsilon\varphi^{+}(z){\mathcal{G}}(z){\mathcal{E}}^{(0)}(z)+\OO(\varepsilon^{2})\right)\right],\end{split}

which leads to (
112
). The uniformity bounds for
\OO(\varepsilon^{2})
,
|{\mathcal{E}}^{(0)}(z)|
, and
|{\mathcal{A}}(z){\mathcal{B}}(z){\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(z)|
routinely follow from the definitions and we omit their justifications.
∎

4.4
Asymptotic expansion of
{\mathcal{A}}(z)

In this section we derive the second order asymptotic expansion of the quantity
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
as defined in (
96
) in terms of contour integrals of
{\mathcal{B}}(z)
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Under Assumptions
4.1
and
4.4
(either in probabilistic case with (
85
) or in the complex case with (
86
)), there is a branch of
\ln{\mathcal{A}}(z)
such that

\displaystyle\ln{\mathcal{A}}(z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{\rm d}w+\OO\left(\varepsilon\right),\qquad z\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}],

(120)

where
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda
is an arbitrary positively oriented simple contour enclosing
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
z
. The error term
\OO(\varepsilon)
can be chosen to be uniform over
z
in a compact subset of
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
.

We start by fixing two positively oriented contours
\omega_{\pm}\subset\Lambda
, such that they both enclose
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, are inside
\Lambda
, and
\omega_{-}
is inside
\omega_{+}
. Throughout the proof, we deal with
z
inside the annulus bounded by these contours, as in Figure
7
. Thanks to Assumption
4.4
,
\ln{\mathcal{B}}(z)
is well defined on
\Lambda
, and we can introduce two functions:

h_{+}(u):=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{+}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-u}{\rm d}w,\qquad\qquad h_{-}(u):=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-u}{\rm d}w.

(121)

Computing the integral as a residue, we have

\displaystyle\ln{\mathcal{B}}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{+}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{\rm d}w-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{\rm d}w=h_{+}({\mathsf{b}}(z))-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(z)).

(122)

From the construction,
h_{+}({\mathsf{b}}(z))
is holomorphic for
z
inside the contour
\omega_{+}
and
h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(z))
is holomorphic for
z
outside the contour
\omega_{-}
.

Next, we claim that when
z
varies in the annulus enclosed by
\omega_{\pm}
, the functions
h_{+}({\mathsf{b}}(z))
and
h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(z))
are uniformly bounded by a constant
C
which depends only on
\omega_{\pm}
. Indeed, to prove the claim it is sufficient to notice that
\omega_{-}
can be made slightly smaller and
\omega_{+}
can be made slightly larger without changing the values of the integrals in (
121
). Once we do this change, the integrand is uniformly bounded and so is the integral.

The above boundeness allows us to multiply both sides of (
111
) by
\exp(-h_{+}({\mathsf{b}}(z)))
and use the decomposition (
122
) to get

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}(z)e^{-h_{+}({\mathsf{b}}(z))}={\mathcal{A}}(z)e^{-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(z))}+\OO(\varepsilon).

(123)

Since the left-hand side of (
123
) is holomorphic inside
\omega_{+}
, we can use a contour integral to get rid of it. Integrating (
123
) over
\omega_{-}
, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{{\mathcal{C}}(w)e^{-h_{+}({\mathsf{b}}(w))}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{\rm d}w=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{({\mathcal{A}}(w)e^{-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(w))}+\OO(\varepsilon)){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{\rm d}w\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(w)e^{-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(w))}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{\rm d}w+\OO(\varepsilon).\end{split}

(124)

We recall the expression of
{\mathcal{A}}(w)
from (
96
) and notice that it is a meromorphic function of
{\mathsf{b}}(w)
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}(w)={\mathbf{A}}({\mathsf{b}}(w)),\qquad{\mathbf{A}}(u)=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{u-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{u-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right].

(125)

In particular
{\mathbf{A}}(u)
is analytic outside the contour
{\mathsf{b}}(\omega_{-})
. Deforming the integration contour to
\omega_{+}
, collecting the residue at
w=z
, and then changing the variables
u=b(w)
, we transform (
124
) into

\OO(\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega_{-}}\frac{{\mathcal{A}}(w)e^{-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(w))}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{\rm d}w=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\mathsf{b}}(\omega_{+})}\frac{{\mathbf{A}}(u)e^{-h_{-}(u)}}{u-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{\rm d}u-{\mathcal{A}}(z)e^{-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(z))}.

(126)

The
u
–integrand in the right-hand side of (
126
) does not have singularities outside the integration contour and, therefore, the integral evaluates as a residue at
u=\infty
. This residue is
1
, because
{\mathbf{A}}(u)=1+\oo(1)
and
h_{-}(u)\to 0
as
u\to\infty
directly from the definitions of these functions. Thus, (
126
) simplifies to

{\mathcal{A}}(z)e^{-h_{-}({\mathsf{b}}(z))}=1+\OO(\varepsilon).\qed

Remark 4.12
.

We can rewrite the result of Proposition
4.11
in the form

{\mathbf{A}}(u)=e^{h_{-}(u)}+\OO(\varepsilon).

(127)

So far we proved this statement for
u\in{\mathsf{b}}(\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}])
, however, it can readily be extended to all
u
outside
[{\mathsf{b}}(\mathbf{l}),{\mathsf{b}}(\mathbf{r})]
. Indeed, to see that we notice that both sides of (
127
) are holomorphic outside
{\mathsf{b}}(\omega_{-})
, use Cauchy integral formula to rewrite them as contour integrals over
{\mathsf{b}}(\omega_{-})
, and send
\varepsilon\to 0
in this integral form. In particular, (
127
) implies that
\ln{\mathbf{A}}(u)
is well-defined outside
[{\mathsf{b}}(\mathbf{l}),{\mathsf{b}}(\mathbf{r})]
.

We proceed to the second order asymptotic expansion of
{\mathcal{A}}(z)
.

Proposition 4.13
.

Under Assumptions
4.1
and
4.4
(either in probabilistic case with (
85
) or in the complex case with (
86
)), we have

\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{A}}(z)}{{\mathcal{A}}(z)}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right),

(128)

for
z\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
; where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
z
.
{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)
is as in (
112
) and has asymptotic behavior

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)&=\frac{\varphi^{+}(w){\mathcal{G}}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\left(\frac{\theta}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}_{-}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(u){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u)}{({\mathsf{b}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}(w))^{2}}{\rm d}u\right.\\
&\left.+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\int\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(z)({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))-({\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z))^{2}-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}\rho(s;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}s\right)+\OO(\varepsilon),\end{split}

(129)

where the contour
\omega^{\prime}_{-}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
w
. If we assume that
z
belongs to a compact subset of
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
and fix the contours
\omega_{-}
,
\omega^{\prime}_{-}
, then the implicit constants in
\OO(\cdot)
errors can be chosen uniformly.

We recall the result of Proposition
4.10
and its logarithmic derivative:

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{C}}(z)&={\mathcal{A}}(z){\mathcal{B}}(z)\left(1+\varepsilon{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(z)\right),\\
\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{C}}(z)}{{\mathcal{C}}(z)}&=\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{A}}(z)}{{\mathcal{A}}(z)}+\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{B}}(z)}{{\mathcal{B}}(z)}+\partial_{z}\ln\bigl(1+\varepsilon{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(z)\bigr).\end{split}

(130)

Thanks to Assumption
4.4
, Proposition
4.10
, and Proposition
4.11
, whenever
z
belongs to a compact subset of
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, we have

c\leqslant|{\mathcal{A}}(z)|\leqslant 1/c,\qquad c\leqslant|{\mathcal{B}}(z)|\leqslant 1/c,\qquad c\leqslant|{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(z)|\leqslant 1/c,

for a constant
c
depending on the choice of this compact set, but not on
z
. Hence, all terms in the right-hand side of (
130
) are holomorphic for
z\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
.

Claim 4.14
.

If
\varepsilon
is small enough, then
\partial_{z}{\mathcal{C}}(z)/{\mathcal{C}}(z)
is holomorphic for all
z\in\Lambda
(including
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
).

To prove the claim, we note that
{\mathcal{C}}(z)
is holomorphic by Theorem
1.1
, hence, we only need to show that it has no zeros. Outside
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
there are no zeros by (
130
) and we only focus on a neighborhood of
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
. By the Cauchy’s argument principle, the total number of zeros is
{\mathcal{C}}(z)
in a neighborhood of
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
can be computed as

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{C}}(z)}{{\mathcal{C}}(z)}{\rm d}z,

(131)

where
\omega
is a contour enclosing this neighborhood. Hence, we need to prove that (
131
) vanishes. Using (
130
), Assumption
4.4
, and definition (
125
) we transform (
131
) as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega}\left(\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{A}}(z)}{{\mathcal{A}}(z)}+\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{B}}(z)}{{\mathcal{B}}(z)}+\partial_{z}\ln(1+\varepsilon{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(z))\right){\rm d}z

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{A}}(z)}{{\mathcal{A}}(z)}{\rm d}z+\OO(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\mathsf{b}}(\omega)}\frac{\partial_{u}{\mathbf{A}}(u)}{\mathbf{A}(u)}{\rm d}u+\OO(\varepsilon).

(132)

Remark
4.12
guarantees that
\partial_{u}{\mathbf{A}}(u)/\mathbf{A}(u)=\partial_{u}\ln\mathbf{A}(u)
is holomorphic outside
\omega
; therefore, we can compute the contour integral in the right-hand side of (
132
) as the residue at the infinity. Since
{\mathbf{A}}(u)=1+\frac{c}{u}+\dots
as
u\to\infty
, we have
\partial_{u}\ln{\mathbf{A}}(u)\sim-c/u^{2}
and the residue vanishes. We conclude that (
132
) is
\OO(\varepsilon)
and, hence, so is (
131
). Because this number is an integer counting the number of zeros, it has to vanish for small
\varepsilon
. The claim is proven.

Because of the claim, we can use a contour integral to get rid of
\partial_{z}{\mathcal{C}}(z)/{\mathcal{C}}(z)
and recover
\partial_{z}{\mathcal{A}}(z)/{\mathcal{A}}(z)
. We take a contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda
enclosing
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
and
z
outside this contour. By performing the same contour integral on both sides of (
130
), using Assumption
4.4
and the claim we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\partial_{w}{\mathcal{A}}(w)}{{\mathcal{A}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\left(\frac{\partial_{w}{\mathcal{B}}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}+\partial_{w}\ln(1+\varepsilon{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w))-\frac{\partial_{w}{\mathcal{C}}(w)}{{\mathcal{C}}(w)}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln(1+\varepsilon{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}+\OO\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right).\end{split}

(133)

Here, in the second line, we used that
\partial_{w}{\mathcal{C}}(w)/{\mathcal{C}}(w)
is holomorphic inside the contour
{\omega_{-}}
, thus, the corresponding contour integrals vanish. Let us transform the left-hand side of (
133
). For that we change the variable
u={\mathsf{b}}(w)
and use
{\mathbf{A}}(u)={\mathcal{A}}(w)
from (
125
) to get:

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\partial_{w}{\mathcal{A}}(w)}{{\mathcal{A}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}&=\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{A}}(z)}{{\mathcal{A}}(z)}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{+}}}\frac{\partial_{w}{\mathcal{A}}(w)}{{\mathcal{A}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}\\
&=\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{A}}(z)}{{\mathcal{A}}(z)}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\mathsf{b}}({\omega_{+}})}\frac{\partial_{u}{\mathbf{A}}(u)}{{\mathbf{A}}(u)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}u}{u-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}=\frac{\partial_{z}{\mathcal{A}}(z)}{{\mathcal{A}}(z)},\end{split}

(134)

where the contour
{\omega_{+}}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
and
z
; for the last inequality, we used the same computation of the integral as the residue at
u=\infty
, as for the integral in the right-hand side of (
132
).

The combination of (
133
) with (
134
) gives (
128
) and it remains to prove the asymptotic expansion (
129
) for
{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)
. By plugging (
113
) into (
112
), we have the following expression for
{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)
with
w\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)=\frac{\theta\varphi^{+}(w){\mathcal{G}}(w)}{{\mathcal{A}}(w){\mathcal{B}}(w)}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\left(\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right)\right]\\
+\frac{\varphi^{+}(w){\mathcal{G}}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(z)({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))-({\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z))^{2}-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}\rho(s;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}s+\OO(\varepsilon),\end{split}

(135)

where the uniform control over
\OO(\varepsilon)
term relies on
|{\mathcal{A}}(w)|\geqslant c
and
|{\mathcal{B}}(w)|\geqslant c
from Proposition
4.11
and Assumption
4.4
, respectively. The second term in the right-hand side of (
135
) matches the second line in (
129
) and it remains to study the first term on the righthand side of (
135
) as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
It follows from combining (
106
) with upper bound (
105
) that

\displaystyle(\partial_{w}\ln{\mathcal{A}}(w)){\mathcal{A}}(w)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}\left(\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right)\right]+\OO(\varepsilon).

(136)

On the other hand, differentiating (
120
) (we can differentiate the asymptotic expansion, because we deal with holomorphic functions and can represent derivative through the Cauchy integral formula), we get

\partial_{w}\ln{\mathcal{A}}(w)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}_{-}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(u){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{({\mathsf{b}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}(w))^{2}}{\rm d}u+\OO(\varepsilon),

(137)

where the contour
\omega^{\prime}_{-}\in\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
w
. Combining (
136
) with (
137
) gives the desired expression for the first term in the right-hand side of (
135
), which matches the first line in (
129
).
∎

Remark 4.15
.

Similarly to Remark
4.12
, we can rewrite the statement of Proposition
4.13
in terms of the function
{\mathbf{A}}(u)
of (
125
):

\frac{\partial_{u}{\mathbf{A}}(u)}{{\mathbf{A}}(u)}=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-u)^{2}}{\rm d}w+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-u)^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right).

(138)

The advantage of (
138
) is that
u
can be taken in it to be an arbitrary point in
\mathbb{C}\setminus[{\mathsf{b}}(\mathbf{l}),{\mathsf{b}}(\mathbf{r})]
, as long as
{\omega_{-}}
is chosen so that
u
is outside
{\mathsf{b}}(\omega_{-})
. The proof of this extension is the same as in Remark
4.12
.

4.5
Mean Estimate

In this subsection we compute the first two terms in the right-hand side of (
4.5
) in Theorem
4.5
.

Proposition 4.16
.

Under Assumptions
4.1
,
4.4
in the probabilistic case (
85
) we have for
z\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w

\displaystyle+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)}(w,z)\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right),

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
