, but not
z
, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)}(w,z)&=\frac{\varphi^{+}(w){\mathcal{G}}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\left(\frac{\theta}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}_{-}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(u){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u)}{({\mathsf{b}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}(w))^{2}}{\rm d}u+\frac{\theta{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(w)}\right.\\
&\quad\left.+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(z)({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))-({\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z))^{2}-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}\rho(s;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}s\right)-\frac{\partial_{w}{\mathcal{B}}(w)}{2{\mathcal{B}}(w)},\end{split}

(139)

the contour
\omega^{\prime}_{-}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
w
. If we assume that
z
belongs to a compact subset of
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, then the error
\OO(\varepsilon^{2})
is uniformly (in
z
) small.

We fix an arbitrarily small
\eta>0
, take
v
with
\dist(v,[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}])>2\eta
, and consider a deformation of the transition probability
\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})=a_{\bm{e}}
of (
81
):

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{\bm{e}}:=\frac{a_{\bm{e}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta)}\right)^{e_{i}}}{\sum\limits_{{\bm{e}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}a_{{\bm{e}}^{\prime}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta)}\right)^{e^{\prime}_{i}}}.

(140)

Transition probability
\tilde{a}_{\bm{e}}
is again in the form (
81
) but with modified function
\varphi^{+}(z)
in (
83
):

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}^{+}(z)=\varphi^{+}(z)\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)}.

(141)

Claim 4.17
.

The transition probabilities
\tilde{a}_{\bm{e}}
satisfy Assumptions
4.1
and
4.4
in the complex case (
86
) with
\Lambda
replaced by
\Lambda\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\dist(z,[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}])<\eta\}
.

We postpone proving Claim
4.17
until after we finish the proof of Proposition
4.16
. We let
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
denote expectation for the transition probability
\tilde{a}_{\bm{e}}
and define the quantities
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(z)
and
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(z)
through

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(z)&=\sum_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\tilde{a}_{\bm{e}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}=\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right],\\
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(z)&={\mathcal{G}}(z)\tilde{\varphi}^{+}(z)+\varphi^{-}(z),\qquad\qquad{\mathcal{G}}(z)=\exp\left[\theta\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right].\end{split}

(142)

We can rewrite
\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(z)
in terms of
\ln{\mathcal{B}}(z)
from (
87
) with a small error:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(z)&=\ln\left({\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(z)\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)}+\varphi^{-}(z)\right)\\
&=\ln{\mathcal{B}}(z)+\ln\left(1+\frac{{\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(z)}{{\mathcal{B}}(z)}\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)}\right)\\
&=\ln{\mathcal{B}}(z)+\frac{\varepsilon\theta{\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(z)}{{\mathcal{B}}(z)}\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}+\OO(\varepsilon^{2}).\end{split}

(143)

Applying Proposition
4.13
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right),

(144)

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\dist(z,[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}])<\eta\}
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
v
or
z
. Here

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)&=\frac{\tilde{\varphi}^{+}(w){\mathcal{G}}(w)}{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\left(\frac{\theta}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}_{-}}\frac{\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(u){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u)}{({\mathsf{b}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}(w))^{2}}{\rm d}u\right.\\
&\left.+\frac{\theta^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}(z)({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))-({\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z))^{2}-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}\rho(s;{\bm{x}}){\rm d}s\right)+\OO(\varepsilon),\end{split}

(145)

where the contour
\omega^{\prime}_{-}\subset\Lambda\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\dist(z,[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}])<\eta\}
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
w
.
Using (
143
), we rewrite

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(z)&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w\\
&+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\left({\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)+\frac{\theta{\mathcal{G}}(w)\varphi^{+}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(w)}\right)\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right),\end{split}

(146)

where
{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)
, as in (
129
), is obtained from
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)
by replacing
\tilde{\varphi}^{+}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
with
\varphi^{+}
and
{\mathcal{B}}
, respectively.

Note that we used Proposition
4.13
under Claim
4.17
to prove (
146
). Hence,
z
in (
146
) should belong to the set
\Lambda\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\dist(z,[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}])<\eta\}
. However, we can extend to all
z
in
\Lambda
outside
\omega_{-}
by using Remark
4.15
; this is important for us, because we later would like to set
z=v
.

On the other hand, using (
106
) we have

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{A}}(z)=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(x)}{\rm d}s-\frac{\theta}{2}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}{\rm d}s+\OO(\varepsilon^{2})\right],

(147)

where we emphasize that the right-hand side involves the expectation
\mathbb{E}
with respect to the original un-deformed measure.

By comparing the leading terms in (
146
) and (
147
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO\left(\varepsilon\right),

(148)

where the contour
\omega_{-}
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
z
.
Introducing another contour
\omega_{-}^{\prime}\subset\Lambda
, which contains
\omega_{-}
, but not
z
, and using (
148
), we can rewrite the second term on the righthand side of (
147
) as a contour integral

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{\theta}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(s)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{({\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s))^{2}}{\rm d}s\biggr]\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}_{-}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u)}{({\mathsf{b}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\theta}{2}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right]{\rm d}u\\
&=\frac{\varepsilon}{4\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}_{-}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u)}{({\mathsf{b}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(u))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO(\varepsilon)\right){\rm d}u.\end{split}

(149)

We first integrate in
u
by using an identity valid for any holomorphic function
f(u)
:

\oint\limits_{\text{around }w}\frac{f(u){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(u))^{2}}{\rm d}u=\oint\limits_{\text{around }{\mathsf{b}}(w)}\frac{f({\mathsf{b}}^{-1}(a))}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-a)^{2}}{\rm d}a=2\pi\mathbf{i}\partial_{a}\Bigl[f({\mathsf{b}}^{-1}(a))\Bigr]_{a={\mathsf{b}}(w)}=2\pi\mathbf{i}\frac{f^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}.

Thus, taking
f(u)={\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u)/({\mathsf{b}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}
in the above identity, we simplify (
149
) to

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{4\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\partial_{u}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(u)}{({\mathsf{b}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}\right]_{u=w}\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\rm d}w=-\frac{\varepsilon}{4\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}\frac{\partial_{w}{\mathcal{B}}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}{\rm d}w+\OO(\varepsilon^{2}),

(150)

where we integrated by parts in the last identity. Plugging (
149
), (
150
) into (
147
), and rearranging, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\int\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{y}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}})){\rm d}s}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}\right]&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}\\
&+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)}(w,z)\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}+\OO\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right),\end{split}

where, with
{\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)
as in (
129
), we have:

{\mathcal{E}}^{(2)}(w,z):={\mathcal{E}}^{(1)}(w)+\frac{\theta{\mathcal{G}}(w)\varphi^{+}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(w)}-\frac{\partial_{w}{\mathcal{B}}(w)}{2{\mathcal{B}}(w)}.\qed

In addition to
\tilde{a}_{\bm{e}}
we need another deformation
\hat{a}_{\bm{e}}
of the transition probability:

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\bm{e}}:=\frac{a_{\bm{e}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta)}\right)^{e_{i}}}{\sum\limits_{{\bm{e}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}a_{{\bm{e}}^{\prime}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta)}\right)^{e^{\prime}_{i}}}.

(151)

This transition probability is again of the form (
81
) with modified function
\varphi^{+}(z)
in (
83
):

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}^{+}(z)=\varphi^{+}(z)\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)}\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})-{\mathsf{b}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})-{\mathsf{b}}(z+\varepsilon\theta)}.

(152)

Similarly to (
142
), we define the quantity
\hat{\mathcal{A}}(z)
and
\hat{\mathcal{B}}(z)
for the measure
\hat{a}_{\bm{e}}
from (
140
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\mathcal{A}}(z)&=\sum_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\hat{a}_{\bm{e}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}=\hat{\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta e_{i})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right],\\
\hat{\mathcal{B}}(z)&={\mathcal{G}}(z)\hat{\varphi}^{+}(z)+\varphi^{-}(z),\qquad\qquad{\mathcal{G}}(z)=\exp\left[\theta\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right].\end{split}

(153)

By our construction,
\hat{a}_{\bm{e}}
is a bona-fide real positive measure, and we are in the probabilistic case (
85
). Our choice
\dist(v,[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}])>{2\eta}
, implies that Assumption
4.1
holds for
\hat{a}_{\bm{e}}
with
\Lambda
replaced by
\Lambda\cap\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\dist(z,[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}])<\eta\}
. Moreover, similarly to (
143
), we have

\displaystyle\ln\hat{\mathcal{B}}(z)=\ln{\mathcal{B}}(z)+\OO(\varepsilon).

It follows that
\ln\hat{\mathcal{B}}(z)
is well defined and Assumption
4.4
holds for
\hat{\mathcal{B}}(z)
and small enough
\varepsilon
.

We can check that
\tilde{a}_{\bm{e}}
satisfies Assumptions
4.1
and
4.4
in exactly the same way as we just did for
\hat{a}_{\bm{e}}
. The non-trivial step is to verify the bound (
86
): the difficulty is that the weights are complex and, therefore, we need to check that the denominator in (
140
) is bounded away from
0
. For that we use Proposition
4.11
for the transition probability
\hat{a}_{\bm{e}}
, which gives that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln\hat{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\rm d}w}{{\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})}+\OO(\varepsilon)&=\ln\hat{\mathcal{A}}(\bar{v})=\ln\left[\sum_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\hat{a}_{\bm{e}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta)}{{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i})}\right)^{e_{i}}\right]\\
&=\ln\left[\frac{\sum\limits_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}a_{\bm{e}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta)}\right)^{e_{i}}}{\sum\limits_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}a_{\bm{e}}\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}(\bar{v})-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta)}\right)^{e_{i}}}\right].\end{split}

(154)

In particular, for small
\varepsilon
the expression on the right-hand side of (
154
) is a bounded number. For the denominator in the last term, since
|{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon\theta)|\geqslant\eta/2C
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}a_{\bm{e}}\left(1-\frac{\OO(\varepsilon)}{\eta}\right)^{n}\leqslant{\sum_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}a_{\bm{e}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left|\frac{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(\varepsilon x_{j}+\varepsilon\theta)}\right|^{2e_{i}}}\leqslant\sum_{{\bm{e}}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}a_{\bm{e}}\left(1+\frac{\OO(\varepsilon)}{\eta}\right)^{n}.

(155)

Hence, the denominator in the right-hand side of (
154
) is bounded away from
0
and
\infty
and, therefore, so is the numerator. The latter numerator matches the denominator in (
140
).
∎

4.6
Covariance Estimate

In this subsection we study the stochastic term
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(v)
in the right-hand side of (
4.5
) in Theorem
4.5
.

Proposition 4.18
.

Under Assumptions
4.1
,
4.4
in the probabilistic case (
85
), let us define

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(v):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v)(\rho(s;{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}})-\mathbb{E}[\rho(s;{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}})])}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s,

(156)

where the expectation is with respect to the transition probability (
81
). Then as
\varepsilon\to 0
the random field
\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(v)\}_{v\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]}
is asymptotically Gaussian with zero mean and covariance given by

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(v_{1})}{\varepsilon^{1/2}},\frac{\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(v_{2})}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\right]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}\theta}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}(w)\varphi^{+}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v_{1})}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(v_{1}))^{2}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v_{2})}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(v_{2}))^{2}}{\rm d}w,

(157)

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
but not
v_{1},v_{2}
. If we assume that
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
belong to a compact subset of
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, then the convergence is uniform in
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. The joint moments of
\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(v)\}_{v\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]}
converge to the Gaussian joint moments (uniformly in
v
, if we assume that
v
belongs to a compact subset of
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
).

We study random variables

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{3/2}}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v)(\rho(s;{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right\}_{v\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]},

which differ from
\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}(v)
by a deterministic shift. We let

\displaystyle\chi(z,v):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}\theta}\int_{z}^{z+\varepsilon\theta}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v)}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(s+\varepsilon)}-\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v)}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}\right){\rm d}s=\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}+\OO(\varepsilon).

(158)

We rewrite the random variables of interest as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{3/2}}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v)(\rho(s;{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(v)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s=\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i},v).\end{split}

(159)

In the following, we use Proposition
4.13
to compute the Laplace transform (moment generating function) of
\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i},v)
. For that we need a deformed version of the transition probability
\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})
. We fix any complex numbers
\bm{s}=(s_{1},s_{2},\dots,s_{p})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{p}
, and complex numbers
{\bm{v}}=(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{p})\in({\mathbb{C}}\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}])^{p}

and define a deformed version of
\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})=a_{\bm{e}}
from (
81
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\bm{s},{\bm{v}}}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{a_{\bm{e}}\exp\left(\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{p}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}s_{k}e_{i}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})\right)}{\sum\limits_{{\bm{e}}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}}a_{{\bm{e}}^{\prime}}\exp\left(\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{p}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}s_{k}e^{\prime}_{i}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})\right)}.

(160)

The resulting probability measure is still in the form of (
81
), but with
\varphi^{+}(z)
changed to

\displaystyle\varphi_{{\bm{v}},\bm{s}}^{+}(z)=\varphi^{+}(z)\exp\left(\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p}s_{k}\chi(z;v_{k})\right).

(161)

The expectation with respect to the deformed measure
\mathbb{P}^{\bm{s},{\bm{v}}}
can be rewritten as an expectation with respect to the original transition probability
\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}})
in the following way: for any random variable
\xi:\{0,1\}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{\bm{s},{\bm{v}}}}\left[\xi\right]=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\xi\exp\left(\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{p}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}s_{k}e_{i}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})\right)\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum\limits_{k=1}^{p}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}s_{k}e_{i}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})\right)\right]}.

(162)

The last identity implies that the joint Laplace transform of
\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v)
over
v=v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}
can be computed as an integral:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ln\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p}s_{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})\right)\right]&=\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{t}\ln\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(t\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p}s_{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})\right)\right]{\rm d}t\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}}\left[\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p}s_{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})\right]{\rm d}t,\end{split}

(163)

where
t\bm{s}=(ts_{1},\dots,ts_{p})
. We would like to apply Proposition
4.16
to the right-hand side of (
163
) and need to check the conditions of this theorem. In general,
\mathbb{P}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}
is a complex measure and it is unclear how to verify the condition (
86
). However, we claim that it is sufficient for us to restrict our attention to the case when
\mathbb{P}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}
is a bona fide (real positive) measure. Indeed, the law of
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v)
is uniquely determined by its real and imaginary parts. The joint Laplace transform of the former and the latter is accessed through the identity:

\mathbb{E}\exp\left(r_{1}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v)\right)+r_{2}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v)\right)\right)\\
=\mathbb{E}\exp\left(\frac{r_{1}-\mathbf{i}r_{2}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v)+\frac{r_{1}+\mathbf{i}r_{2}}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};\bar{v})\right).

(164)

If we choose
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
to be real (by the uniqueness theorem for the Laplace transform, this is sufficient for identifying the joint law of
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v)\bigr)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v)\bigr)
), then the random variable in the left-hand side of (
164
) is a positive real number, and the measures
\mathbb{P}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}
appearing in the integral (
163
) are also real-positive. Using a similar identity for the joint Laplace transforms of real and imaginary parts of several
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})
, we conclude that it is sufficient for us to deal in (
163
) with real-positive measures
\mathbb{P}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}
.

For positive measures
\mathbb{P}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}
, checking Assumptions
4.1
and
4.4
is straightforward (cf. Claim
4.17
) and Proposition
4.16
applies. Let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}(z)={\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi_{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}^{+}(z)+\varphi^{-}(z).

We rewrite
\ln{\mathcal{B}}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}(z)
in terms of
\ln{\mathcal{B}}(z)
from (
87
) with a small error:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ln{\mathcal{B}}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}(w)&=\ln\left({\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(w)\exp\left(t\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p}s_{k}\chi(w,v_{k})\right)+\varphi^{-}(w)\right)\\
&=\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w)+\ln\left(1+\frac{{\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(w)\left(\exp\left(t\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p}s_{k}\chi(w,v_{k})\right)-1\right)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\right)\\
&=\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w)+\varepsilon^{1/2}\frac{t{\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\sum_{k=1}^{p}s_{k}\chi(w;v_{k})+\OO\left(\varepsilon\right).\end{split}

(165)

Combining Proposition
4.16
(where we use only the first term of the asymptotic expansion) with (
165
) and (
158
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}}\left[\frac{\theta}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}})-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right]=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO\left(\varepsilon\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\frac{ts_{k}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)\chi(w;v_{k})}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO(\varepsilon)\\
&=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\frac{ts_{k}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v_{k}){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(z))^{2}({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(v_{k}))^{2}}+\OO(\varepsilon),\end{split}

(166)

where the contour
\omega_{-}\in\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
z,v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{p}
. Multiplying by
\varepsilon^{1/2}
, the left-hand side of (
166
) becomes
\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i},z)
, as seen from (
159
). Thus, specializing
z=v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{p}
in (
166
) and summing, we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}^{{\bm{v}},t\bm{s}}}\left[\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p}s_{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i},v_{k})\right]=\frac{1}{\theta\varepsilon^{1/2}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\frac{s_{k}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v_{k})}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(v_{k}))^{2}}{\rm d}w\\
&+\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\frac{ts_{k}s_{\ell}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v_{k}){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v_{\ell})}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(v_{k}))^{2}({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(v_{\ell}))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO(\varepsilon^{1/2}),\end{split}

(167)

where the contour
\omega_{-}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{p}
.
Plugging (
167
) into (
163
), and integrating, we get the joint Laplace transform of
\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})
,
k=1,\dots,p
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\ln\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\varepsilon^{1/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p}s_{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})\right)\right]=\frac{1}{\theta\varepsilon^{1/2}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\frac{s_{k}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v_{k}){\rm d}w}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(v_{k}))^{2}}\\
&+\frac{1}{\theta}\sum_{\ell=1}^{p}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\frac{s_{k}s_{\ell}}{4\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}(z)\varphi^{+}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v_{k}){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(v_{\ell})}{({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(v_{k}))^{2}({\mathsf{b}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}(v_{\ell}))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\OO(\varepsilon^{1/2}),\end{split}

(168)

where the contour
\omega_{-}\subset\Lambda
encloses
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, but not
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{p}
.

The first term in the right-hand side of (
168
) corresponds to the expected value of
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(\varepsilon x_{i};v_{k})
and disappears when we center these random variables. The second term matches the Laplace transform of the Gaussian vector with covariance given by (
157
). Thus, it only remains to show that the convergence of the Laplace transforms in (
168
) implies the convergence of the moments claimed in Proposition
4.18
. This is the content of the following abstract lemma, whose proof we omit.

Lemma 4.19
.

Consider a family of real random variables
\{\xi^{\varepsilon}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
depending on a parameter
\varepsilon>0
and indexed by some set
I
. Suppose that there exists another family
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, such that for any
p=1,2,\dots
, indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{p}\in I
, and real numbers
r_{1},\dots,r_{p}
we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}r_{k}\xi^{\varepsilon}_{i_{k}}\right)\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}r_{k}\xi_{i_{k}}\right)\right],

(169)

where for each fixed
p
and a compact set
\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
, the convergence in (
169
) is uniform over
r_{1},\dots,r_{p}
and over the choices of
i_{1},\dots,i_{p}\in I
. In addition, suppose that the real random variables
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
satisfy a uniform bound: there exists a continuous function
f(r)
such that

\mathbb{E}\exp(r\xi_{i})\leqslant f(r),\qquad\quad r\in\mathbb{R},\quad i\in I.

Then
\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\{\xi^{\varepsilon}_{i}\}_{i\in I}=\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
in the sense of moments uniformly in
i\in I
: for any
i_{1},\dots,i_{q}\in I
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{q}\xi_{i_{k}}^{\varepsilon}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{q}\xi_{i_{k}}\right],

where for each fixed
q
the convergence is uniform over possible choices of
i_{1},\dots,i_{q}\in I
.

This finishes the proof of Proposition
4.18
and, thus, also of Theorem
4.5
.
∎

5
Law of Large Numbers for random tilings

The goal of this section is to prove Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
.

5.1
Setup

We recall the trapezoid domain
{\mathsf{P}}
from Section
1.3
, with right side
[0,{\mathsf{N}}]
, width
{\mathsf{T}}
, and left sides
[{\mathsf{a}}_{i},{\mathsf{b}}_{i}]
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
, where
-{\mathsf{T}}\leqslant{\mathsf{a}}_{1}<{\mathsf{b}}_{1}<{\mathsf{a}}_{2}<{\mathsf{b}}_{2}<\dots<{\mathsf{a}}_{r}<{\mathsf{b}}_{r}\leqslant{\mathsf{N}}
, and

\sum_{i=1}^{r}({\mathsf{b}}_{i}-{\mathsf{a}}_{i})={\mathsf{N}}
; see Figure
2
.

As in Section
3.7.1
, we ignore

lozenges in tilings and trace only the lozenges of the remaining two types

and

. Then these lozenges form
N
non-intersecting paths traversing the trapezoid from the left boundary to the right boundary, as shown in the right panel of Figure
2
and in Figure
6
. At any time
0\leqslant t\leqslant T
, the nonintersecting paths correspond to an
N
point configuration
{\bm{x}}(t)=(x_{1}(t)>x_{2}(t)>\dots>x_{N}(t))\in\mathbb{W}^{N}
. Theorem
3.14
yields that for the
(q,\kappa)
-distribution (
8
) on lozenge tilings of a trapezoidal domain,
\{{\bm{x}}(t)\}_{0\leqslant t\leqslant T}
form a Markov chain with transition probability

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}({\bm{x}}(t+1)={\bm{x}}+{\bm{e}}|{\bm{x}}(t)={\bm{x}})\propto\prod_{i<j}\frac{b_{t}(x_{i}+e_{i})-b_{t}(x_{j}+e_{j})}{b_{t}(x_{i})-b_{t}(x_{j})}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{t}^{+}(x_{i})^{e_{i}}\phi_{t}^{-}(x_{i})^{1-e_{i}},

(170)

where
{\bm{e}}=(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{N})\in\{0,1\}^{N}
,

\displaystyle b_{t}(x)=q^{-x}+\kappa^{2}q^{x-t},

(171)

and the weights

\displaystyle\phi_{t}^{+}(x)=q^{T+N-1-t}(1-q^{x-N+1})(1-\kappa^{2}q^{x-T+1}),\quad\phi_{t}^{-}(x)=-(1-q^{x+T-t})(1-\kappa^{2}q^{x+N-t}).

At this point, we can forget about the lozenge tilings: our task is to study the Markov chain
{\bm{x}}(t)
with the above transition probabilities and with initial condition

{\bm{x}}(0)=(b_{r}-1,b_{r}-2,\dots,a_{r}+1,a_{r},\,\,\dots,\,\,b_{1}-1,b_{1}-2,\dots,a_{1}+1,a_{1}).

(172)

Note that we need to impose restrictions on the
{\bm{x}}(0)
and parameters
q
and
\kappa
in order to guarantee the positivity of probabilities (
170
); as in Section
1.3
there are three possible cases: imaginary, real, and trigonometric.

We introduce a small parameter
\varepsilon>0
, and set

\displaystyle N=\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{N}},\quad T=\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{T}},\quad q={\mathsf{q}}^{\varepsilon},\quad a_{i}=\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{a}}_{i},\quad b_{i}=\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{b}}_{i},\quad 1\leqslant i\leqslant r.

(173)

As in (
82
), we encode
{\bm{x}}(t)
through its smoothed empirical density:

\displaystyle\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t))=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bm{1}(\varepsilon x_{i}\leqslant s\leqslant\varepsilon x_{i}+\varepsilon).

(174)

Note that the parameters
N
,
a_{i}
,
b_{i}
and time
t
should be integers. Being somewhat sloppy, we are going to ignore this and use the formulas (
173
) and (
174
) for the values of
t
and
\varepsilon
, which might lead to non-integer results. Formally, one should add integer parts to the formulas in these situations, but, for the brevity of notations, we are not going to do this. We also define

\displaystyle{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)={\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}},

(175)

which is the version of (
171
) with rescaled variables.

Theorem 5.1
.

Consider the Markov chain
{\bm{x}}(t)=(x_{1}(t)>x_{2}(t)>\dots>x_{N}(t))
,
t=0,1,\dots,T
, on ordered
N
-tuples of integers with transition probabilities (
170
), a (deterministic) initial condition
{\bm{x}}(0)
such that
N>x_{1}(0)>\dots>x_{N}(0)\geqslant T
. Suppose that the data depends on a small parameter
\varepsilon>0
in such a way that
N=\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{N}}
,
T=\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{T}}
,
q={\mathsf{q}}^{\varepsilon}
. Fix
\delta>0
and additionally suppose that the parameters are such that
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is real and satisfies
|\partial_{z}{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)|>\delta
for
z\in[\max(\varepsilon x_{N}(0),{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}),\min(\varepsilon x_{1}(0)+{\mathsf{t}},{\mathsf{N}})]
and
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}
. Finally, suppose that the measures
\rho(x;{\bm{x}}(0))dx
weakly converge to a measure
\rho_{0}
.

Then for each
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}
the smoothed empirical measures converge to a deterministic limit

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\rho(\,\cdot\,;{\bm{x}}(\varepsilon^{-1}t))=\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(\,\cdot\,),\qquad\text{ weakly, in probability,}

(176)

which means that for each continuous function
f(s)
, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(s)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}})){\rm d}s=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(s)\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s){\rm d}s,\qquad\text{ in probability.}

The density
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
is supported inside
[{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}]
and can be determined by either of the equivalent descriptions of Propositions
5.3
,
5.5
, or
5.8
.

Remark 5.2
.

When the initial condition
{\bm{x}}(0)
is given by (
172
) with parameters scaling as in (
173
),
\rho_{0}
has the density

\rho_{0}(s)=\begin{cases}1,&s\in\cup_{i=1}^{r}[{\mathsf{a}}_{i},{\mathsf{b}}_{i}],\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We also have a product formula for the exponentiated (modified) Stieltjes transform:

\begin{split}\exp\left(\int_{-{\mathsf{T}}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{0}(z)\rho_{0}(s)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(s)}{\rm d}s\right)=\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}}\frac{(-\kappa^{-2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+{\mathsf{q}}^{z}){\mathsf{q}}^{s}\ln{\mathsf{q}}}{({\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{z})(\kappa^{-2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-{\mathsf{q}}^{s})}{\rm d}s\right)\\
=\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}}\left(\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{z}-{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}+\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{s}-\kappa^{-2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}\right){\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\left(\frac{1-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}-z}}{1-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}-z}}\cdot\frac{1-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z+{\mathsf{b}}_{i}}}{1-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z+{\mathsf{a}}_{i}}}\right).\end{split}

(177)

Proposition 5.3
.

The densities
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
in Theorem
5.1
are uniquely determined by the complex slope function
10
10

10

If we take
z
to be a real number in the support of
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
, then this is the same complex slope as in (
20
). However, in (
178
) we instead allow complex arguments
z
.

f_{\mathsf{t}}(z):=\exp\left(-\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)(1-\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right),

(178)

where
z
can be any complex number outside the set
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-1}({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}([{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}]))
. Further, for each such
z
,
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
solves a partial differential equation (a relative of the complex Burgers equation):

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)+\partial_{z}\ln\bigl(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\bigr)=\ln({\mathsf{q}})\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}.

(179)

Remark 5.4
.

The density
1-\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
has a transparent meaning in terms of the lozenge tilings: while
\rho_{{\mathsf{t}}}(s)
is the combined density of the lozenges of types

and

,
1-\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
is the density of

lozenges. By a computation similar to (
177
) the complex slope function can be also rewritten as

f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\frac{(1-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}-z})(1-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{T}}})}{(1-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z-{\mathsf{T}}+{\mathsf{t}}})(1-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z+{\mathsf{N}}-{\mathsf{t}}})}\exp\left(\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right).

(180)

Proposition 5.5
.

The complex slope functions (
178
) for the densities
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
in Theorem
5.1
can be computed as

f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\frac{({\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u})-({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z})}{({\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u})-({\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})},

(181)

where the function
u=u({\mathsf{t}},z)
solves the equation involving the complex slope of the initial condition
\rho_{0}
:

{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}-f_{0}(u)\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}(f_{0}(u){\mathsf{q}}^{-u}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u})}{1-f_{0}(u)}.

(182)

Remark 5.6
.

The equation (
182
) might have several solutions and one should choose the correct branch of
u({\mathsf{t}},z)
: the exact specification of this choice depends on
{\mathsf{q}}
and
\kappa
we work with. In Step 5 of Section
5.4
we explain in details the specification under Assumption
5.11
. In this case, if we deal with
z
satisfying
0<\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z<-\tfrac{\pi}{\ln{\mathsf{q}}}
,
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}/2-\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa
(the values at all other
z
can be reconstructed from these using symmetries of
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
), then we can choose the unique
u
such that

0<\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}u<-\tfrac{\pi}{\ln{\mathsf{q}}},{}\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}u<-\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa,\text{ and }\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left[{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1)\frac{f_{0}(u){\mathsf{q}}^{-u}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}}{1-f_{0}(u)}\right]<0.

Remark 5.7
.

Combination of (
177
) and (
180
) gives
f_{0}(u)
in (
182
) as an explicit product involving parameters (
173
) encoding the trapezoid. In this situation (
182
) turns into an algebraic equation in terms of the variable
{\mathsf{q}}^{u}
. This equation (after renaming
z
into
x
) coincides with (
19
).

Proposition 5.8
.

Define a function
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)
, through the relation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z))={\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)},\qquad w={\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z),

(183)

where
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is as in (
178
). Then:

1.

For each
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}
there exist a compactly supported measure
\mu_{\mathsf{t}}
on
\mathbb{R}
and a real constant
C_{\mathsf{t}}
, such that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)=\frac{w}{{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}}+C_{\mathsf{t}}-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\rm d}\mu_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{w-s}.

(184)

2.

The functions
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)
for different values of
{\mathsf{t}}
are linked by the functional relation

{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{(-1)}(v)+{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}}v={\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{(-1)}(v)+v,

(185)

where
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{(-1)}(v)
is the functional inverse of
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}
, which satisfies
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{(-1)}(v)=({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}})v+\oo(v)
as
v\to\infty
.

Remark 5.9
.

The function
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}
will be produced using the first integral (
219
) for the characteristics of the PDE (
179
), but we could also use another first integral (
220
) or, perhaps, the ratio of them. In each situation we would be getting a different version of (
185
) and a different definition of
\mu_{\mathsf{t}}
. Finding the best form of the functional relation is an interesting open problem.

Remark 5.10
.

The relation (
185
) has similarities with
[
28
, Eq. (2.9) and Proposition 3.4]
, which describes the limit shape in the case of the uniform measure on tilings (which can be obtained by setting
q={\mathsf{q}}=1
in (
9
)). The same paper also described how such formulas can be transformed to the formulas for the free projection in the free probability theory by the means of the Markov-Krein transform. It would be interesting to find an analogue of such transform in the present
(q,\kappa)
–setting.

Several possible choices of
{\mathsf{q}}
and
\kappa
make
b_{t}(x)
a monotone function of
x
in Theorem
5.1
, which guarantees that (
170
) is positive and corresponds to the condition
|\partial_{z}{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)|>\delta
in the statement of the theorem. The choices correspond to real, imaginary, and trigonometric cases outlined in Section
1.3
. In our proofs, we are only going to detail (a subcase of) the real case by imposing the following restriction:

Assumption 5.11
.

We require
0<{\mathsf{q}}<1
and we require
\kappa
to be a positive real number such that for some fixed
\delta>0
and all small enough
\varepsilon>0
we have

\kappa^{2}>\max_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}\left[{\mathsf{q}}^{-2\min(\varepsilon x_{1}(0)+{\mathsf{t}}/2,{\mathsf{N}}-{\mathsf{t}}/2)}\right]+\delta={\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{N}}-\varepsilon x_{1}(0)}+\delta.

The arguments for other possible choices of
{\mathsf{q}}
and
\kappa
in the imaginary and real cases are similar. The trigonometric case needs additional efforts and explanations. In order to keep our presentation concise, we excluded it from Theorem
5.1
by requiring
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)
to be a real function of real
x
there.

The proofs of Theorem
5.1
and Propositions
5.3
,
5.5
,
5.8
are presented in the rest of this section: in Subsection
5.2
we check that Theorem
4.5
is applicable by verifying its assumptions; in Subsection
5.3
we transform the conclusion of Theorem
4.5
into the form reminiscent to the PDE (
179
); in Subsection
5.4
we solve this PDE and show that Propositions
5.3
,
5.5
, and
5.8
are equivalent; in Subsection
5.5
we finish the proof of Theorem
5.1
; and in Subsection
5.6
we deduce Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
as its corollaries.

5.2
Assumptions in Theorem
4.5

Our proofs in this section rely on Theorem
4.5
with
\theta=1
applied to transition probabilities (
170
). Here we check that the required assumptions are satisfied and record the conclusion of the theorem. We start by introducing various notations.

We use three sets of functions:

\displaystyle\phi_{t}^{+}(x)=q^{T+N-1-t}(1-q^{x-N+1})(1-\kappa^{2}q^{x-T+1}),

\displaystyle\phi_{t}^{-}(x)=-(1-q^{x+T-t})(1-\kappa^{2}q^{x+N-t}),

\displaystyle\varphi^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\phi_{{\mathsf{t}}/\varepsilon}^{+}(z/\varepsilon),

\displaystyle\varphi^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\phi_{{\mathsf{t}}/\varepsilon}^{-}(z/\varepsilon),

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)={\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}+{\mathsf{N}}-{\mathsf{t}}}(1-{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{N}}})(1-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{T}}}),

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)=-(1-{\mathsf{q}}^{z+{\mathsf{T}}-{\mathsf{t}}})(1-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z+{\mathsf{N}}-{\mathsf{t}}}).

(186)

The relation between
\phi_{t}^{\pm}
and
\varphi_{\mathsf{t}}^{\pm}
is the same as between
\phi^{\pm}
and
\varphi^{\pm}
in the setting of Theorem
4.5
. On the other hand,
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\pm}
do not have shifts by
1
appearing in
\phi_{t}^{\pm}
. Let us also define

{\psi^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}}(z):=\ln({\mathsf{q}})\left(-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}+{\mathsf{N}}-{\mathsf{t}}}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{2z-{\mathsf{t}}}\right).

Then we have

\varphi^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\tilde{\varphi}^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)+\varepsilon\psi^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)+\OO(\varepsilon^{2}),\qquad\qquad\varphi^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\tilde{\varphi}^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}(z).

Note that the function
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)={\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}
is symmetric under
z\mapsto-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa
:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)={\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa),\quad{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa).

(187)

An advantage of the functions
\tilde{\varphi}^{\pm}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
over
\varphi^{\pm}(z)
is that they agree with this symmetry:

\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)}=\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)}=-\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2z}}{\kappa^{2}}.

(188)

Next, we recall the smoothed empirical density
\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t))
of
{\bm{x}}(t)
given by (
174
) and note its support:

\supp(\rho(s;{\bm{x}}({\mathsf{t}}/\varepsilon)))\subset[\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})],\quad\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}):=\max(\varepsilon x_{N}(0),{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}),\quad\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}}):=\min({\mathsf{N}},\varepsilon x_{1}(0)+{\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon).

(189)

We need several integral transforms of
\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t))
defined for complex
z
outside
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-1}({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}([\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]))
:

{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z):=\exp\left(\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}({\mathsf{t}}/\varepsilon))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right),\qquad\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z):=-{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)},

(190)

{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z):={\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\varphi_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)+\varphi_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z),\qquad\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z):={\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)+\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)=\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)(1-\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)).

(191)

Let us emphasize that since
{\bm{x}}(t)
is random, so are all the above integral transforms.
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is the function which we eventually need, because it enters Assumption
4.4
. However, it is simpler to analyze
\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
and
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
, because of the following symmetries under
z\mapsto-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa
transformations: using (
187
) we obtain

{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)};

further, using (
188
) we get

\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)=-{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)}=-\frac{1}{{\mathcal{G}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)}\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)}=\frac{1}{\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)};

and, finally, again using (
188
) we get

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)+\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)

\displaystyle=-\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}\left(\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)+{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)\right)=-\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z).

(192)

Proposition 5.12
.

In the framework of Theorem
5.1
, for each
t=0,1,2\dots,T-1
transition probabilities (
170
) satisfy the requirements of Theorem
4.5
, which means that Assumptions
4.1
and
4.4
hold almost surely for
{\bm{x}}={\bm{x}}(t)
.

We fix
t
with
{\mathsf{t}}=\varepsilon t
and set in Theorem
4.5

\theta=1
,
b=b_{t}
,
{\mathsf{b}}={\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}
,
\phi^{\pm}=\phi_{t}^{\pm}
,
\varphi^{\pm}=\varphi_{\mathsf{t}}^{\pm}
,
\mathbf{l}=\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})
,
\mathbf{r}=\bf({\mathsf{t}})
, and
{\mathcal{B}}(z)={\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
. For
{\bm{x}}
we are going to choose all possible values of
{\bm{x}}(t)
which arise with positive probabilities. For the set
\Lambda
we choose a large positive constant
C
and define

\Lambda=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,\Big|\,-\tfrac{\pi}{\ln({\mathsf{q}})}<\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(z)<\tfrac{\pi}{\ln({\mathsf{q}})},\quad-C<\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}(z)<\tfrac{{\mathsf{t}}}{2}-\log_{\mathsf{q}}(\kappa)-1/C\right\}.

(193)

Checking Assumption
4.1
is straightforward and we only focus on Assumption
4.4
. For that we first check the conditions of the latter assumption for the function
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
rather than
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
. For the check we need to identify all the zeros and singularities of
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
, which is split into several steps.

Step 1.
We would like to understand the solutions to
\tilde{f}_{t}(z)=1
for
z
such that
{\mathsf{q}}^{z}
is outside the set
[{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{r}},{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{l}}]\cup[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-\mathbf{l}}\kappa^{-2},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-\mathbf{r}}\kappa^{-2}]
. We claim that the solutions are at
z
satisfying
{\mathsf{q}}^{z}=\pm{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}/2}\kappa^{-1}
and
\tilde{f}_{t}^{\prime}(z)\neq 0
at these points. First, note that for such values of
{\mathsf{q}}^{z}
we have
{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=0
,
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=1
,
\tilde{\varphi}^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=-\tilde{\varphi}^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
, and therefore
\tilde{f}_{t}(z)=1
. In order to show that there are no other solutions, we rewrite
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
and
\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
using the same computation as in (
177
) and
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]\subset[{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}]
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}})}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right)=\exp\left(\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\left(\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{z}-{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}+\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}\right)\rho(s;{\bm{x}})\right),

(194)

\displaystyle-\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{N}}})(1-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{T}}})}{(1-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z-{\mathsf{T}}+{\mathsf{t}}})(1-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z+{\mathsf{N}}-{\mathsf{t}}})}=\exp\left(-\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}\left(\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{z}-{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}+\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}\right)\right),

(195)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{t}(z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)}\tilde{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\exp\left(\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}\left(\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}-\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}\right)(1-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))\right).

(196)

Denote

G(Z)=\int_{{\mathsf{q}}^{N}}^{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}-{\mathsf{t}}}}\frac{1-\rho(\log_{\mathsf{q}}(S);{\bm{x}})}{Z-S}{\rm d}S,

and notice that under the change of variable
Z={\mathsf{q}}^{z}
, we have

\tilde{f}_{t}(z)=\exp\left(G\Bigl(Z\Bigr)-G\Bigl(\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{t}\kappa^{-2}}{Z}\Bigr)\right).

(197)

For real
S
and complex
Z=Z_{1}+\mathbf{i}Z_{2}
, we have

\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left(\frac{1}{Z-S}\right)=-\frac{Z_{2}}{(S-Z_{1})^{2}+Z_{2}^{2}}.

Hence, for
Z
with positive imaginary part we have

0>\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}G(Z)>-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{Z_{2}}{(S-Z_{1})^{2}+Z_{2}^{2}}{\rm d}S=-\pi.

Similarly, for
Z
with negative imaginary part we have
0<\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}G(Z)<\pi
. Therefore, the expression under exponent in (
197
) has imaginary part between
-2\pi
and
0
if
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(Z)>0
and has imaginary part netween
0
and
2\pi
if
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(Z)<0
. We conclude that for
\tilde{f}_{t}(z)=1
it is necessary for
Z
to be real.

At this point the argument splits into two cases. Recall that we work under Assumption
5.11
of large real
\kappa
. The first case is that
\kappa^{2}>{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2{\mathsf{N}}}
, which means that the intervals
[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\kappa^{-2},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2}]
and
[{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{N}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}]
are disjoint (the intervals are images of each other under
Z\leftrightarrow\tfrac{{\mathsf{q}}^{t}\kappa^{-2}}{Z}
). The second case is when these two intervals intersect (note, however, that we always have the ordering
{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\kappa^{-2}<{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{N}}
and
{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2}<{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}
).

In the first case we note that directly from (
197
)
\tilde{f}_{t}(z)
is a decreasing function of
Z\in\mathbb{R}_{<0}
; hence, there is at most one solution to
\tilde{f}_{t}(z)=1
for
Z\in\mathbb{R}_{<0}
and this solution is at
Z=-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}/2}\kappa^{-1}
. Simultaneously, (
197
) implies that the logarithmic derivative (and hence, also the usual derivative) of
\tilde{f}_{t}
is bounded away from
0
at this
Z
. Furthermore,
\tilde{f}_{t}(Z)
is a decreasing function of
Z\in[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2},{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{N}}]
; hence, there is at most one solution to
\tilde{f}_{t}(z)=1
on this segment and this solution is at
Z={\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}/2}\kappa^{-1}
, with non-zero derivative of
\tilde{f}_{t}
. There are no other real
Z
leading to solutions of
\tilde{f}_{t}(z)=1
: For
0<Z<{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\kappa^{-2}
both terms in the exponent in (
197
) are negative; for
Z>{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}
both terms in the exponent are positive; for
Z\in[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\kappa^{-2},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2}]\setminus[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-\mathbf{l}}\kappa^{-2},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-\mathbf{r}}\kappa^{-2}]
and for
Z\in[{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{N}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}]\setminus[{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{r}},{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{l}}]
the values of
\tilde{f}_{t}(z)
are negative, because both
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
and
\tfrac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)}
are positive.

In the second case of intersecting intervals
[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}}\kappa^{-2},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2}]
and
[{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{N}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}]
, the argument remains mostly the same, except for the new case
Z\in[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2}]
replacing the previous
Z\in[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}]
. In this new case, we can no longer rely on (
197
), because we are at the singularities of the integrands and the meaning of the integrals are unclear; however we can still rely on (
194
) because
Z
is between the intervals
[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-\mathbf{l}}\kappa^{-2},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-\mathbf{r}}\kappa^{-2}]
and
[{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{r}},{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{l}}]
, we can also use the first formula of (
195
). We, thus, modify (
197
) into

\tilde{f}_{t}(z)=\frac{H(Z)}{H\Bigl(\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{t}\kappa^{-2}}{Z}\Bigr)},

(198)

where
Z={\mathsf{q}}^{z}
, and
H(Z)
is

\frac{Z-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{N}}}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}+{\mathsf{t}}}-Z}\exp\left(\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{Z-{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}\rho(s;{\bm{x}})\right)=\exp\left(\ln\left[\frac{Z-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{N}}}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{r}}-Z}\right]-\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{Z-{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}(1-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))\right).

(199)

Notice that for
Z\in[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2}]

\displaystyle\partial_{Z}\ln H(Z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{N}}}}+\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{r}}-Z}+\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{(Z-{\mathsf{q}}^{s})^{2}}(1-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}))

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{Z-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{N}}}}+\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{r}}-Z}+\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{(Z-{\mathsf{q}}^{s})^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{N}}}}+\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}-Z}>0.

We conclude that
H(Z)
is increasing in
Z
and so is
H\Bigl(\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{t}\kappa^{-2}}{Z}\Bigr)^{-1}
. Therefore,
\tilde{f}_{t}(z)
is increasing in
Z
, and the equation
\tilde{f}_{t}(z)=1
is solved on
Z\in[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2}]
only by
Z={\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}/2}\kappa^{-1}
. The above bounds on the derivative of
\ln H(Z)
imply that the derivative of
\tilde{f}_{t}
at
Z={\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}/2}\kappa^{-1}
does not vanish.

Step 2.
We rewrite
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}({\mathsf{q}}^{z})
and analyze the zeros and singularities of
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}(Z)
. We claim that

•

All singularities of
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}(Z)
are inside the set
[{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{r}},{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathbf{l}}]\cup[{\mathsf{q}}^{-\mathbf{l}+{\mathsf{t}}}\kappa^{-2},{\mathsf{q}}^{-\mathbf{r}+{\mathsf{t}}}\kappa^{-2}]
.

•

The zeros of
\mathfrak{B}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(Z)
outside this set are at points
Z=\pm{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}/2}\kappa^{-1}
.

Observe that the singularities of
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}({\mathsf{q}}^{z})
are a subset of singularities of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
, and the latter are singularities of the integral in (
194
).

For the zeros, we note that according to the definition (
191
),
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}({\mathsf{q}}^{z})
vanishes whenever either
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)=\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)=0
or
\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=1
. In the first case, since zeros of
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)
are at
{\mathsf{q}}^{z}\in\{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{N}},{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{T}}\kappa^{-2}\}
and zeros of
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)
are at
{\mathsf{q}}^{z}\in\{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2}\}
, we should have either
{\mathsf{q}}^{z}={\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{N}}={\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{N}}}\kappa^{-2}
or
{\mathsf{q}}^{z}={\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{T}}\kappa^{-2}={\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}
; in both situations the equality requires special values of
\kappa
and implies
w={\mathsf{q}}^{z}={\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}/2}\kappa^{-1}
. The second case
\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=1
was analyzed in Step 1.

Step 3.
We now check Assumption
4.4
for
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
. In Step 2 we identified all zeros and singularities of
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
. Comparing with (
193
) we conclude that they are all outside
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
; we recall
\Lambda
from (
193
). Hence,
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is bounded away from
0
and
\infty
on compact subsets of
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, as claimed in the assumption (the constant
c
can be chosen to be independent of
{\bm{x}}
). It remains to check the second condition, i.e. we want to verify

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\rm d}z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}0,

(200)

where
\omega
is an arbitrary simple contour inside
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, enclosing the segment
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
.
Let
\omega^{\prime}
be an image of
\omega
under the map
z\mapsto-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa

and assume that both
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
are positively oriented.
Then (
200
) can be transformed using (
5.2
) and its
z
–derivative into

-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}}\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)}{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)}{\rm d}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}}\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\rm d}z+\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}}\partial_{z}\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}\right){\rm d}z.

(201)

We claim that the second integral in the right-hand side vanishes. Indeed, the definition of
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
readily implies that
\ln\left(\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}\right)
is a holomorphic function on the contour
\omega^{\prime}
; hence, the contour integral of its derivative vanishes.
Combining (
200
) with its equivalent form (
201
), we rewrite the desired identity as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega\cup\omega^{\prime}}\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\rm d}z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}0,

As in Step 2, we change variables and rewrite
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(z)=\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}(Z)
,
Z={\mathsf{q}}^{z}
. We have,

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega\cup\omega^{\prime}}\frac{\partial_{z}\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\rm d}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega\cup\omega^{\prime}}\frac{\partial_{z}\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}({\mathsf{q}}^{z})}{\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}({\mathsf{q}}^{z})}{\rm d}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\mathsf{q}}^{\omega}\cup{\mathsf{q}}^{\omega^{\prime}}}\frac{\mathfrak{B}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(Z)}{\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}(Z)}{\rm d}Z.

(202)

We compute the last integral as (minus) the sum of the residues outside the integration contour. These residues are:

•

Simple zeros of
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}(Z)
at points
Z=\pm{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}/2}\kappa^{-1}
as identified in Steps 1 and 2, lead to two poles in the integrand, each one coming with residue
-1
for the integrand.

•

At
Z=0
the function
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}(Z)
is holomorphic and there is no residue for the integrand.

•

As
Z\to\infty
the function
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}(Z)
grows as
cZ^{2}
for a certain non-zero constant
c
, giving the residue
2
for the integrand.

Altogether, the residues cancel out and (
202
) integrates to
0
.

Step 4.
We come back to
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
as in (
191
) and check that it satisfies Assumption
4.4
. We have

{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)+\varepsilon\psi^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)+\OO(\varepsilon^{2}).

(203)

Hence, because
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is bounded away from
0
and
\infty
on compact subsets of
\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, so is
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
for small enough
\varepsilon
.

Next, we note that the value of the contour integral
\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\rm d}z
is an integer, because this is the integral of the derivative of the multi-valued function
\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\ln({\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z))
, whose values at a point differ by integers from each other (equivalently, the integral counts the winding number of the curve
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(\omega)
). On the other hand, (
203
) and the result of Step 3 imply that

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\rm d}z=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega}\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\rm d}z+\OO(\varepsilon)=\OO(\varepsilon).

Hence, for small
\varepsilon
the contour integral vanishes.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

The proof of (
200
) in Proposition
5.12
might look mysterious and one can be wondering about simpler reasons to expect this identity to be true. If we replace
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
by
B_{\mathsf{t}}(z):=\tilde{\varphi}^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))
, where we used the form of (
191
) with complex slope of (
178
), (
180
), then the vanishing of the contour integral can be related to the version of the complex Burger’s equation of (
179
). Indeed, this equation gives

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\partial_{z}\ln B_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=-\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln f_{\mathsf{t}}+\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}+\ln{\mathsf{q}}\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\left[\int_{\mathbf{l}}^{\mathbf{r}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right]-\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln\tilde{\varphi}^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}+\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln\tilde{\varphi}^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}+\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}+\ln{\mathsf{q}}\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}.

The last term and
-\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln\tilde{\varphi}^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}
are analytic in a neighborhood of
[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]
, thus, their contour integrals vanish. An important cancellation is
\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln\tilde{\varphi}^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}+\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}=0
, because
\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}
is a function of
z-{\mathsf{t}}
.
The only thing left is the
{\rm d}s
–integral; its contour integral gives the time derivative of the total mass of
\rho_{t}
, which is zero.

Let us record the conclusion of Theorem
4.5
. In our present setting (
4.5
) implies that for
{\mathsf{t}}=t\varepsilon
,
\Lambda_{\mathsf{t}}
given by
\Lambda
of (
193
), and
z\in\Lambda_{\mathsf{t}}\setminus[\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]
we have

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)(\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t+1))-\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t)))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{\rm d}s\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w\,+\varepsilon R_{t}(z)+\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)+\,\OO\left(\varepsilon^{2}\right),

(204)

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda_{\mathsf{t}}
encloses
[\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]
, but not
z
;
R_{t}(z)
is a bounded deterministic term; and conditionally on
{\bm{x}}(0),{\bm{x}}(1),\dots,{\bm{x}}(t)
,
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)
are mean
0
random variables such that
\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)\}_{z\in\Lambda\setminus[\mathbf{l},\mathbf{r}]}
are asymptotically Gaussian with covariance given by

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z_{1})}{\varepsilon^{1/2}},\frac{\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z_{2})}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)\varphi^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}(z_{1})}{({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z_{1}))^{2}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z_{2})}{({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z_{2}))^{2}}{\rm d}w,

(205)

with
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
outside the integration contour.

5.3
Application of Theorem
4.5

The aim of this subsection is to transform the result of Theorem
4.5
for lozenge tilings, as recorded in (
204
), (
205
), into the following statement:

Proposition 5.14
.

In the framework of Theorem
5.1
and using the notations of Sections
5.1
,
5.2
,
for
{\mathsf{t}}=t\varepsilon
we have as
\varepsilon\to 0
:

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t+1))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon}(s)}{\rm d}s-\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right]\\
=-\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)+\ln({\mathsf{q}})\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}-\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)+\varepsilon R_{t}(z)+\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)+O(\varepsilon^{2}),

(206)

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda_{\mathsf{t}}
encloses
[\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]
, but not
z
; and
R_{t}(z)
is a term (we do not claim that it is the same as in (
204
)) which can be expressed as an integral of a uniformly bounded function against the measure
\rho(s,{\bm{x}}(t)){\rm d}s
. After conditioning on
{\bm{x}}(0),{\bm{x}}(1),\dots,{\bm{x}}(t)
, the terms
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)
are mean
0
random variables such that
\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)\}_{z\in\Lambda_{\mathsf{t}}\setminus[\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]}
are asymptotically Gaussian with covariance given by

\frac{\mathbb{E}[\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z_{1})\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z_{2})|{\bm{x}}(t)]}{\varepsilon}\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}{\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-1}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(z_{1})}{({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z_{1}))^{2}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(z_{2})}{({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z_{2}))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\oo(1),

(207)

with
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
outside the integration contour. The asymptotic Gaussianity is in the convergence of moments sense, as in Theorem
4.5
.
O(\varepsilon^{2})
is in the sense of moments, uniformly in
{\mathsf{t}}
and
z\in\Lambda_{\mathsf{t}}
bounded away from
[\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]
.

The left-hand side of (
206
) differs from the left-hand side of (
204
) by the term

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon}(s)}-\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}\right)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t+1)){\rm d}s\\
=\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\partial_{t}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}\right)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t)){\rm d}s+\OO(\varepsilon).

(208)

The asymptotic covariance for the term
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)
in (
206
) and in (
204
) is the same: we only used the identity

\frac{{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)\varphi^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}{{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}=\frac{\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}{\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-1}+\OO(\varepsilon)

to rewrite the covariance (
205
) as (
207
). This identity follows from
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)=\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)+\OO(\varepsilon)
,
\varphi^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)=\tilde{\varphi}^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)
and definitions (
190
), (
191
).

We further simplify the leading term in the right-hand side (
204
), integrating by parts and then deforming the integration contour through
w=z
. Denoting through
{\omega_{+}}
a contour including both
[\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]
and
z
, we have

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\ln{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}}{\rm d}w=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\ln{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)\partial_{w}\left[\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}\right]{\rm d}w\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{\partial_{w}\ln{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\rm d}w=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{+}}}\frac{\partial_{w}\ln{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w){\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\rm d}w-\partial_{z}\ln{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z).

(209)

We replace
{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)=\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)+\OO(\varepsilon)
throughout the last formula, accumulating
\OO(\varepsilon)
total error. The second term
-\partial_{z}\ln{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
then turns into the first term in the right-hand side of (
206
) and it remains to deal with

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{+}}}\left[\partial_{w}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)\right]\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\rm d}w\\
&=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{+}}}\left[\partial_{w}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)\right]\cdot\left(\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{z}-{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}+\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{w}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}\right).\end{split}

(210)

It is helpful to make a change of variables
w=-w^{\prime}+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa
in the second integral (
210
); denoting the image of
{\omega_{+}}
contour by
\omega^{\prime}
(both are positively oriented) and using (
5.2
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{+}}}\left[\partial_{w}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)\right]\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{w}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}}\left[-\partial_{w^{\prime}}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(-w^{\prime}+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)\right]\cdot\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{-w^{\prime}+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{-w^{\prime}+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}}\left[-\partial_{w^{\prime}}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w^{\prime})+2\ln{\mathsf{q}}+\partial_{w^{\prime}}\ln{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w^{\prime})\right]\cdot\left(-{\rm d}w^{\prime}-\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w^{\prime}}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{z}-{\mathsf{q}}^{w^{\prime}}}\right).

The
{\rm d}w^{\prime}
integral vanishes and so does the integral of
2\ln{\mathsf{q}}\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w^{\prime}}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{z}-{\mathsf{q}}^{w^{\prime}}}
. Hence, renaming back
w^{\prime}
into
w
, we convert (
210
) into

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{+}}\cup\omega^{\prime}}\partial_{w}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{w}-{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}}\partial_{w}\ln{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{w}-{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}.

(211)

We compute two integrals in (
211
) separately. For the first integral, we recall the notation
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)=\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}({\mathsf{q}}^{w})
and make a change of variables
W={\mathsf{q}}^{w}
, converting the integral into

\frac{\ln{\mathsf{q}}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\omega_{+}}}\cup{\mathsf{q}}^{\omega^{\prime}}}W\left[\partial_{W}\ln\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}(W)\right]\frac{{\rm d}W}{W-{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}.

We compute the last integral as minus the sum of the residues outside the integration contour. This is similar to Steps 2 and 3 in the proof of Proposition
5.12
. The residues are at
Z=\pm{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}/2}\kappa^{-1}
and at
Z=\infty
. At each of the former points,
\partial_{W}\ln\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}(W)
has a simple pole of residue
1
; hence, these points give contribution:

-2\ln{\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}}\kappa^{-2}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}}\kappa^{-2}-{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}\right)=-2\ln{\mathsf{q}}-2\ln{\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}}\kappa^{-2}-{\mathsf{q}}^{2z}}\right).

(212)

At large
W
,
\mathfrak{B}_{\mathsf{t}}(W)
grows as
cW^{2}
and therefore, the integrand behaves as
\frac{2}{W}
, resulting in the contribution
2\ln{\mathsf{q}}
. Summing with (
212
) and also noticing that
\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln\tilde{\varphi}^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=-\ln{\mathsf{q}}
, we conclude that

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{+}}\cup\omega^{\prime}}\partial_{w}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{w}-{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}=-\ln{\mathsf{q}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}\right)+\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln\tilde{\varphi}^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z).

Combining with
-\partial_{z}\ln{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
from (
209
) we obtain all three first terms in (
206
). It remains to show that (
208
) cancels with the second integral in (
211
). We transform the latter using the formula for
{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)
in (
194
):

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}}\partial_{w}\ln{\mathcal{G}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{w}-{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}
