\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}}\partial_{w}\left[\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\left(\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{w}-{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}+\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-w+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}\right)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t))\right]\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{w}-{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}.

In the terms of the last formula the first one integrates to
0
because there are no
w
–singularities inside the
\omega^{\prime}
contour. For the second term we swap the order of integration and integrate over
w
first. Changing the variables
W={\mathsf{q}}^{w}
and then evaluating the integral as (minus) sum of the residues outside the integration contour, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\partial_{w}\left[\frac{1}{{\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-w+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}\right]\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{w}-{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\int_{\omega^{\prime}}\frac{\ln{\mathsf{q}}\cdot{\mathsf{q}}^{-w+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}{({\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-w+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa})^{2}}\cdot\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{w}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{w}-{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\int_{{\mathsf{q}}^{\omega^{\prime}}}\frac{W\ln{\mathsf{q}}\cdot{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}{(W{\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa})^{2}}\cdot\frac{{\rm d}W}{W-{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{z}\ln{\mathsf{q}}\cdot{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}{({\mathsf{q}}^{z}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa})^{2}}=\frac{\ln{\mathsf{q}}\cdot{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}{({\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa})^{2}}.

We conclude that the second integral in (
211
) is

\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{\ln{\mathsf{q}}\cdot{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}{({\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa})^{2}}\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t)){\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}.

On the other hand, the integral in (
208
) is readily transformed to the same expression:

\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\partial_{t}\ \left(\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{z}-{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}+\frac{{\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}}\right)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t))=\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{\ln{\mathsf{q}}\cdot{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa}\cdot}{({\mathsf{q}}^{s}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa})^{2}}\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t)){\rm d}{\mathsf{q}}^{s}.\qed

5.4
Equivalent descriptions of the limit shape

In this subsection we show that the descriptions of
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
given in Propositions
5.3
,
5.5
, and
5.8
are equivalent.

Theorem 5.15
.

Take a family
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
,
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}
of measures of total mass
{\mathsf{N}}
, supported on
[{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}]
, and of density
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
at most
1
. Suppose that
\rho_{0}
is given, while
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
,
{\mathsf{t}}>0
are unknown. If these measures are descibed by either of Proposition
5.3
, Proposition
5.5
, or Proposition
5.8
, the resulting family
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
is the same.

Step 1.
We start by explaining that the complex slope
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
defined through (
178
) uniquely determines the density
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
. Indeed, for
x\in[{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}]
and
\varepsilon>0
we write

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\arg f_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)=-\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left(\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)(1-\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right)

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)}{[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)]+[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)]}\right)(1-\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)){\rm d}s

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)]-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)]}{[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)]^{2}-[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)]^{2}}\right)(1-\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)){\rm d}s.

Let us send
\varepsilon\to 0+
in the last formula. Assuming
{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)\neq 0
, we have (uniformly over
s\in\mathbb{[}{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}]
)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left(\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)]}{[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)]^{2}-[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)]^{2}}\right)=\frac{\varepsilon{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)]+\OO(\varepsilon^{2})}{[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)]^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)^{2}+\OO(\varepsilon^{3})},

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left(\frac{-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)]}{[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)]^{2}-[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)]^{2}}\right)=\frac{-\varepsilon({\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x))^{2}+\OO(\varepsilon^{2})}{[{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)]^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)^{2}+\OO(\varepsilon^{3})}.

Hence, using the fact

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\varepsilon}{y^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}}{\rm d}y=\pi,\quad y=\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(x)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(x)},

and that the integral is sharply concentrated around
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, we conclude that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\frac{1}{\pi}\arg f_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)=1-\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(x).

Step 2.
Next, we take
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
satisfying the PDE of Proposition
5.3
, which we rewrite as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}+\frac{\partial_{z}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}=\ln({\mathsf{q}})\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}.

(213)

Our goal is to solve
11
11

11

We only search for solutions of the form (
178
) and do not investigate possibility of other solutions.
this PDE arriving at the expression of Proposition
5.5
. For that we are going to use the characteristics method.

The method guides to search for the three–dimensional hypersurface of triplets
({\mathsf{t}},z,f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))
solving (
213
) as a union of one-dimensional characteristic curves
{\mathsf{t}}(\tau)
,
z(\tau)
,
f(\tau)
, with each curve solving the differential equation

\partial_{\tau}{\mathsf{t}}=\frac{1}{f},\qquad\partial_{\tau}z=\frac{1}{f-1},\qquad\partial_{\tau}f=\ln({\mathsf{q}})\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}.

Because we only care about the hypersurface itself, we can replace
\tau
by any other parameter. The key simplifying observation, leading to the relatively explicit expressions for
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
in our case, is that we are able to choose
{\mathsf{t}}
as the parameter. Hence, we are looking for a two-dimensional family of curves
12
12

12

Somewhat abusing the notations, the upper index
{\mathsf{t}}
in
z^{{\mathsf{t}}}
and
f^{\mathsf{t}}
indicates the time coordinate they depend on; on the other hand,
{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}
stays for the number
{\mathsf{q}}
raised to the power
{\mathsf{t}}
.

(z^{\mathsf{t}},f^{\mathsf{t}})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant 0
, which we are going to enumerate by their initial conditions
(z^{0},f^{0})=(u,f_{0}(u))
. We choose the
z
–component of the curve to be solving the differential equation

\partial_{\mathsf{t}}z^{\mathsf{t}}=\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\mathsf{t}})}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\mathsf{t}})-1},\quad{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant 0,\qquad z^{0}=u.

(214)

The equation for the
f
–component can be read from the fact that
f^{\mathsf{t}}
comes from substituting
z=z_{\mathsf{t}}
into the function
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
solving (
213
):

\displaystyle\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f^{\mathsf{t}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathsf{t}}[f_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\mathsf{t}})]=\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\Big|_{z=z^{\mathsf{t}}}+\partial_{z}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\Big|_{z=z^{\mathsf{t}}}\partial_{\mathsf{t}}z^{\mathsf{t}}=f_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\mathsf{t}})\left[\frac{\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}+\frac{\partial_{z}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}\right]_{z=z^{\mathsf{t}}}

\displaystyle=\ln({\mathsf{q}})f^{\mathsf{t}}\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z^{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z^{\mathsf{t}}}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z^{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z^{\mathsf{t}}}},\quad{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant 0,\qquad f^{0}=f_{0}(u).

(215)

We further introduce a domain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}=\left\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\,0\leqslant\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z\leqslant-\tfrac{\pi}{\ln{\mathsf{q}}},\,\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}/2-\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa\right\},

(216)

and notice that
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}
is a bijection between this domain and closed lower half-plane, which is a conformal bijection between the interior of this domain denoted
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}
and open lower half-plane.
Thanks to the relations (which are all immediate from the definition):

f_{\mathsf{t}}(-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)=\frac{1}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)},\qquad f_{\mathsf{t}}(\bar{z})=\overline{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)},\qquad f_{\mathsf{t}}\left(z+\tfrac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{\ln{\mathsf{q}}}\right)=f_{\mathsf{t}}(z),

(217)

we can restrict our study of
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
to
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
: the values of
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
outside
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
can be determined from its value inside
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
. Hence, we can summarize our task: for each
z\in{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
, we would like to find an initial condition
u
and corresponding solution to the following system of differential equations

\begin{cases}\partial_{\mathsf{t}}z^{\mathsf{t}}=\frac{f^{\mathsf{t}}}{f^{\mathsf{t}}-1},&z^{0}=u,\\
\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f^{\mathsf{t}}=\ln({\mathsf{q}})f^{\mathsf{t}}\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z^{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z^{\mathsf{t}}}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z^{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z^{\mathsf{t}}}},\qquad\qquad&f^{0}=f_{0}(u),\end{cases}

(218)

such that
z^{\mathsf{t}}=z
; once they are found, we obtain
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=f^{\mathsf{t}}
.

The system of differential equations (
218
) has two first integrals, which are expressions staying constant along the trajectories
({\mathsf{t}},z^{\mathsf{t}},f^{\mathsf{t}})
, cf.
[
19
, Theorem 2.1]
. As can be verified by straightforward differentiation, they are given by

\displaystyle U(u)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}\frac{f^{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{q}}^{-z^{\mathsf{t}}}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z^{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f^{\mathsf{t}}}=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}}{2}\left(-({\mathsf{q}}^{-z^{\mathsf{t}}}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z^{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}})+({\mathsf{q}}^{-z^{\mathsf{t}}}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z^{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}})\frac{1+f^{\mathsf{t}}}{1-f^{\mathsf{t}}}\right),

(219)

\displaystyle V(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-z^{\mathsf{t}}}-f^{\mathsf{t}}\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z^{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f^{\mathsf{t}}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(({\mathsf{q}}^{-z^{\mathsf{t}}}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z^{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}})+({\mathsf{q}}^{-z^{\mathsf{t}}}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z^{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}})\frac{1+f^{\mathsf{t}}}{1-f^{\mathsf{t}}}\right).

(220)

Because trajectories are parameterized by initial conditions
u=z^{0}
, the values of the first integrals in (
219
), (
220
) depend on
u
. This dependence can be made explicit by plugging
{\mathsf{t}}=0
into (
219
), (
220
):

U(u)=\frac{f_{0}(u){\mathsf{q}}^{-u}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}}{1-f_{0}(u)},\qquad V(u)=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}-f_{0}(u)\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}}{1-f_{0}(u)}.

(221)

Combining (
219
) and (
220
) and recalling
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z_{\mathsf{t}})={\mathsf{q}}^{-z_{\mathsf{t}}}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z_{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{t}}}
, we express
z^{\mathsf{t}}
in terms of
{\mathsf{t}}
and
u
:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z_{\mathsf{t}})=V(u)-\frac{U(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}}.

(222)

If we treat (
222
) as an equation determining
u
as a function of
{\mathsf{t}}
and
z=z^{\mathsf{t}}
, then it matches (
182
). Once we find
u
, it is also helpful to subtract (
222
) from its
{\mathsf{t}}=0
version resulting in

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z_{\mathsf{t}})-{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(u)}{1-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}}=U(u).

(223)

Equating the expressions for
U(u)
of (
223
) with (
219
), we can express
f^{\mathsf{t}}
through
z^{\mathsf{t}}
,
u
, and
{\mathsf{t}}
. Replacing
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=f^{\mathsf{t}}
and
z=z^{\mathsf{t}}
, we arrive at the desired (
181
) of Proposition
5.5
. Note that (
222
) might have several
u
satisfying it (or none) and we have not yet explained how to choose the correct
u
— this will be done in Step 5.

Step 3.
Further, we present an alternative way to package the characteristics method for finding
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
, which solves the PDE of Proposition
5.3
. This will lead us to the expression of Proposition
5.8
.

Guided by (
219
) and using symmetries (
217
) we notice that the expression

{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}

is unchanged under
z\mapsto z+\frac{2\pi\mathbf{i}}{\ln{\mathsf{q}}}
and
z\mapsto-z+{\mathsf{t}}-2\ln(\kappa)
. Therefore, there exists a function
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)
of complex argument
w
, such that

{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z))={\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)},

(224)

for all
z
such that
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is outside
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}([{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}])
. The function
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)
is holomorphic in
w
, except, perhaps, at
w\in{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}([{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}])
. Notice that once we know
({\mathsf{t}},z,{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)))
, we also know
({\mathsf{t}},z,f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))
and vice-versa.

We now come back to (
218
) and introduce
w^{\mathsf{t}}:={\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\mathsf{t}})
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{t}}={\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w^{\mathsf{t}})
. Let us describe the evolution of the triplet
({\mathsf{t}},w^{\mathsf{t}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{t}})
,
{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant 0
. Notice that
U(u)
in (
219
) becomes
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{t}}
. Therefore, the value of
{\mathcal{S}}^{{\mathsf{t}}}
does not depend on
{\mathsf{t}}
. On the other hand, (
223
) becomes

w^{\mathsf{t}}=w^{0}+(1-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}})U(u)=w^{0}-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}^{0}.

(225)

Therefore, the equation
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{t}}={\mathcal{S}}^{0}
can be rewritten as

{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}\left(w_{0}-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w_{0})\right)={\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w_{0}).

(226)

If we now rename
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w_{0})=v
, then we get

{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{(-1)}(v)-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1)v={\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{(-1)}(v),

which is precisely (
185
) of Proposition
5.8
.

Step 4.
We are now going to show that the function
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)
defined in the previous step by (
224
) has the form (
184
) for some measure
\mu_{\mathsf{t}}
. For that we would like to check that
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)
is a Nevanlinna function:

\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)<0\quad\text{ whenever }\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}w<0.

(227)

Recall that
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is a conformal bijection between the interior
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}
of the domain of (
216
) and lower half-plane. Hence, (
227
) is equivalent to

\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)<0\quad\text{ whenever }z\in{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ},\qquad\text{ where }\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)={\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}.

(228)

It is helpful to also use an alternative representation of
\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
:

\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}}{2}\left(-({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})+({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})\frac{1+f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}\right).

(229)

For
z
on the right boundary of
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
, i.e.
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z={\mathsf{t}}/2-\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa
, we have
|f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)|=1
, because the integrand in (
178
) is purely imaginary. Further,
{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}
is real and
{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}
is purely imaginary. Plugging into (
229
) we conclude that
\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is real on the right boundary of
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
.

For
z
on the bottom boundary of
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
by the argument of Step 1,
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\geqslant 0
and therefore also
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left[\frac{1+f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}\right]\geqslant 0
. On the other hand,
{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}
is real and
{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}
is negative real. Plugging into (
229
) we conclude that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\leqslant 0
on the bottom boundary of
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
. Note that for the points on the bottom boundary where
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)\in\{0,1\}
, the same argument shows that
\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)
is real.

For
z
on the top boundary of
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
, the integrand in (
178
) is real (and non-singular); therefore,
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is real, and so are
{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}
and
{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}
. Plugging into (
229
) we conclude that
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=0
on the top boundary of
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
.

{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
does not have a left boundary, but we should also study the case
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z\to-\infty
. We investigate the integrand in (
178
) using

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}=\ln{\mathsf{q}}+\OO({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}),\qquad\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z\to-\infty,

Since the total mass of the measure
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
is
{\mathsf{N}}
, we get
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)={\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}+\OO({\mathsf{q}}^{-z})
and therefore,

\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}}+\OO(1),\qquad\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z\to-\infty.

(230)

Inside
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
we have
0<\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}z<-\frac{\pi}{\ln{\mathsf{q}}}
, leading to the imaginary part of the last expression being negative.

The function
\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is harmonic in
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}
. We have shown that it is non-positive on the boundary of this domain and as
\mathop{\mathrm{Re}}z\to-\infty
. Using the maximum principle, we conclude that (
228
) and, thus, also (
227
) holds.

Using the representation theorem for Nevanlinna functions (we refer to Appendix A in
[
6
]
for a detailed discussion), (
227
) implies that

{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)=\alpha w+\beta-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{1}{w-s}+\frac{s}{1+s^{2}}\right)\mu_{\mathsf{t}}(ds),

(231)

for a certain measure
\mu_{\mathsf{t}}
on
\mathbb{R}
and real constants
\alpha>0
and
\beta
. Our definitions imply that
\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
does not have any singularities for large complex
z
; therefore,
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)
is holomorphic for all large enough
w
and the measure
\mu_{{\mathsf{t}}}(ds)
should be compactly supported. Hence,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{s}{1+s^{2}}\mu_{\mathsf{t}}(ds)
can be absorbed into
\beta
. On the other hand, (
230
) implies that for large
w

{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)=\frac{w}{{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}}+\oo(w).

This fixes the constant
\alpha
in (
231
) and turns it into (
184
).

Step 5.
In the previous steps we explained how using the values of
z^{\mathsf{t}}
or
w^{\mathsf{t}}
one can reconstruct the values of
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\mathsf{t}})
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w^{\mathsf{t}})
, respectively. In this step we analyze the values which
z^{\mathsf{t}}
and
w^{\mathsf{t}}
take.

We define the liquid region of the measure
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
through:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{t}}=\{s\in[{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}]\mid 0<\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)<1\}.

(232)

Observe that by the argument of Step 4, the only points of the boundary of
{\mathbb{D}}_{0}
where
\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is non-real are those of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{t}}
.

Take a point
u\in{\mathbb{D}}_{0}^{\circ}
and consider the evolution
z^{\mathsf{t}}=z^{{\mathsf{t}},u}
solving (
218
). Let
{\mathsf{t}}(u)
be the first time when
z^{{\mathsf{t}},u}
hits the boundary of
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
. Note that
z^{{\mathsf{t}}(u),u}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{t}}
, because
\mathbf{U}_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{{\mathsf{t}}(u),u})=\mathbf{U}_{0}(u)
is non-real.

Lemma 5.16
.

For each
{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant 0
, the map
u\mapsto z^{{\mathsf{t}},u}
is a conformal bijection between
\{u\in{\mathbb{D}}_{0}\mid{\mathsf{t}}<{\mathsf{t}}(u)\}
and
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}
, which continuously extends to a homeomorphism between the closures of these two domains.

Instead of dealing with
u\mapsto z^{{\mathsf{t}},u}
directly, we consider the map
{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(u)\mapsto{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{{\mathsf{t}},u})
; in the notations of Step 4 this is the map
w_{0}\mapsto w^{\mathsf{t}}
, which is given explicitly by (
225
):

w^{0}\mapsto w^{0}-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}^{0}.

(233)

According to Step 4,
{\mathcal{S}}^{0}={\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w^{0})
is a complex number with negative imaginary part. Hence,
w^{\mathsf{t}}
, as a function of
{\mathsf{t}}
, has increasing imaginary part and the time
{\mathsf{t}}({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-1}(w))
is the first time when
w^{\mathsf{t}}
becomes real. Since
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}
is a bijection between
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}
and (open) lower halfplane, the lemma becomes equivalent to the study of the map (
233
) between the set
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(w-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w))\leqslant 0\}
and the closed lower halfplane
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(w)\leqslant 0\}
. Using the representation (
184
), we rewrite the map as

w\mapsto w\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}-1}-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1)C_{0}+({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{{\rm d}\mu_{0}(s)}{w-s}.

(234)

The maps of this type (up to rescalings and real shifts, which preserve the lower halfplane and therefore are irrelevant) has been studied in the free probability theory before and the desired statements on being homeomorphism and being comformal is
[
11
, Lemma 4]
. This finishes the proof of Lemma
5.16
.
∎

Remark 5.17
.

The statement of Lemma
5.16
leads to the definition of the map
u\mapsto z^{{\mathsf{t}},u}
on the boundary of
\{u\in{\mathbb{D}}_{0}\mid{\mathsf{t}}<{\mathsf{t}}(u)\}
; equivalently, we can define the map
w_{0}\mapsto w^{{\mathsf{t}}}
on the boundary of the domain
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(w-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w))\leqslant 0\}
. By continuity, these maps are defined by exactly the same formulas:
z^{{\mathsf{t}},u}
can be found solving (
218
), while
w^{{\mathsf{t}}}
is given by (
225
) or (
233
).

We also observe that the boundary of
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(w-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w))\leqslant 0\}
naturally splits into two parts: the part with strictly negative imaginary part of
w
is being mapped by
w_{0}\mapsto w^{{\mathsf{t}}}
into the liquid region
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{t}})
and the part on the real axis is mapped to the complement
\mathbb{R}\setminus{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{t}})
.

The role of Lemma
5.16
is two-fold. First, it guarantees that the procedures of Steps 2 and 3 work for all relevant values of
z
or
w
, because all of them are possible values for
z^{\mathsf{t}}
or
w^{\mathsf{t}}
. Second, it explains which
u
solving (
182
) we should choose: this must be the unique
u
such that:

u\in{\mathbb{D}}_{0}^{\circ},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left[{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(u)-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}_{0}({\mathsf{b}}_{0}(u))\right]<0,

which is precisely the condition of Remark
5.6
.

Step 6.
At this point we have verified that if
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
is described by the PDE of Proposition
5.3
, then it can be found through the formulas of either Proposition
5.5
or Proposition
5.8
. For the sake of completeness, we also would like to check that if
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is found from the formulas of the latter two propositions, then it solves the PDE (
179
). We start from Proposition
5.5

Let
u=u(z,t)
and
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
be obtained by the procedure of Proposition
5.5
. We use functions
U(u)
and
V(u)
as defined by (
221
). We claim that

U(u(z,{\mathsf{t}}))={\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)},\qquad V(u(z,{\mathsf{t}}))=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}.

(235)

To prove the claim we notice that (
182
) can be rewritten as

{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}=V(u)-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}U(u).

(236)

On the other hand, expressing
f_{0}(u)
from the definitions of
U(u)
and
V(u)
in (
221
) and equating the results, we get

\frac{U(u)+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+U(u)}=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}-V(u)}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}-V(u)};\qquad\text{Equivalently: }\quad{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}=V(u)-U(u).

(237)

\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}
arising in the last formula, can be reexpressed using (
181
) as

{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z}-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)({\mathsf{q}}^{-z+{\mathsf{t}}}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)},

(238)

Combining (
236
), (
237
), and (
238
) we get a system of two linear equations on
U(u)
,
V(u)
, which is solved by (
235
).

Next, we take (
235
) and differentiate it in
{\mathsf{t}}
and
z
, getting four identities:

\displaystyle U^{\prime}(u(z,{\mathsf{t}}))\partial_{z}u(z,{\mathsf{t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{z}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z})-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}}{(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}},

\displaystyle U^{\prime}(u(z,{\mathsf{t}}))\partial_{\mathsf{t}}u(z,{\mathsf{t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z})+f_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}}{(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}},

\displaystyle V^{\prime}(u(z,{\mathsf{t}}))\partial_{z}u(z,{\mathsf{t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{z}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}}{(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}},

\displaystyle V^{\prime}(u(z,{\mathsf{t}}))\partial_{\mathsf{t}}u(z,{\mathsf{t}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})+f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}}{(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}}.

The product of the left-hand sides for the first and forth identities is the same as the product for the second and third identities. Therefore,

\bigl[\partial_{z}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z})-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}\bigr]\\
\times\bigl[\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})+f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}\bigr]\\
=\bigl[\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z})+f_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}\bigr]\\
\times\bigl[\partial_{z}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\ln{\mathsf{q}}\bigr],

which simplifies to

-[\partial_{z}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)]f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z})\ln{\mathsf{q}}\\
+[\partial_{\mathsf{t}}f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)](1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z})\ln{\mathsf{q}}\\
=f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}(\kappa^{4}{\mathsf{q}}^{2z-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-2z})(\ln{\mathsf{q}})^{2}.

Dividing by
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}({\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}})({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z})\ln{\mathsf{q}}
, we arrive at (
179
).

Step 7.
Finally, we assume that
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is obtained by the procedure of Proposition
5.8
. Our task is to show that it satisfies the PDE (
179
). Recasting (
185
) as (
226
), the procedure can be summarized as follows. Given
({\mathsf{t}},z)
, we first find
w=w({\mathsf{t}},z)
such that

w-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)={\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}.

(239)

Then we find
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
by solving

{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)={\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z){\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)},

which results in

f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\frac{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}},\qquad 1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}}.

(240)

Differentiating (
239
) in
z
and
{\mathsf{t}}
, we get:

\displaystyle[\partial_{z}w](1-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{0}(w))

\displaystyle=-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}\ln{\mathsf{q}}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}\ln{\mathsf{q}},

\displaystyle[\partial_{\mathsf{t}}w](1-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{0}(w))

\displaystyle=-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}\ln{\mathsf{q}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)\ln{\mathsf{q}}.

(241)

On the other hand, differentiating (
240
), we get

\partial_{t}\ln f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)+\partial_{z}\ln(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))\\
=\frac{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{0}(w)[\partial_{t}w]}{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}-\frac{{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{0}(w)[\partial_{t}w]+{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}\ln{\mathsf{q}}}{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}}+\frac{-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}\ln{\mathsf{q}}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z}\ln{\mathsf{q}}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}-\frac{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}^{\prime}(w)[\partial_{z}w]-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}\ln{\mathsf{q}}}{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}}\\
={\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}_{0}(w)\left(\frac{[\partial_{t}w]}{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z}}-\frac{[\partial_{t}w]+[\partial_{z}w]}{{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}}\right)-\frac{{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}{{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}}\ln{\mathsf{q}}.

(242)

The last term in the right-hand side matches the right-hand side in (
179
). Hence, it remains to show that the first term in the right-hand side of (
242
) vanishes. We check:

[\partial_{t}w]({\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}([\partial_{t}w]+[\partial_{z}w])({\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z});

\text{Equivalently:}\qquad[\partial_{t}w]({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}-z}-\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{=}}[\partial_{z}w]({\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z}).

The last identity is checked by taking the ratio of two identities in (
241
).
∎

5.5
Proof of Theorem
5.1

The measures
\rho(\cdot;{\bm{x}}(t))
of densities
\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t))
as given by (
174
), are originally defined for integer values of
t
and we now extend the definition to all
t\in[0,T]
by linear interpolation between integers.

Let
C([0,{\mathsf{T}}],\mathscr{M}_{1}({\mathbb{R}})])
denote the space of continuous functions from
[0,{\mathsf{t}}]
to the set of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
equipped with the weak topology of measures. Then
\rho(\cdot;{\bm{x}}(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}))
represents a random element of this space. In fact, there is a compact set
\mathcal{K}\subset C([0,{\mathsf{T}}],\mathscr{M}_{1}({\mathbb{R}})])
, such that the distribution of
\rho(\cdot;{\bm{x}}(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}))_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}
is supported on
\mathcal{K}
— this is because measures
\rho(\cdot;{\bm{x}}(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}))
are supported inside
[{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}]
and the dependence on
{\mathsf{t}}
is Lipschitz (each component of
{\bm{x}}(t)
jumps at most by
1
when
t
grows by
1
), cf.
[
4
, Lemma 4.3.13]
.

Since the space of probability measures on a compact set is compact, we conclude that the stochastic processes
\rho(\cdot;{\bm{x}}(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}))_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}
have subsequential limits in distribution as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We let
(\rho_{\mathsf{t}})_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}
be one of the limiting points. Our task is to show that
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
are described by Propositions
5.3
,
5.5
,
5.8
. (Implying, in particular, that all the limiting points are the same and, therefore, coincide with
\varepsilon\to 0
limit.) Note that for each
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}
,
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
is an absolutely continuous measures of density at most
1
, because so were the prelimit measures.

For any
{\mathsf{t}}\in[0,{\mathsf{T}}]\cap\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}
, using Proposition
5.14
, we have

\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(s)}{\rm d}s=\int_{\mathbf{l}(0)}^{\mathbf{r}(0)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{0}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(0))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(s)}{\rm d}s\\
+\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)+\varepsilon\sum_{\tau\in[0,{\mathsf{t}})\cap\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}}\left[-\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\tau}(z)+\ln({\mathsf{q}})\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-\tau}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-\tau}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}-\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}^{+}(z)\right]+\OO\left(\varepsilon\right),

(243)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is a martingale given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sum_{\tau\in[0,{\mathsf{t}})\cap\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\varepsilon^{-1}\tau}(z).

(244)

We can estimate the
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
term by Doob/Kolmogorov’s inequality for martingales:

{\rm Prob}\left(\sup_{0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}}|\varepsilon\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)|>\lambda\right)\leqslant\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\sum_{\tau\in[0,{\mathsf{T}})\cap\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{E}[\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\varepsilon^{-1}\tau}(z)]^{2},\qquad\lambda>0.

Using (
207
), each term in the last sum is
\OO(\varepsilon)
. There are
\OO(\varepsilon^{-1})
terms and therefore the probability decays as
\OO(\varepsilon^{2}/\lambda^{2})
. We conclude that the martingale part in (
243
) goes to
0
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Hence,
\varepsilon\to 0
limit of (
243
) gives an integral equation for
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(s)}{\rm d}s&=\int_{\mathbf{l}(0)}^{\mathbf{r}(0)}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{0}(z)\rho_{0}(s)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(s)}{\rm d}s\\
&+\int_{0}^{{\mathsf{t}}}\left[-\partial_{z}\ln\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\tau}(z)+\ln({\mathsf{q}})\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-\tau}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-\tau}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}-\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}^{+}(z)\right]{\rm d}\tau,\end{split}

(245)

where
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\tau}(z)
is the limit of
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{\tau}(z)
given by
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}_{\tau}(z)=\tilde{\varphi}_{\tau}^{-}(z)(1-f_{\tau}(z))
using the notation (
178
). We also recall that

f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=-\frac{\tilde{\varphi}^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\tilde{\varphi}^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}\exp\left(\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right).

Hence, differentiating (
245
) in
{\mathsf{t}}
, we get

\displaystyle\phantom{{}={}}\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln\tilde{\varphi}^{+}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)+\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\tilde{\varphi}^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{z}\ln\varphi_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)-\partial_{z}\ln(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(z))+\ln({\mathsf{q}})\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{z-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-z}}-\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z).

Cancelling
-\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\ln\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)
terms and noting that
\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\tilde{\varphi}^{-}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=-\partial_{z}\ln\varphi_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)
from the definition (
186
), we arrive at the PDE (
179
) of Proposition
5.3
. Theorem
5.15
then yields that the measures
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
,
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}
, are given by the procedures of Propositions
5.5
or
5.8
.

5.6
Proof of Theorems
1.3
and
1.6

In Theorem
5.1
we identified the limit shape for lozenge tilings of the trapezoids and in this subsection we explain that the description of this theorem matches those of Theorems
1.3
and
1.6
in the introduction.

We start by analyzing the equation (
19
). Note that this is the same equation as (
182
); however the variable
z
is a complex number outside the support of
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
in the latter, while a similar variable
x
in the former is a real number, which might belong to the support of
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
. Let us recast the equation. We have
f_{0}(u)
is given by

\frac{({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{u})(\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-u})}{(\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-u})({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{u})}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{u})(\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-u})}{(\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-u})({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{u})}=\exp\left(-\int_{{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(u)(1-\rho_{0}(s))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(u)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}{\rm d}s\right),

(246)

where we used Remark
5.2
to match two forms of
f_{0}(u)
. Let us introduce the new variable
\mathbf{u}={\mathsf{q}}^{u}
, so that

f_{0}(u)=g_{0}({\mathsf{q}}^{u}),\qquad g_{0}(\mathbf{u})={\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}\frac{(\mathbf{u}-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}})(\mathbf{u}-\kappa^{-2}{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{T}}})}{(\mathbf{u}-\kappa^{-2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{N}}})(\mathbf{u}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}})}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{(\mathbf{u}-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}})(\mathbf{u}-\kappa^{-2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{b}}_{i}})}{(\mathbf{u}-\kappa^{-2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{a}}_{i}})(\mathbf{u}-{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}})}.

(We used
\sum_{i=1}^{r}({\mathsf{b}}_{i}-{\mathsf{a}}_{i})={\mathsf{N}}
in transformation of
f_{0}
into
g_{0}
.)
Using (
15
), the equation (
19
) has the form

\displaystyle{\mathsf{q}}^{-}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}=\frac{({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1)(\kappa^{2}\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{u}^{-1})}{1-g_{0}(\mathbf{u})}+\kappa^{2}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{u}^{-1}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}.

(247)

Proposition 5.18
.

Take
{\mathsf{t}}\in[0,{\mathsf{T}}]
and
x\in[{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}},{\mathsf{N}}]
. For the equation (
247
) either:

•

all its solutions
\mathbf{u}
have real
\mathbf{u}^{-1}+\kappa^{2}\mathbf{u}
; or

•

there is a unique quadruple of complex solutions
(\mathbf{u},\bar{\mathbf{u}},\kappa^{-2}\mathbf{u}^{-1},\kappa^{-2}\bar{\mathbf{u}}^{-1})
with complex
\mathbf{u}^{-1}+\kappa^{2}\mathbf{u}
and all other solutions have real
\mathbf{u}^{-1}+\kappa^{2}\mathbf{u}
.

Recall that we still only detail the case of
0<{\mathsf{q}}<1
and large real
\kappa
as in Assumption
5.11
.

Multiplying (
247
) by
\mathbf{u}(1-g_{0}(\mathbf{u}))
and then by all denominators in the definition of
g_{0}(\mathbf{u})
we arrive at a polynomial equation of degree
2r+4
with real coefficients, which has at most
2r+4
complex solutions. Note that the equation is unchanged if we replace
\mathbf{u}
by
\kappa^{-2}\mathbf{u}^{-1}
(
g_{0}(\mathbf{u})
turns into
\tfrac{1}{g_{0}(\mathbf{u})}
under this transformation). Hence, all its complex solutions come in quadruples
(\mathbf{u},\bar{\mathbf{u}},\kappa^{-2}\mathbf{u}^{-1},\kappa^{-2}\bar{\mathbf{u}}^{-1})
. If
\mathbf{u}
is non-real, then all these numbers are distinct unless
\mathbf{u}=\kappa^{-2}\bar{\mathbf{u}}^{-1}
. In the latter case
\mathbf{u}^{-1}+\kappa^{-2}\mathbf{u}
is real. Hence, non-real
\mathbf{u}^{-1}+\kappa^{-2}\mathbf{u}
leads to four distinct non-real solutions to (
247
). Therefore, the statement of the proposition would follow, if we manage to find
2r-3
distinct real solutions to (
247
).

Let us search for real solutions satisfying
\mathbf{u}>\kappa^{-1}
. Due to
\mathbf{u}\mapsto\kappa^{-2}\mathbf{u}^{-1}
symmetry of the equation it would be sufficient to find
r-1
of such solutions. For that we study the behavior of the function
g_{0}(\mathbf{u})-1
on the segment
[\kappa^{-1},+\infty)
.

Immediately from its definition,
g_{0}
has
r
zeros at points
\mathbf{u}={\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}}
and
r
poles at points
\mathbf{u}={\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}}
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
. There is another pole at
\mathbf{u}={\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}
. Finally, there are two cases: in case A,
{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{N}}}
is another zero in
[\mathbf{u},+\infty)
; in case B,
\kappa^{-2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{N}}}
is another pole in
[\mathbf{u},+\infty)
. (Only one of these two points belongs to
[\kappa^{-1},+\infty)
; this point is inside
(\kappa^{-1},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{r}})
.) Other zeros and poles of
g_{0}
are outside
[\kappa^{-1},+\infty)
. In particular, we conclude that
g_{0}
is positive on each segment
({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}})
.

Note that
g_{0}(\mathbf{u})=1
is a polynomial equation of degree
2r+2
. It has two explicit solutions at
\mathbf{u}=\pm\kappa^{-1}
. In addition, on each segment
[{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{i}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{a}}_{i}}]
the continuous function
g_{0}(\mathbf{u})
changes from
+\infty
to
0
; hence, there are points
{\mathsf{c}}_{i}\in({\mathsf{a}}_{i},{\mathsf{b}}_{i})
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
, such that
g_{0}({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{c}}_{i}})=1
. Due to symmetry
g_{0}(\kappa^{-2}\mathbf{u}^{-1})=(g_{0}(\mathbf{u}))^{-1}
, we also have
g_{0}(\kappa^{-2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{c}}_{i}})=1
. Therefore, we have found all
2r+2
zeros of
g_{0}(\mathbf{u})-1
and all these zeros are simple.

We can now analyze the function
\frac{1}{1-g_{0}(\mathbf{u})}
on
[\kappa^{-1},+\infty)
: it has poles at
r+1
points
\{\kappa^{-1},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{c}}_{r}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{c}}_{r-1}},\dots,{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{c}}_{1}}\}
and zeros at
r+1
points
\{{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{r}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{r-1}},\dots,{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{b}}_{1}},{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}\}
; there is one more zero at
\kappa^{-2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{N}}}
in case
B
. This is schematically shown in Figure
8
together with signs of
\frac{1}{1-g_{0}(\mathbf{u})}
, which can be understood from zeros, poles, and observation
\lim_{\mathbf{u}\to+\infty}g_{0}(\mathbf{u})={\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{T}}}>1
. We conclude that on each segment
({\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{c}}_{i}},{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{c}}_{i-1}})
,
1<i\leqslant r
, the function
\frac{1}{1-g_{0}(\mathbf{u})}
changes from
+\infty
to
-\infty
and so does the right-hand side of (
247
). Thus, there is a solution to (
247
) on each these
r-1
segments and we found the desired
r-1
real solutions on
(\kappa^{-1},+\infty)
.

Fig 8:
Zeros (gray circles), poles (vertical lines), and signs for the function
\frac{1}{1-g_{0}(\mathbf{u})}
for
u\in(\kappa^{-1},+\infty)
.

∎

Proposition 5.19
.

A point
({\mathsf{t}},x)
,
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{T}}
,
{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}\leqslant x\leqslant{\mathsf{N}}
, belongs to the liquid region
{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}})
of (
13
) (in the notations of Theorem
5.1
it means that
0<\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(x)<1
) if and only if the equation (
247
) has a solution such that
\mathbf{u}^{-1}+\kappa^{2}\mathbf{u}
is non-real.

Suppose that
0<\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(x)<1
. Then, as we observed in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem
5.15
,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}f_{\mathsf{t}}(x+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)
is non-real. Using Proposition
5.5
, this implies through (
181
) that
{\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}
is non-real. Since
{\mathsf{q}}^{u}
solves (
182
), which coincides at
z=x
with (
247
) in the variable
\mathbf{u}={\mathsf{q}}^{u}
, we conclude that (
247
) has a solution with non-real
\mathbf{u}^{-1}+\kappa^{2}\mathbf{u}
.

In the opposite direction, suppose that (
247
) has a solution with non-real
\mathbf{u}^{-1}+\kappa^{2}\mathbf{u}
; among such solutions let
w^{0}
be the one with negative imaginary part. As in Step 3 in the proof of Theorem
5.15
, we start the dynamics
({\mathsf{t}},w^{\mathsf{t}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{t}})_{{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant 0}
from this particular
w^{0}
. The dynamics is rewritten as the linear evolution in (
225
) and comparing this evolution with (
247
) we conclude that
w^{\mathsf{t}}
hits the real axis at the time
{\mathsf{t}}
given in the statement of Proposition
5.19
and at point
w^{\mathsf{t}}={\mathsf{q}}^{-x}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{x-{\mathsf{t}}}
. On the other hand, the value of
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{t}}
is preserved in the time evolution and equals
{\mathcal{S}}^{0}
, which is non-real according to Step 4 in the proof of Theorem
5.15
(applied for
{\mathsf{t}}=0
). Looking back to the definition of
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathsf{t}}
from (
224
), we conclude that
f_{\mathsf{t}}(x)
should be non-real. Hence, as in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem
5.15
, we should necessary have
0<\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(x)<1
.
∎

We use Theorem
5.1
and note that the height function of Theorem
1.6
can be written as

\varepsilon h(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}},\varepsilon^{-1})=\int_{-\infty}\rho(s,{\bm{x}}(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}})){\rm d}s.

Using (
176
) and noting that
h(\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}},\varepsilon^{-1})
is uniformly Lipschitz in
({\mathsf{t}},)
, we conclude that the convergence of (
18
) holds and the limiting height function is described through

{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},)=\int_{-\infty}^{\rho}{}_{\mathsf{t}}(s){\rm d}s.

(248)

Proposition
5.19
implies the description of the liquid region
{\mathsf{L}}({\mathsf{P}})
, as claimed in Theorem
1.6
. We further use the description of
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
of Proposition
5.5
. Taking
({\mathsf{t}},)
in the liquid region and sending
z\to+\mathbf{i}0
in (
181
), we arrive at the formula (
20
). The only difference is that we approached the definition of the complex slope
f_{\mathsf{t}}
in a different way: in Proposition
5.5
we used (
178
) in terms of the measure
\rho_{\mathsf{t}}
, while in Theorem
1.6
we used definition (
17
) in terms of the local proportions of lozenges. Hence, we need to show that these two definitions coincide: for that we take
f_{\mathsf{t}}
as given by (
178
) and show it satisfies (
17
). We computed
\tfrac{1}{\pi}\arg f_{\mathsf{t}}(+\mathbf{i}0)=1-\rho_{\mathsf{t}}()
in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem
5.15
. Comparing (
248
) with (
12
), we conclude that
p_{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}({\mathsf{t}},)=1-\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(x)
and arrive at the first identity of (
17
).

For the second identity of (
17
) we need to use the PDE (version of the complex Burgers equation) of (
179
). Choose
M
to be a very large positive number. We have

\displaystyle p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}}+p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_down.pdf}}}}

\displaystyle=1-\partial{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},)+\partial{\mathsf{h}}(,{\mathsf{t}})+\partial_{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},)=1+\partial_{\mathsf{t}}{\mathsf{h}}({\mathsf{t}},)=1+\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\int_{-M}^{\rho}{}_{\mathsf{t}}(s){\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\int_{-M}\left[(\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s)-1)\mathbf{1}_{s\geqslant{\mathsf{t}}-{\mathsf{T}}}\right]{\rm d}s=-\frac{1}{\pi}\partial_{\mathsf{t}}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int_{-M}^{\arg}f_{\mathsf{t}}(s+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon){\rm d}s

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int_{-M}^{\partial}{}_{{\mathsf{t}}}\left[\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\ln f_{\mathsf{t}}(s+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)\right]{\rm d}s=-\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int_{-M}^{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}}\left[\partial_{{\mathsf{t}}}\ln f_{\mathsf{t}}(s+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)\right]{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int_{-M}^{\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}}\left[\partial_{s}\ln(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(s+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon))-\ln({\mathsf{q}})\frac{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{s+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon-{\mathsf{t}}}+{\mathsf{q}}^{-s-\mathbf{i}\varepsilon}}{\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{s+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon-{\mathsf{t}}}-{\mathsf{q}}^{-s-\mathbf{i}\varepsilon}}\right]{\rm d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}\left[\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0+}\int_{-M}^{\partial}{}_{s}\ln(1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(s+\mathbf{i}\varepsilon)){\rm d}s\right]=\frac{1}{\pi}\arg\left(\frac{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(+\mathbf{i}0)}{1-f_{\mathsf{t}}(-M+\mathbf{i}0)}\right).

For
z=-M+\mathbf{i}0
with large positive
M
, the integrand in (
178
) is close to
\ln{\mathsf{q}}\cdot(1-\rho_{\mathsf{t}}(s))
. Hence, the exponent is positive and
f_{\mathsf{t}}(-M+\mathbf{i}0)
is a real number greater than
1
. We conclude that

p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\includegraphics[]{lozenge_hor.pdf}}}}+p_{{\scalebox{0.15}{\,\includegraphics[]{lozenge_v_down.pdf}}}}=\frac{1}{\pi}\arg\left(\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(+\mathbf{i}0)-1}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(-M+\mathbf{i}0)-1}\right)=\frac{1}{\pi}\arg\bigl(f_{\mathsf{t}}(+\mathbf{i}0)-1\bigr),

which matches the second identity of (
17
).
∎

We have shown in Propositions
5.18
,
5.19
that for
({\mathsf{t}},)
in the liquid region, (
247
) has non-real complex conjugate solutions, while outside the liquid region all the solutions are real. Hence, on the arctic curve bounding the liquid region, the two complex solutions glue together and (
247
) has a double root, which is the same as (
19
) having a double root. Hence, the
u
–derivative of the right-hand side in (
19
) should vanish and we have a system of two equations:

\begin{cases}{\mathsf{q}}^{-}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}=V(u)-\frac{U(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}},\\
V^{\prime}(u)=\frac{U^{\prime}(u)}{{\mathsf{q}}^{\mathsf{t}}}.\end{cases}

(249)

We parameterize solutions to (
249
) by
u
: given
u
,
{\mathsf{q}}^{{\mathsf{t}}}
is found from the second equation and then
{\mathsf{q}}
is found by solving the quadratic equation in
{\mathsf{q}}
of the first equation. This is precisely (
16
).

It remains to understand the possible values for the parameter
u
. It is convenient to argue in terms of
w={\mathsf{q}}^{-u}+\kappa^{2}{\mathsf{q}}^{u}
. As we know from Steps 3 and 5 in the proof of Theorem
5.15
, if we take
{\mathsf{t}}\in(0,{\mathsf{T}})
and
w
in the lower half-plane, then given by the first equation of (
249
) is the point where the flow
w^{\mathsf{t}}
(started from
w^{0}=w
) first leaves the lower half-plane and
({\mathsf{t}},)
belong to the liquid region in this case; because everything is invariant under conjugations, the same argument applies to
w
in the upper half-plane. We conclude that
w
has to be real.

Let us now show that each real
w
(except for those where some parts of (
249
) are singular) leads to a point on the arctic curve in this way. For that we recall Lemma
5.16
and Remark
5.17
: at time
{\mathsf{t}}
the part of the boundary
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(w-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w))\leqslant 0\}
with negative imaginary part forms a collection of curves
\gamma_{\mathsf{t}}
in the lower half-plane. These curves are in bijection with the section
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{t}}
of the liquid region at time
{\mathsf{t}}
and, therefore, their end-points (which are on the real axis) correspond to the points of the arctic boundary at times
{\mathsf{t}}
. As time
{\mathsf{t}}
grows, the set
\{w\in\mathbb{C}\mid\mathop{\mathrm{Im}}(w-({\mathsf{q}}^{-{\mathsf{t}}}-1){\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w))\leqslant 0\}
becomes larger and larger, until it swallows entire lower halfplane at time
{\mathsf{T}}
, as follows from the representation (
184
) for
{\mathcal{S}}_{0}(w)
. Hence, the end-points of the curves
\gamma_{\mathsf{t}}
eventually reach all points on the real as
{\mathsf{t}}
changes from
0
to
{\mathsf{T}}
.
∎

6
Gaussian Free Field

In this section we prove Theorem
1.11
.

6.1
Stochastic evolution of fluctuations

Our first task is to use the result of Theorem
4.5
, as recorded in Section
5.3
, to produce a stochastic evolution equation for the Gaussian macroscopic fluctuations of random tilings. We use the notations of Section
5.2
, in particular,
{\mathsf{t}}=\varepsilon t
. We introduce the field of centered fluctuations:

G_{t}(z)=\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}-\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}\right],z\in\mathbb{C},{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\notin{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}([\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]).

(250)

Proposition 6.1
.

In the framework of Theorem
5.1
and using the notations of Sections
5.1
,
5.2
,
for
{\mathsf{t}}=t\varepsilon
we have as
\varepsilon\to 0
:

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigl[G_{t+1}(z)-G_{t}(z)\Bigr]=\partial_{z}\left[G_{t}(z)\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{1-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}\right]+\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)+\mathcal{R},

(251)

where
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)
are mean
0
(both conditionally on
{\bm{x}}(t)
, and unconditionally) random variables such that
\{\varepsilon^{-1/2}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)\}_{z\in\Lambda_{\mathsf{t}}\setminus[\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]}
are asymptotically Gaussian with covariance given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}[\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z_{1})\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t^{\prime}}(z_{2})]}{\varepsilon}=\frac{\delta_{t=t^{\prime}}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-1}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(z_{1})}{({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z_{1}))^{2}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(z_{2})}{({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z_{2}))^{2}}{\rm d}w+\oo(1),

(252)

the contour
{\omega_{-}}\subset\Lambda_{\mathsf{t}}
encloses
[\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]
, but not
z_{1}
or
z_{2}
;
f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)
is as in (
178
), (
180
). The asymptotic Gaussianity is in the convergence of moments sense, as in Theorem
4.5
. The (random) remainder
\mathcal{R}
satisfies an upper bound

|\mathcal{R}|=\oo(\varepsilon)+\oo(1)\left|G_{t}(z)\right|+\oo(1)\mathbb{E}\left|G_{t}(z)\right|,

(253)

where all the implicit constant can be made deterministic and uniform in
\varepsilon
, in
t=0,1,\dots,T-1
, and in
z
belonging to compact subsets of
\mathbb{C}
, such that
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is away from
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}([\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})])
.

Subtracting from the result of Proposition
5.14
its expectation, we get

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigl[G_{t+1}(z)-G_{t}(z)\Bigr]=\bigl(\mathbb{E}\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)-\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)\bigr)+\varepsilon(R_{t}(z)-\mathbb{E}R_{t}(z))+\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)+\OO(\varepsilon^{2}).

(254)

Using Theorem
5.1
, we conclude that
\varepsilon(R_{t}(z)-\mathbb{E}R_{t}(z))
can be absorbed into
o(\varepsilon)
term in (
253
).
O(\varepsilon^{2})
in (
254
) is also absorbed in the same remainder. Further, writing
\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)+(\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z))
and using (
191
), we have

\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)=\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\varphi}_{t}^{-}(z)-\frac{\partial_{z}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{1-\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}\\
=\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\varphi}_{t}^{-}(z)-\frac{\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{1-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}-\frac{\left[\partial_{z}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\right]}{1-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}-\frac{\left[\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\right]\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{(1-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}}\\
+\left|\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\right|\OO\left(\left|\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\right|+\left|\partial_{z}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\right|\right).

(255)

The implicit constant in the remainder admits a deterministic bound
C(z)
, which stays uniformly bounded as long as
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is bounded away from
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}([\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})])
.

Let us introduce a notation

m_{t}(z)=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}})}^{\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\rho(s;{\bm{x}}(t))}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(s)}\right].

Then we have using (
190
):

\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\exp(G_{t}(z))\left[-\exp(m_{t}(z))\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)}\right]=(1+G_{t}(z))\left[-\exp(m_{t}(z))\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)}\right]+\OO\bigl(|G_{t}(z)|^{2}\bigr).

(256)

The remainder in the last formula admits a bound
C(z)|G_{t}(z)|^{2}
with a deterministic function
C(z)
, which stays uniformly bounded as long as
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is bounded away from
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}([\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})])
. Thus, taking expectation of (
256
), we get

\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\left[-\exp(m_{t}(z))\frac{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{+}(z)}{\tilde{\varphi}_{\mathsf{t}}^{-}(z)}\right]+\OO\bigl(\mathbb{E}|G_{t}(z)|^{2}\bigr).

(257)

Hence, subtracting (
257
) from (
256
), we get

\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=G_{t}(z)\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)+\OO\bigl(|G_{t}(z)|^{2}\bigr)+\OO\bigl(\mathbb{E}|G_{t}(z)|^{2}\bigr)+\OO\bigl(|G_{t}(z)|\mathbb{E}|G_{t}(z)|^{2}\bigr).

(258)

Because
|G_{t}(z)|
is bounded and tends to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
by Theorem
5.1
, we can replace all the remainders in the last formula by
\oo(1)\left|G_{t}(z)\right|+\oo(1)\mathbb{E}\left|G_{t}(z)\right|
. Applying the same procedure to
\partial_{z}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
(leading to the answer, which is
z
–derivative of (
258
)), we also conclude that

\partial_{z}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)=\partial_{z}\left(G_{t}(z)\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\right)+\oo(1)\left|G_{t}(z)\right|+\oo(1)\mathbb{E}\left|G_{t}(z)\right|.

(259)

The approximations we have just developed, imply (using Theorem
5.1
) that the remainder in (
255
) can be absorbed into
\oo(1)\left|G_{t}(z)\right|
term in (
253
). Thus, subtracting (
255
) from its expectation and using (
258
), (
259
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)-\partial_{z}\ln\tilde{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathsf{t}}}(z)=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{z}[G_{t}(z)\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)]}{1-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}+\frac{G_{t}(z)\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{(1-\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z))^{2}}

\displaystyle+\oo(1)\left|G_{t}(z)\right|+\oo(1)\mathbb{E}\left|G_{t}(z)\right|.

Plugging the last expression into (
254
), and noting that the conditional covariance of the martingale part (
207
), by Theorem
5.1
, approximates the unconditional covariance of (
252
), we reach (
251
).
∎

6.2
Fluctuations along the characteristic flow

The evolution equation (
251
) for fluctuations might seem complicated. However, it plays nicely with the characteristic flow (
214
), which we used in Section
5.4
, because the ratio
\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}
appears in both.

We introduce a function of two variables
z({\mathsf{t}},u)
defined through the flow
z^{\mathsf{t}}
with initial condition
u
:

z({\mathsf{t}},u):=z^{\mathsf{t}},\qquad\qquad\begin{cases}\partial_{\mathsf{t}}z^{\mathsf{t}}=\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\mathsf{t}})}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\mathsf{t}})-1},&{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant 0,\\
z^{0}=u.\end{cases}.

(260)

Here
u
is complex, and
{\mathsf{t}}\geqslant 0
is real. The map
u\mapsto z({\mathsf{t}},u)
agrees with complex conjugations:
z({\mathsf{t}},\bar{u})=\overline{z({\mathsf{t}},u)}
.
We also recall that (
222
), or (
223
), or (
225
) give more explicit forms for
z({\mathsf{t}},u)
, not involving any differential equations.

We also need a discrete
\varepsilon
–dependent version of the characteristic flow (
260
), which is defined on the grid
{\mathsf{t}}=\varepsilon t
,
t=0,1,2,\dots
, through

z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u):=z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}},\qquad\qquad\begin{cases}z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon}=z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}+\varepsilon\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}})}{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}})-1},&{\mathsf{t}}\in\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\\
z^{\varepsilon;0}=u.\end{cases}.

(261)

Let us emphasize that
z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)
is a deterministic map. As
\varepsilon\to 0
, it approximates
z({\mathsf{t}},u)
, as we now explain.

Definition 6.2
.

We call a pair
(\hat{\mathsf{t}},u)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\times\mathbb{C}

admissible
, if the differential equation (
260
) has a solution for times
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant\hat{\mathsf{t}}
and at all these times
{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\mathsf{t}})\notin{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}([\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]
and
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\mathsf{t}})
is finite and not equal to
1
. In particular, the condition implies that
z(\hat{\mathsf{t}},u)
is well-defined.

Following the notations of Section
5.4
, if we assume
u\in{\mathbb{D}}_{0}^{\circ}
and
0\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}\leqslant{\mathsf{t}}(u)
, where
{\mathsf{t}}(u)
is the first time when
z^{{\mathsf{t}}}
hits the boundary of
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}
, then Lemma
5.16
shows that
({\mathsf{t}},u)
is admissible and
z({\mathsf{t}},u)
takes all values in
{\mathbb{D}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\circ}
, as
u
varies in
{\mathbb{D}}_{0}^{\circ}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Take any admissible pair
(\hat{\mathsf{t}},\hat{u})
. There exists
\varsigma>0
, such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)=z({\mathsf{t}},u),\quad\text{ and }\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)=\partial_{u}z({\mathsf{t}},u)

(262)

for all
({\mathsf{t}},u)
with
{\mathsf{t}}\geq 0
and satisfying
|{\mathsf{t}}-\hat{\mathsf{t}}|<\varsigma
,
|u-\hat{u}|<\varsigma
. The limits are uniform over such pairs
({\mathsf{t}},u)
.

Note that from the definition, if
(\hat{\mathsf{t}},\hat{u})
is admissible pair, then so is
(\hat{\mathsf{t}}+\varsigma_{1},\hat{u})
, as long as
\varsigma_{1}>0
is small enough. Consider the trajectory of the flow
z^{{\mathsf{t}}}
(
260
) started at times
0
at
\hat{u}
and up to time
\hat{\mathsf{t}}+\varsigma_{1}
. We can choose small
\varsigma_{2}>0
, such that
f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)
is well-defined and bounded away from
1
for all
{\mathsf{t}}\in[0,\hat{\mathsf{t}}+\varsigma_{1}]
and
z
in
\varsigma_{2}
–neighborhood of
z^{{\mathsf{t}}}
.
By Theorem
5.1
, for such pairs
({\mathsf{t}},z)
, uniformly, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}=\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}.

(263)

To compare the
\varepsilon
–dependent characteristic flow
z^{\varepsilon,{\mathsf{t}}}
with
z^{\mathsf{t}}
, we introduce the following forward Euler discretization of the ordinary differential equation (
260
)

\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u):=\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}},\qquad\qquad\begin{cases}\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon}=\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}+\varepsilon\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}})}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}})-1},&{\mathsf{t}}\in\varepsilon\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\\
\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;0}=u.\end{cases}.

(264)

By standard theorems on the forward Euler method for approximating ordinary differential equations (e.g.
[
5
, Theorem 2.4]
) the discrete flow
\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon,{\mathsf{t}}}
of (
264
) approximates as
\varepsilon\to 0
the continuous flow
z^{{\mathsf{t}}}
of (
260
) with an error
\OO(\varepsilon)
.

It remains to compare two discrete flows:
\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon,{\mathsf{t}}}
and
z^{\varepsilon,{\mathsf{t}}}
. Taking difference of (
261
) and (
264
), noticing that
\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}
is Lipschitz
in
z
, and using (
263
), we get for
C>0
:

\left|\frac{z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}+1}-\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}+1}}{\varepsilon}\right|-\left|\frac{z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}-\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}}{\varepsilon}\right|\leq\left|\frac{z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}+1}-\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}+1}}{\varepsilon}-\frac{z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}-\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}}{\varepsilon}\right|\\
=\left|\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}})}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}})-1}-\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}})}{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}})-1}\right|\leqslant C|z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}-\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}|+\oo_{\varepsilon}(1),

where the term
\oo_{\varepsilon}(1)
tends to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Hence, applying the discrete Grönwall’s inequality (see, e.g.,
[
66
]
) to
|z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}-\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}|
, we conclude that
|z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}-\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}|
is uniformly small and the discrete flow
z^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}
approximates as
\varepsilon\to 0
the discrete flow
\widehat{z}^{\varepsilon;{\mathsf{t}}}
for
{\mathsf{t}}\in[0,\hat{\mathsf{t}}+\varsigma_{1}]
.

Therefore, if we start the discrete flow
z^{\varepsilon,{\mathsf{t}}}
of (
260
) close to
\hat{u}
at
{\mathsf{t}}=0
, it will approximate as
\varepsilon\to 0
the continuous flow
z^{{\mathsf{t}}}
of (
253
) for any
|{\mathsf{t}}-\hat{\mathsf{t}}|\leqslant\varsigma
; uniformity in
u
follows from uniform continuity of
\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}
and
\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}
in
\varepsilon
and in
z
belonging to the above
\varsigma_{2}
neighborhoods. This proves the first limit relation in (
262
).

For the second identity note that
z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)
is a holomorphic function of
u
. Hence, expressing the derivative as the Cauchy contour integral, the second limit in (
262
) follows from the first one.
∎

Let us now explain why
z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)
is helpful in studying fluctuations.

Corollary 6.4
.

In the framework of Theorem
5.1
and using the notations of Sections
5.1
,
5.2
, and
5.4

for
{\mathsf{t}}=t\varepsilon
, we have as
\varepsilon\to 0
:

\frac{G_{\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}+1}\left(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon,u)\right)\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon,u)-G_{\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}}\left(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)\right)\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)}{\varepsilon}\\
=\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\bigl(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)\bigr)\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon,u)+\mathcal{R},

(265)

where random mean
0
asymptotically Gaussian function
\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)
is as in (
251
), while
\mathcal{R}
is different, but admits a similar upper bound:

|\mathcal{R}|=\oo(\varepsilon)+\oo(1)\left|G_{t}(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u))\right|+\oo(1)\mathbb{E}\left|G_{t}(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u))\right|.

(266)

Taylor-expanding
G_{t+1}(z)
in
z
and with the exact meaning of the remainder
\mathcal{R}
changing from line to line, (
251
) implies that

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[G_{t+1}\left(z+\varepsilon\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}\right)-G_{t}(z)\right]+G_{t}(z)\partial_{z}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}\right]=\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z)+\mathcal{R}.

(267)

Recalling (
261
), note that

\displaystyle\partial_{u}\frac{z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon,u)-z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)}{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\partial_{u}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u))}{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u))-1}\right]=\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)\,\partial_{z}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}\right]_{z=z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon,u)+O(\varepsilon)\bigr)\,\partial_{z}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)}{\mathbb{E}\tilde{f}_{\mathsf{t}}(z)-1}\right]_{z=z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)}.

We plug
z=z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)
into (
267
), multiply by
\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon,u)
, and use the last identity to get (the remainder
\mathcal{R}
is again different, yet it satisfies (
266
)):

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Bigl[G_{t+1}\left(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon,u)\right)-G_{t}(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u))\Bigr]\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon,u)\\
+G_{t}(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u))\partial_{u}\frac{z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon,u)-z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)}{\varepsilon}=\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{t}(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u))\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}+\varepsilon,u)+\mathcal{R}.

(268)

Simplifying the left-hand side of (
268
), we get (
265
).
∎

An advantage of (
265
) over (
251
) is that while the latter is complicated, the former stochastic evolution equation is straightforward to solve by summing the right-hand side. Let us state the result of the summation.

Theorem 6.5
.

Fix
k=1,2,\dots
and take
k
admissible pairs
({\mathsf{t}}_{i},u_{i})
,
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
. In the setting of Theorem
5.1
, the random variables
\varepsilon^{-1}G_{\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}_{i}}(z({\mathsf{t}}_{i},u_{i}))
converge in the sense of moments (uniformly over admissible pairs belonging to compact sets) to a
k
–dimensional Gaussian random vector with zero mean and covariance given by:

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-2}\mathbb{E}\bigl[G_{\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}_{i}}(z({\mathsf{t}}_{i},u_{i}))G_{\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}_{j}}(z({\mathsf{t}}_{j},u_{j}))\bigr]\\
=\frac{1}{\partial_{u_{i}}z({\mathsf{t}}_{i},u_{i})\partial_{u_{j}}z({\mathsf{t}}_{j},u_{j})}\partial_{u_{i}}\partial_{u_{j}}\ln\left[\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(u_{i})-{\mathsf{b}}_{0}(u_{j})}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\tau}\bigl(z(u_{i},\tau)\bigr)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\tau}\bigl(z(u_{j},\tau)\bigr)}\right],\qquad\tau=\min({\mathsf{t}}_{i},{\mathsf{t}}_{j}).

(269)

In the rest of Section
6.2
we prove Theorem
6.5
through a series of lemmas. Assuming
\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}
to be a positive integer, summing the result of Corollary
6.4
, noting that
G_{0}(z)=0
,
and inserting the dependence of the remainders
\mathcal{R}
on
m
into the notations. we get

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}G_{\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}}\left(z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)\right)\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}},u)=\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{m}\bigl(z^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon m,u)\bigr)\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon m+\varepsilon,u)+\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{s}}}\mathcal{R}_{m}.

(270)

Our first step is to analyze the martingale sum in (
270
).

Lemma 6.6
.

In the setting of Theorem
6.5
, the random variables

\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon^{-1}{\mathsf{t}}}\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{m}\bigl(z^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon m,u)\bigr)\partial_{u}z^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon m+\varepsilon,u)\Bigr|_{({\mathsf{t}},{\mathsf{u}})=({\mathsf{t}}_{i},{\mathsf{u}}_{i})},\qquad 1\leq i\leq k,

(271)

converge in distribution to
k
–dimensional Gaussian random vector with zero mean and covariance given by (
269
). If we metrize the distributional convergence, then the convergence is uniform as in Theorem
6.5
.

By Corollary
6.4
, each term in (
271
) is a martingale difference. Hence, by the martingale central limit theorem
[
64
]
, this sum is asymptotically Gaussian in the sense of convergence in distribution. The asymptotic covariance of the
i
th and
j
th sum is given by

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\partial_{u_{i}}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}_{i},u_{i})\partial_{u_{j}}z^{\varepsilon}({\mathsf{t}}_{j},u_{j})}\sum_{m=0}^{\varepsilon^{-1}\min({\mathsf{t}}_{i},{\mathsf{t}}_{j})}\Biggl(\partial_{u_{i}}z^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon m+\varepsilon,u_{i})\partial_{u_{j}}z^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon m+\varepsilon,u_{j})\\
\times\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{m}\bigl(z^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon m,u_{i})\bigr)\Delta{\mathcal{M}}_{m}\bigl(z^{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon m,u_{j})\bigr)\Bigr]\Biggr).

(272)

Using (
252
) and Lemma
6.3
, the sum over
m
approximates as
\varepsilon\to 0
the integral:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\min({\mathsf{t}}_{i},{\mathsf{t}}_{j})}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial_{u_{i}}z({\mathsf{t}},u_{i})\partial_{u_{j}}z({\mathsf{t}},u_{j})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}

\displaystyle\times\left[\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-1}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(z({\mathsf{t}},u_{i}))}{({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z({\mathsf{t}},u_{i})))^{2}}\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w){\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(z({\mathsf{t}},u_{j}))}{({\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z({\mathsf{t}},u_{j})))^{2}}{\rm d}w\right]{\rm d}{\mathsf{t}},

where the contour
{\omega_{-}}
(which might depend on
{\mathsf{t}}
) encloses
[\mathbf{l}({\mathsf{t}}),\mathbf{r}({\mathsf{t}})]
, but not
z({\mathsf{t}},u_{i})
or
z({\mathsf{t}},u_{j})
. We further notice that the last integral can be rewritten as

\int_{0}^{\min({\mathsf{t}}_{i},{\mathsf{t}}_{j})}\partial_{u_{1}}\partial_{u_{2}}\left[\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-1}\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z({\mathsf{t}},u_{i}))}\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z({\mathsf{t}},u_{j}))}{\rm d}w\right]{\rm d}{\mathsf{t}}.

(273)

Let us analyze the
{\rm d}w
contour integral. For that we let
\omega^{\prime}_{-}
denote the image of the contour
{\omega_{-}}
under the map
w\mapsto-w+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa
. Recalling the symmetries

\displaystyle{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)={\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(-w+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa),\quad{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)=-{\mathsf{b}}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{t}}(-w+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa),

and
f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)=1/f_{\mathsf{t}}(-w+{\mathsf{t}}-2\log_{\mathsf{q}}\kappa)
, we conclude that

\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{{\omega_{-}}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-1}\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z({\mathsf{t}},u_{i}))}\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z({\mathsf{t}},u_{j}))}{\rm d}w\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\oint_{\omega^{\prime}_{-}}\frac{f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)}{f_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-1}\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z({\mathsf{t}},u_{i}))}\cdot\frac{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}^{\prime}(w)}{{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(w)-{\mathsf{b}}_{\mathsf{t}}(z({\mathsf{t}},u_{j}))}{\rm d}w\\
